Model Category Structure on Simplicial Algebras via Dold-Kan Correspondence
Hossein Faridian

Abstract
. This expository article sets forth a self-contained and purely algebraic proof of a deep result of Quillen stating that the category of simplicial commutative algebras over a commutative ring is a model category. This is accomplished by starting from the model structure on the category of connective chain complexes, transferring it to the category of simplicial modules via Dold-Kan Correspondence, and further transferring it to the category of simplicial commutative algebras through Quillen-Kan Transfer Machine. The subtlety of overcoming the acyclicity condition is addressed by introducing and studying the shuffle product of connective chain complexes, establishing a variant of Eilenberg-Zilber Theorem, and carefully scrutinizing the subtle structures under study.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
18N40; 18G31; 18G50; 18N50.

1.
Introduction

Homological ideas grew out of attempts to investigate deeper properties of topological spaces by means of studying certain algebraic objects, namely homology and cohomology groups. These ideas took abstract forms later and planted the early seeds of homological algebra in the framework of category theory. In the grand scheme of things, the main goal of homological algebra is to construct derived functors of a given functor which are in some sense the best approximations of the functor at hand.

To get a flavor of derived functors in a classical setting, let
R
and
S
be two rings, and let
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(S)
be an additive covariant functor between module categories. For any
i\geq 0
, the
i
th left derived functor of
\mathcal{F}
is a functor
\textrm{L}_{i}\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(S)
, whose effect on a left
R
-module
M
is given by
\textrm{L}_{i}\mathcal{F}(M)=H_{i}\left(\mathcal{F}(P)\right)
where
P
is a chosen projective resolution of
M
, and the
i
th right derived functor of
\mathcal{F}
is a functor
\textrm{R}^{i}\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(S)
, whose effect on a left
R
-module
M
is given by
\textrm{R}^{i}\mathcal{F}(M)=H_{-i}\left(\mathcal{F}(I)\right)
where
I
is a chosen injective resolution of
M
. To go one step further, one can assemble all these derived functors into a single total derived functor. More specifically, let
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{C}(R)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(S)
be a covariant functor between categories of chain complexes that preserves homotopy. Then the total left derived functor of
\mathcal{F}
is a functor
\textrm{L}\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{D}(R)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(S)
between derived categories, whose effect on an
R
-complex
X
is given by
\textrm{L}\mathcal{F}(X)=\mathcal{F}(P)
where
P
is a chosen semi-projective resolution of
X
, and the total right derived functor of
\mathcal{F}
is a functor
\textrm{R}\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{D}(R)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(S)
, whose effect on an
R
-complex
X
is given by
\textrm{R}\mathcal{F}(X)=\mathcal{F}(I)
where
I
is a chosen semi-injective resolution of
X
. This machinery can be developed in abelian categories and their corresponding derived categories. However, there are certain contexts in which one requires to have some sort of derived functors in a non-abelian setting where the notion of a chain complex is missing.

One such situation arises in the context of commutative algebras over commutative rings. Let
R
be a commutative ring,
A
a commutative
R
-algebra, and
M
an
A
-module. A derivation of
A
over
R
with coefficients in
M
is an
R
-homomorphism
D:A\rightarrow M
that satisfies the Leibniz rule, i.e.
D(ab)=aD(b)+bD(a)
for every
a,b\in A
. The set of all derivations of
A
over
R
with coefficients in
M
, denoted by
\operatorname{Der}_{R}(A,M)
, is an
A
-submodule of
\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(A,M)
. In addition, the induced functor
\operatorname{Der}_{R}(A,-):\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(A)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(A)
is representable, i.e. there exists an
A
-module
\Omega_{A|R}
, the so-called module of differentials of
A
over
R
, and a universal derivation
d_{A|R}:A\rightarrow\Omega_{A|R}
that induces a natural
A
-isomorphism
\operatorname{Der}_{R}(A,M)\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{A}\left(\Omega_{A|R},M\right)
. The module of differentials
\Omega_{A|R}
can be concretely described as
\Omega_{A|R}=\mathfrak{k}/\mathfrak{k}^{2}
where
\mathfrak{k}=\operatorname{Ker}(\mu)
in which
\mu:A\otimes_{R}A\rightarrow A
is given by
\mu(a\otimes b)=ab
for every
a,b\in A
. In addition, the universal derivation
d_{A|R}:A\rightarrow\Omega_{A|R}
is given by
d_{A|R}(a)=(1\otimes a-a\otimes 1)+\mathfrak{k}^{2}
for every
a\in A
. Now assume that
R\rightarrow S\rightarrow T
are two homomorphisms of commutative rings, and
M
is a
T
-module. Then one has the following exact sequences:

\Omega_{S|R}\otimes_{S}M\rightarrow\Omega_{T|R}\otimes_{T}M\rightarrow\Omega_{T|S}\otimes_{T}M\rightarrow 0

and

0\rightarrow\operatorname{Der}_{S}(T,M)\rightarrow\operatorname{Der}_{R}(T,M)\rightarrow\operatorname{Der}_{R}(S,M)

Historically speaking, people grew interested in extending these sequences beyond the above-displayed three terms. Viewing derivations and differentials as functors of commutative algebras, one requires to have some sort of derived functors in order to extend the sequences. However, the category of commutative algebras is not abelian; it fails to have a zero object or a zero morphism. As a consequence, the machinery of homological algebra as we know it fails in this situation.

The solution comes from Quillen’s model category theory. The ideas behind the theory stem from homotopy theory in algebraic topology that are suitably adapted to the language of category theory. This has led to the emergence of non-abelian homological algebra. A model category is a locally small bicomplete category equipped with three distinguished classes of morphisms called fibrations, cofibrations, and weak equivalences that satisfy certain axioms. A model category supports a notion of replacement and a notion of homotopy that allow one to construct derived functors in a more general setting than the derived categories of abelian categories. We outline the general theory of derived functors as follows. If
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{G}:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
are two functors between arbitrary categories, and
\mathcal{G}^{\ast}:\mathcal{D}^{\mathcal{E}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\mathcal{C}}
denotes the right composition functor, then a left Kan extension of
\mathcal{F}
along
\mathcal{G}
is a functor
\operatorname{LKan}_{\mathcal{G}}(\mathcal{F}):\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
together with a natural transformation
\sigma:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\operatorname{LKan}_{\mathcal{G}}(\mathcal{F})\mathcal{G}
such that the pair
\left(\operatorname{LKan}_{\mathcal{G}}(\mathcal{F}),\sigma\right)
is an initial object in the comma category
\left(\mathcal{F}\downarrow\mathcal{G}^{\ast}\right)
, and a right Kan extension of
\mathcal{F}
along
\mathcal{G}
is a functor
\operatorname{RKan}_{\mathcal{G}}(\mathcal{F}):\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
together with a natural transformation
\varsigma:\operatorname{RKan}_{\mathcal{G}}(\mathcal{F})\mathcal{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
such that the pair
\left(\operatorname{RKan}_{\mathcal{G}}(\mathcal{F}),\varsigma\right)
is a terminal object in the comma category
\left(\mathcal{G}^{\ast}\downarrow\mathcal{F}\right)
. Now if
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
is a functor between model categories, then the left derived functor of
\mathcal{F}
is
\textrm{L}\mathcal{F}=\operatorname{RKan}_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{C}}}(\mathcal{\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}F}):\operatorname{Ho}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\operatorname{Ho}(\mathcal{D})
, and the right derived functor of
\mathcal{F}
is
\textrm{R}\mathcal{F}=\operatorname{LKan}_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{C}}}(\mathcal{\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}F}):\operatorname{Ho}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\operatorname{Ho}(\mathcal{D})
, where
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{C}}:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\operatorname{Ho}(\mathcal{C})
and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{D}}:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\operatorname{Ho}(\mathcal{D})
are localization functors. Derived functors at this level of generality can be shown to exist under mild conditions by leveraging fibrant and cofibrant replacements which in turn can be thought of as generalizations of resolutions. It is also worth noting that if
R
is a ring, then the category
\mathcal{C}(R)
of
R
-complexes has two model structures under any of which, one has
\operatorname{Ho}\left(\mathcal{C}(R)\right)\cong\mathcal{D}(R)
, and the general theory of derived functors reduces to the special abelian case described before.

In order to effectively apply Quillen’s theory to the situation of interest, i.e. commutative algebras over commutative rings, one needs a non-abelian substitute for chain complexes. The Dold-Kan Correspondence is inspiring in this direction. It states that the category of connective chain complexes over a ring is equivalent to the category of simplicial modules over the ring. Although chain complexes are not available in non-abelian categories, simplicial objects are always at our disposal in any category. The punch line is that simplicial objects can serve as the non-abelian analogues of chain complexes. In particular, one should consider the category of simplicial commutative algebras in this situation and equip it with a model structure. Unfortunately, this task is by far harder than it seems. In addition, all the existing arguments in the literature rely one way or another on the model structure of simplicial sets; see for example
[
Qu2
, Chapter II, Section 3]
. Simplicial sets can be regarded as combinatorial models for topological spaces up to homotopy. Both topological spaces and simplicial sets are primordial prototypes of model categories. Let us look at the classical model structure of simplicial sets. Fibrations are Kan fibrations, cofibrations are injective morphisms, and weak equivalences are morphisms whose geometric realizations induce isomorphisms on topological homotopy groups. As it stands, the weak equivalences lack a purely algebraic description, so the proof of model structure of simplicial sets and hence simplicial commutative algebras is based partly on topological foundations. From an algebraist’s point of view, it can be reasonable to look for a purely algebraic proof of the model structure of simplicial commutative algebras. This is in fact the motivation of writing this article.

Back to the commutative algebra setting, we observe that after equipping the category of simplicial commutative algebras with a model structure, one can apply the general theory of derived functors to construct the cotangent complex
\mathbb{L}^{A|R}\in\operatorname{Obj}\left(\mathcal{D}(A)\right)
as the image of the identity map
1^{A}:A\rightarrow A
under the left derived functor of the abelianization functor followed by the right derived functor of the normalization functor. The cotangent complex is in turn used to define the André-Quillen homology and cohomology modules as
H_{i}^{AQ}\left(A|R;M\right)=\operatorname{Tor}_{i}^{A}\left(\mathbb{L}^{A|R},M\right)
and
H_{AQ}^{i}\left(A|R;M\right)=\operatorname{Ext}_{A}^{i}\left(\mathbb{L}^{A|R},M\right)
for every
i\geq 0
. These modules enable one to extend the above-mentioned sequences to the following exact sequences:

\displaystyle\cdots\rightarrow H_{2}^{AQ}\left(S|R;M\right)\rightarrow H_{2}^{AQ}\left(T|R;M\right)\rightarrow H_{2}^{AQ}\left(T|S;M\right)\rightarrow H_{1}^{AQ}\left(S|R;M\right)\rightarrow

\displaystyle H_{1}^{AQ}\left(T|R;M\right)\rightarrow H_{1}^{AQ}\left(T|S;M\right)\rightarrow\Omega_{S|R}\otimes_{S}M\rightarrow\Omega_{T|R}\otimes_{T}M\rightarrow\Omega_{T|S}\otimes_{T}M\rightarrow 0

and

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow\operatorname{Der}_{S}(T,M)\rightarrow\operatorname{Der}_{R}(T,M)\rightarrow\operatorname{Der}_{R}(S,M)\rightarrow H_{AQ}^{1}\left(T|S;M\right)\rightarrow H_{AQ}^{1}\left(T|R;M\right)\rightarrow

\displaystyle H_{AQ}^{1}\left(S|R;M\right)\rightarrow H_{AQ}^{2}\left(T|S;M\right)\rightarrow H_{AQ}^{2}\left(T|R;M\right)\rightarrow H_{AQ}^{2}\left(S|R;M\right)\rightarrow\cdots

As it turns out, André-Quillen homology and cohomology modules are powerful tools in commutative algebra that can characterize various classes of rings and algebras such as regular rings, complete intersection rings, smooth algebras, étale algebras, etc. They have numerous applications in number theory and deformation theory as well; see for example
[
An
]
,
[
Av
]
,
[
BI
]
,
[
BIK
]
,
[
DI
]
,
[
Fa
]
,
[
Il
]
,
[
Iy
]
,
[
MR
]
,
[
Qu1
]
,
[
Qu2
]
, and
[
Qu3
]
. Despite being a desirable object of study for algebraists, André-Quillen homology and cohomology modules have not received the kind of attention they merit from the algebra community. This could be mostly due to its foundation that has not been properly developed in an algebraic language. The surprising fact is that the foundation of the theory, i.e. the model structure of simplicial commutative algebras, is rooted in topology, while its applications, i.e. the characterization of various classes of rings and algebras, fit completely within the realm of commutative algebra. In this article, we strive to provide a purely algebraic proof of the model structure of simplicial commutative algebras that does not rely on the model structure of simplicial sets or topological spaces.

The article is designed as follows. In section two, we collect all we need from the theory of model categories with suitable references in order to set up the language and notation. We further prove a few lemmas that will be used later. In section three, we provide a proof of the model structure of connective chain complexes. The direct proof of this fact is only outlined in the literature in a disperse and incomplete manner; see for example
[
GS
, Theorem 1.5]
or
[
DS
, Theorem 7.2]
. We give a neat and complete proof for the sake of reference. This model structure is described completely algebraically and serves as our archetypal example of a model category. Furthermore, we show that this model structure is cofibrantly generated which allows us to transfer it along adjunctions. In section four, we review simplicial objects, the normalization and Dold-Kan functors, and the Dold-Kan Correspondence. In addition, we transfer the model structure of connective chain complexes to simplicial modules. In section five, we take advantage of the Dold-Kan functor to introduce and study the shuffle product of connective chain complexes. The shuffle product of elements of chain complexes has been studied in the literature; see
[
Mc2
, Theorem 8.8]
and
[
Lu
, Proposition 2.5.7.1 and Notation 2.5.7.2]
. However, the shuffle product of connective chain complexes is a new perspective. We then prove a variant of the Eilenberg-Zilber Theorem. The contents of this section are of grave significance in the sequel. In section six, we deploy the materials of section five and the Quillen-Kan Transfer Machine to transport the model structure of connective chain complexes to simplicial commutative algebras. One should note that
[
GS
]
adopts the same approach, but at the most crucial step, i.e. the establishment of the acyclicity condition, it resorts to the model structure of simplicial sets; see
[
GS
, Proposition 4.12, Corollary 4.14, and Theorem 4.17]
and
[
GJ
, Proposition 11.5, Axiom 3.1, Theorem 4.1, and Example 5.2]
. We mainly follow the ideas of
[
GS
]
, but leverage the shuffle product to avoid the model structure of simplicial sets altogether. As it happens, we wind up with an organized, self-contained, and completely algebraic proof with no reliance on topology.

2.
Preliminaries on Model Categories

Quillen’s theory of model categories provides a general framework to practice homological algebra far beyond the realm of abelian categories and their corresponding derived categories. In this section, we collect some basic definitions and facts from the theory of model categories and also prove a few lemmas that will be needed later. For general references on model categories, refer to
[
Ci
]
,
[
DS
]
,
[
GJ
]
,
[
Hi
]
,
[
Ho
]
,
[
Qu2
]
, and
[
Ri
]
.

Definition 2.1
.

A
model category
is a locally small bicomplete category
\mathcal{C}
endowed with three classes of morphisms called
fibrations
,
cofibrations
, and
weak equivalences
. A morphism that is a fibration as well as a weak equivalence is called a
trivial fibration
, and a morphism that is a cofibration as well as a weak equivalence is called a
trivial cofibration
. Moreover, these morphisms should satisfy the following conditions:

(i)

Lifting
: Any commutative diagram on the left in which
f
is a trivial cofibration and
g
is a fibration, or
f
is a cofibration and
g
is a trivial fibration, can be completed to the commutative diagram on the right:

\lxSVG@closescope {

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(ii)

Retract
: If
f
is a retract of
g
in the arrow category
\operatorname{Arr}(\mathcal{C})
, i.e. there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

in which
lh=1^{A}
and
qp=1^{B}
, and if
g
is a fibration (cofibration, or weak equivalence), then
f
is a fibration (cofibration, or weak equivalence).

(iii)

Two-Out-of-Three
: If
f=gh
, and any two of
f
,
g
, or
h
are weak equivalences, then so is the third.

(iv)

Factorization
: For any morphism
f
, there is a factorization
f=gh
for a fibration
g
and a trivial cofibration
h
, and also a factorization
f=g^{\prime}h^{\prime}
for a trivial fibration
g^{\prime}
and a cofibration
h^{\prime}
.

A model category supports a notion of homotopy which is the bedrock of constructing derived functors. In order to define this notion, we need the definitions of cylinder and path objects which could be shown to exist in every model category.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a model category, and
A\in\operatorname{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
. Then:

(i)

A
cylinder object
for
A
is an object
\operatorname{Cyl}(A)
through which the codiagonal morphism
\nabla_{A}:A\sqcup A\rightarrow A
factors as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\sqcup A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Cyl}(A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{A}}

in which
\kappa_{A}
is a cofibration and
\xi_{A}
is a weak equivalence.

(ii)

A
path object
for
A
is an object
\operatorname{Path}(A)
through which the diagonal morphism
\Delta_{A}:A\rightarrow A\sqcap A
factors as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\sqcap A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Path}(A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{A}}

in which
\nu_{A}
is a fibration and
\zeta_{A}
is a weak equivalence.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a model category, and
f,g:A\rightarrow B
two morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
. Then:

(i)

f
is said to be
left homotopic
to
g
, denoted by
f\sim_{l}g
, if there is a cylinder object
\operatorname{Cyl}(A)
for
A
, as in Definition
2.2
, such that
f
and
g
factor through
\operatorname{Cyl}(A)
as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Cyl}(A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{A}\iota_{1}^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{A}\iota_{2}^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

in which
\iota_{1}^{A}:A\rightarrow A\sqcup A
and
\iota_{2}^{A}:A\rightarrow A\sqcup A
are canonical injections. Moreover in this case,
\phi
is said to be a
left homotopy
from
f
to
g
.

(ii)

f
is said to be
right homotopic
to
g
, denoted by
f\sim_{r}g
, if there is a path object
\operatorname{Path}(B)
for
B
, as in Definition
2.2
, such that
f
and
g
factor through
\operatorname{Path}(B)
as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Path}(B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}^{B}\nu_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{B}\nu_{B}}

in which
\pi_{1}^{B}:B\sqcap B\rightarrow B
and
\pi_{2}^{B}:B\sqcap B\rightarrow B
are canonical projections. Moreover in this case,
\psi
is said to be a
right homotopy
from
f
to
g
.

(iii)

f
is said to be
homotopic
to
g
, denoted by
f\sim g
, if
f\sim_{l}g
and
f\sim_{r}g
.

Recall that a model category is bicomplete, i.e. it has all small direct and inverse limits. In particular, a model category has initial and terminal objects. If
\mathcal{C}
is a model category,
\circ
is its initial object, and
\ast
is its terminal object, then an object
A
in
\mathcal{C}
is said to be
fibrant
if the unique morphism
A\rightarrow\ast
is a fibration, and is said to be
cofibrant
if the unique morphism
\circ\rightarrow A
is a cofibration. Now we can see that homotopy is an equivalence relation on morphisms.

Proposition 2.4
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a model category, and
f,g:A\rightarrow B
two morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
. Then the following assertions hold:

(i)

If
f\sim_{l}g
and
A
is cofibrant, then
f\sim_{r}g
and
\sim_{l}
is an equivalence relation on
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,B)
.

(ii)

If
f\sim_{r}g
and
B
is fibrant, then
f\sim_{l}g
and
\sim_{r}
is an equivalence relation on
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,B)
.

(iii)

If
A
is cofibrant and
B
is fibrant, then
f\sim_{l}g
if and only if
f\sim_{r}g
, so
\sim
is an equivalence relation on
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,B)
.

Proof.

See
[
Hi
, Proposition 7.4.5]
.
∎

A model category enjoys a concept of replacement which can be thought of as a generalization as well as a non-abelian analogue of a resolution in abelian homological algebra. If
\mathcal{C}
is a model category, and
A
is an object in
\mathcal{C}
, then a
fibrant replacement
of
A
is a trivial cofibration
\varrho_{A}:A\rightarrow A_{{\textrm{f}}}
in which
A_{{\textrm{f}}}
is fibrant, and a
cofibrant replacement
of
A
is a trivial fibration
\rho_{A}:A_{{\textrm{c}}}\rightarrow A
in which
A_{{\textrm{c}}}
is cofibrant. Now we have a comparison lemma for replacements as expected.

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a model category. Then the following assertions hold:

(i)

Every object
A
of
\mathcal{C}
has a fibrant replacement
\varrho_{A}:A\rightarrow A_{\emph{f}}
. Choosing a fibrant replacement for each object of
\mathcal{C}
, if
f:A\rightarrow B
is a morphism in
\mathcal{C}
, then there is a morphism
f_{\emph{f}}:A_{\emph{f}}\rightarrow B_{\emph{f}}
which is unique up to homotopy and makes the following diagram commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\emph{f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{\emph{f}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\emph{f}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho_{B}}

Moreover, if
g:B\rightarrow C
is another morphism in
\mathcal{C}
, then
(gf)_{\emph{f}}\sim g_{\emph{f}}f_{\emph{f}}
, and
(1^{A})_{\emph{f}}\sim 1^{A_{\emph{f}}}
.

(ii)

Every object
A
of
\mathcal{C}
has a cofibrant replacement
\rho_{A}:A_{\emph{c}}\rightarrow A
. Choosing a cofibrant replacement for each object of
\mathcal{C}
, if
f:A\rightarrow B
is a morphism in
\mathcal{C}
, then there is a morphism
f_{\emph{c}}:A_{\emph{c}}\rightarrow B_{\emph{c}}
which is unique up to homotopy and makes the following diagram commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\emph{c}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{\emph{c}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\emph{c}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{B}}

Moreover, if
g:B\rightarrow C
is another morphism in
\mathcal{C}
, then
(gf)_{\emph{c}}\sim g_{\emph{c}}f_{\emph{c}}
, and
(1^{A})_{\emph{c}}\sim 1^{A_{\emph{c}}}
.

Proof.

See
[
Hi
, Corollary 8.1.8]
.
∎

The notions of fibrant and cofibrant replacements also allow us to define the homotopy category of a model category which can be thought of as a generalization as well as a non-abelian analogue of the derived category of an abelian category.

Definition 2.6
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a model category. Choose a fibrant replacement
\varrho_{A}:A\rightarrow A_{{\textrm{f}}}
as well as a cofibrant replacement
\rho_{A}:A_{{\textrm{c}}}\rightarrow A
for every
A\in\operatorname{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
. Then the
homotopy category

\operatorname{Ho}(\mathcal{C})
of
\mathcal{C}
is defined as follows:

(i)

\operatorname{Obj}\left({\textrm{Ho}}(\mathcal{C})\right):=\operatorname{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
.

(ii)

\operatorname{Mor}_{{\textrm{Ho}}(\mathcal{C})}(A,B):=\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}(A_{{\textrm{fc}}},B_{{\textrm{fc}}})/\sim
for every
A,B\in\operatorname{Obj}\left({\textrm{Ho}}(\mathcal{C})\right)
.

We next have the universal property of the homotopy category which in turn shows that the homotopy category is the localization of the model category with respect to the class of weak equivalences.

Proposition 2.7
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a model category. Choose a fibrant replacement
\varrho_{A}:A\rightarrow A_{\emph{f}}
as well as a cofibrant replacement
\rho_{A}:A_{\emph{c}}\rightarrow A
for every
A\in\operatorname{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
. Then there is a covariant functor
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{C}}:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\operatorname{Ho}(\mathcal{C})
defined as
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{C}}(A):=A
for every
A\in\operatorname{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
, and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{C}}(f):=[f_{\emph{fc}}]
for every
f\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,B)
. Moreover,
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{C}}
has the following properties:

(i)

A morphism
f
in
\mathcal{C}
is a weak equivalence if and only if
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{C}}(f)
is an isomorphism in
\operatorname{Ho}(\mathcal{C})
.

(ii)

If
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
is a functor that maps weak equivalences in
\mathcal{C}
to isomorphisms in
\mathcal{D}
, then
\mathcal{F}
factors uniquely through
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{C}}
, i.e. there is a unique functor
\mathcal{\check{F}}:\operatorname{Ho}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
that makes the following diagram commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ho}(\mathcal{C})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{\check{F}}}

Proof.

See
[
Hi
, Proposition 8.3.7 and Theorem 8.3.10]
.
∎

We next focus on cofibrantly generated model categories whose importance stems from the transferability of their model structure along adjunctions.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category, and
f:A\rightarrow B
and
g:C\rightarrow D
two morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
. If any commutative diagram as the one on the left can be completed to the commutative diagram on the right

\hbox to77.87pt{\vbox to46.31pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 38.93292pt\lower-23.15279pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

then we say that
f
has the
left lifting property against

g
and
g
has the
right lifting property against

f
. Given a class
\mathcal{X}
of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
, let
\operatorname{LLP}(\mathcal{X})
denote the class of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
that have the left lifting property against all morphisms in
\mathcal{X}
, and
\operatorname{RLP}(\mathcal{X})
denote the class of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
that have the right lifting property against all morphisms in
\mathcal{X}
. If
\mathcal{C}
is a model category, then by
[
Hi
, Proposition 7.2.3]
,
g
is a fibration if and only if it has the right lifting property against all trivial cofibrations;
g
is a trivial fibration if and only if it has the right lifting property against all cofibrations;
f
is a cofibration if and only if it has the left lifting property against all trivial fibrations; and
f
is a trivial cofibration if and only if it has the left lifting property against all fibrations.

Recall that if
\mathcal{C}
is a locally small cocomplete category and
\mathcal{X}
is a class of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
, then an object
A
of
\mathcal{C}
is said to be
small
with respect to
\mathcal{X}
if the functor
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,-):\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}
preserves sequential direct limits of direct systems in
\mathcal{X}
, i.e. for any sequence
\left\{g_{n}:B_{n}\rightarrow B_{n+1}\right\}_{n\geq 1}
of morphisms in
\mathcal{X}
, the natural morphism

\underset{n\geq 1}{\varinjlim}\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,B_{n})\rightarrow\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(A,\underset{n\geq 1}{\varinjlim}B_{n}\right)

is an isomorphism.

Quillen devised a method to transfer model structures along adjunctions which is primarily based on his well-known ”small object argument”. A set
\mathcal{X}
of morphisms in a locally small cocomplete category
\mathcal{C}
is said to
permit the small object argument
if the source of any morphism in
\mathcal{X}
is small with respect to
\operatorname{LLP}\left(\operatorname{RLP}(\mathcal{X})\right)
. This condition is necessary in order to be able to run the small object argument. We now recall the notion of a cofibrantly generated model category.

Definition 2.8
.

A model category
\mathcal{C}
is said to be
cofibrantly generated
if there exist two sets
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
of morphisms in
C
that satisfy the following conditions:

(i)

\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
permit the small object argument.

(ii)

\operatorname{RLP}(\mathcal{X})
is the class of trivial fibrations.

(iii)

\operatorname{RLP}(\mathcal{Y})
is the class of fibrations.

The next theorem is known as Quillen-Kan Transfer Machine.

Theorem 2.9
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a cofibrantly generated model category with the corresponding sets of morphisms
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
,
\mathcal{D}
a locally small bicomplete category, and
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}):\mathcal{C}\leftrightarrows\mathcal{D}
an adjoint pair of functors. Then:

(i)

Let a morphism
f:A\rightarrow B
in
\mathcal{D}
be a fibration if
\mathcal{G}(f):\mathcal{G}(A)\rightarrow\mathcal{G}(B)
is a fibration in
\mathcal{C}
.

(ii)

Let a morphism
f:A\rightarrow B
in
\mathcal{D}
be a weak equivalence if
\mathcal{G}(f):\mathcal{G}(A)\rightarrow\mathcal{G}(B)
is a weak equivalence in
\mathcal{C}
.

(iii)

Let a morphism
f:A\rightarrow B
in
\mathcal{D}
be a cofibration if it has the left lifting property against trivial fibrations defined in (i) and (ii).

With the above definitions of fibrations, cofibrations, and weak equivalences in place, if the sets of morphisms
\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{X})
and
\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{Y})
permit the small object argument, and any cofibration in
\mathcal{D}
that has the left lifting property against fibrations is also a weak equivalence, then
\mathcal{D}
becomes a cofibrantly generated model category.

Proof.

See
[
Hi
, Theorem 11.3.2]
.
∎

The last condition in Theorem
2.9
is commonly known as the
“acyclicity condition”
which is in practice notoriously hard to establish. We next prove a lemma that facilitates the permission of small object argument in our cases of interest.

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
be two locally small cocomplete categories, and
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}):\mathcal{C}\leftrightarrows\mathcal{D}
an adjoint pair of functors in which
\mathcal{G}
preserves sequential direct limits. Suppose that
\mathcal{X}
is a set of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
such that the source of any morphism in
\mathcal{X}
is small with respect to
\mathcal{G}\left(\operatorname{LLP}\left(\operatorname{RLP}\left(\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{X})\right)\right)\right)
. Then
\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{X})=\left\{F(f)\;|\;f\in\mathcal{X}\right\}
permits the small object argument.

Proof.

Let
f:A\rightarrow B
be a morphism in
\mathcal{X}
, and consider the morphism
\mathcal{F}(f):\mathcal{F}(A)\rightarrow\mathcal{F}(B)
in
\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{X})
. We need to show that
\mathcal{F}(A)
is small with respect to
\operatorname{LLP}\left(\operatorname{RLP}\left(\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{X})\right)\right)
. To this end, let
\left\{h_{n}:C_{n}\rightarrow C_{n+1}\right\}_{n\geq 1}
be a sequence of morphisms in
\operatorname{LLP}\left(\operatorname{RLP}\left(\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{X})\right)\right)
. Let
\phi_{m}:C_{m}\rightarrow\underset{n\geq 1}{\varinjlim}C_{n}
be the canonical injection for every
m\geq 1
. Then there is a unique morphism
\eta:\underset{n\geq 1}{\varinjlim}\mathcal{G}(C_{n})\rightarrow\mathcal{G}\left(\underset{n\geq 1}{\varinjlim}C_{n}\right)
that makes the following diagram commutative for every
m\geq 1
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(C_{m})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(C_{m+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{n\geq 1}{\varinjlim}\mathcal{G}(C_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}\left(\underset{n\geq 1}{\varinjlim}C_{n}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(h_{m})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(\phi_{m})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{m+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(\phi_{m+1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

Since
\mathcal{G}
preserves sequential direct limits,
\eta
is an isomorphism. Moreover, there is a unique morphism
\theta:\underset{n\geq 1}{\varinjlim}\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(A,\mathcal{G}(C_{n})\right)\rightarrow\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(A,\underset{n\geq 1}{\varinjlim}\mathcal{G}(C_{n})\right)
that makes the following diagram commutative for every
m\geq 1
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(A,\mathcal{G}(C_{m})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(A,\mathcal{G}(C_{m+1})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{n\geq 1}{\varinjlim}\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(A,\mathcal{G}(C_{n})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(A,\underset{n\geq 1}{\varinjlim}\mathcal{G}(C_{n})\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(A,\mathcal{G}(h_{m})\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,\psi_{m})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{m+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,\psi_{m+1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}

Also, there is a unique morphism
\vartheta:\underset{n\geq 1}{\varinjlim}\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(A,\mathcal{G}(C_{n})\right)\rightarrow\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(A,\mathcal{G}\left(\underset{n\geq 1}{\varinjlim}C_{n}\right)\right)
that makes the following diagram commutative for every
m\geq 1
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(A,\mathcal{G}(C_{m})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(A,\mathcal{G}(C_{m+1})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{n\geq 1}{\varinjlim}\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(A,\mathcal{G}(C_{n})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(A,\mathcal{G}\left(\underset{n\geq 1}{\varinjlim}C_{n}\right)\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(A,\mathcal{G}(h_{m})\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(A,\mathcal{G}(\phi_{m})\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{m+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(A,\mathcal{G}(\phi_{m+1})\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta}

Now we see that the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{n\geq 1}{\varinjlim}\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(A,\mathcal{G}(C_{n})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(A,\mathcal{G}\left(\underset{n\geq 1}{\varinjlim}C_{n}\right)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(A,\underset{n\geq 1}{\varinjlim}\mathcal{G}(C_{n})\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,\eta)}

Indeed, we have for every
m\geq 1
:

\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,\eta)\theta\chi_{m}=\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,\eta)\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,\psi_{m})=\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,\eta\psi_{m})=\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(A,\mathcal{G}(\phi_{m})\right)=\vartheta\chi_{m}

It follows that
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,\eta)\theta=\vartheta
as desired. We next note that for any
n\geq 1
, we have
h_{n}\in\operatorname{LLP}\left(\operatorname{RLP}\left(\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{X})\right)\right)
, so
\mathcal{G}(h_{n})\in\mathcal{G}\left(\operatorname{LLP}\left(\operatorname{RLP}\left(\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{X})\right)\right)\right)
. By the hypothesis,
A
is small with respect to
\mathcal{G}\left(\operatorname{LLP}\left(\operatorname{RLP}\left(\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{X})\right)\right)\right)
, so we conclude that
\theta
is an isomorphism. We had also observed above that
\eta
was an isomorphism, so
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,\eta)
is an isomorphism. Therefore,
\vartheta=\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,\eta)\theta
is an isomorphism. On the other hand, there is a unique morphism
\lambda:\underset{n\geq 1}{\varinjlim}\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}\left(\mathcal{F}(A),C_{n}\right)\rightarrow\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}\left(\mathcal{F}(A),\underset{n\geq 1}{\varinjlim}C_{n}\right)
that makes the following diagram commutative for every
m\geq 1
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}\left(\mathcal{F}(A),C_{m}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}\left(\mathcal{F}(A),C_{m+1}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{n\geq 1}{\varinjlim}\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}\left(\mathcal{F}(A),C_{n}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}\left(\mathcal{F}(A),\underset{n\geq 1}{\varinjlim}C_{n}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}\left(\mathcal{F}(A),h_{m}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}\left(\mathcal{F}(A),\phi_{m}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{m+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}\left(\mathcal{F}(A),\phi_{m+1}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}

Let
\sigma_{U,V}:\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}\left(\mathcal{F}(U),V\right)\rightarrow\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(U,\mathcal{G}(V)\right)
be the natural bijection of the adjunction for every
U\in\operatorname{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
and
V\in\operatorname{Obj}(\mathcal{D})
. Then the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{n\geq 1}{\varinjlim}\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}\left(\mathcal{F}(A),C_{n}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{n\geq 1}{\varinjlim}\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(A,\mathcal{G}(C_{n})\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}\left(\mathcal{F}(A),\underset{n\geq 1}{\varinjlim}C_{n}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(A,\mathcal{G}\left(\underset{n\geq 1}{\varinjlim}C_{n}\right)\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{n\geq 1}{\varinjlim}\sigma_{A,C_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{A,\underset{n\geq 1}{\varinjlim}C_{n}}}

Indeed, the commutativity of the above diagram follows from the naturality of the vertical morphisms applied to the natural transformation
\sigma_{A,-}:\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}\left(\mathcal{F}(A),-\right)\rightarrow\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(A,\mathcal{G}(-)\right)
. Now since all the morphisms in the above diagram are isomorphisms except possibly for
\lambda
, we infer that
\lambda
must also be an isomorphism. This means that
\mathcal{F}(A)
is small with respect to
\operatorname{LLP}\left(\operatorname{RLP}\left(\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{X})\right)\right)
. Therefore,
\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{X})
permits the small object argument.
∎

Corollary 2.11
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
be two locally small cocomplete categories, and
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}):\mathcal{C}\leftrightarrows\mathcal{D}
an adjoint pair of functors in which
\mathcal{G}
preserves sequential direct limits. Suppose that
\mathcal{X}
is a set of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
such that the source of any morphism in
\mathcal{X}
is small with respect to
\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{C})
. Then the source of any morphism in
\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{X})
is small with respect to
\operatorname{Mor}(\mathcal{D})
. In particular,
\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{X})
permits the small object argument.

Proof.

Follows from the proof of Lemma
2.10
.
∎

The following lemma might be of independent interest.

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
be two locally small categories, and
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}):\mathcal{C}\leftrightarrows\mathcal{D}
an adjoint pair of functors. Let
\sigma_{A,B}:\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}\left(\mathcal{F}(A),B\right)\rightarrow\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(A,\mathcal{G}(B)\right)
be the natural bijection of the adjunction for every
A\in\operatorname{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
and
B\in\operatorname{Obj}(\mathcal{D})
. Then the following assertions hold:

(i)

For any
f\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}\left(\mathcal{F}(A),B\right)
and
g\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(B,B^{\prime})
, we have
\sigma_{A,B^{\prime}}(gf)=\mathcal{G}(g)\sigma_{A,B}(f)
.

(ii)

For any
f\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(A,\mathcal{G}(B)\right)
and
g\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(B,B^{\prime})
, we have
\sigma_{A,B^{\prime}}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{G}(g)f\right)=g\sigma_{A,B}^{-1}(f)
.

(iii)

For any
g\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(A,\mathcal{G}(B)\right)
and
f\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}(A^{\prime},A)
, we have
\sigma_{A^{\prime},B}^{-1}(gf)=\sigma_{A,B}^{-1}(g)\mathcal{F}(f)
.

(iv)

For any
g\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}\left(\mathcal{F}(A),B\right)
and
f\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}(A^{\prime},A)
, we have
\sigma_{A^{\prime},B}\left(g\mathcal{F}(f)\right)=\sigma_{A,B}(g)f
.

Proof.

(i): Let
f\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}\left(\mathcal{F}(A),B\right)
and
g\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(B,B^{\prime})
. Let
u_{A}:A\rightarrow\mathcal{G}\left(\mathcal{F}(A)\right)
be the unit morphism of
A
. Then using
[
Mc1
, Page 82, Eq. (5)]
, we have
\sigma_{A,B^{\prime}}(gf)=\mathcal{G}(gf)u_{A}=\mathcal{G}(g)\mathcal{G}(f)u_{A}=\mathcal{G}(g)\sigma_{A,B}(f)
.

(ii): Let
f\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(A,\mathcal{G}(B)\right)
and
g\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(B,B^{\prime})
. Set
h=\sigma_{A,B^{\prime}}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{G}(g)f\right)
and
l=\sigma_{A,B}^{-1}(f)
. Using (i), we get
\sigma_{A,B^{\prime}}(gl)=\mathcal{G}(g)\sigma_{A,B}(l)=\mathcal{G}(g)f=\sigma_{A,B^{\prime}}(h)
. Since
\sigma_{A,B^{\prime}}
is bijective, we conclude that
g\sigma_{A,B}^{-1}(f)=gl=h=\sigma_{A,B^{\prime}}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{G}(g)f\right)
.

(iii): Similar to (i) using the counit morphism.

(iv): Similar to (ii).
∎

We can now compare the lifting properties along an adjunction. Note that the next lemma is in
[
Hi
, Theorem 7.2.17]
, but the proof there skips some crucial steps which we include here for clarity.

Lemma 2.13
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
be two locally small categories, and
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}):\mathcal{C}\leftrightarrows\mathcal{D}
an adjoint pair of functors. Let
f:A\rightarrow B
be a morphism in
\mathcal{C}
, and
g:C\rightarrow D
a morphism in
\mathcal{D}
. Then
\mathcal{F}(f)
has the left lifting property against
g
if and only if
f
has the left lifting property against
\mathcal{G}(g)
.

Proof.

Let
\sigma_{U,V}:\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}\left(\mathcal{F}(U),V\right)\rightarrow\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}}\left(U,\mathcal{G}(V)\right)
be the natural bijection of the adjunction for every
U\in\operatorname{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
and
V\in\operatorname{Obj}(\mathcal{D})
. Suppose that
\mathcal{F}(f)
has the left lifting property against
g
. Consider a commutative diagram as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(D)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}

Then the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{A,C}^{-1}(h)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{B,D}^{-1}(l)}

Indeed, using Lemma
2.12
(ii) and (iii), we have:

\sigma_{B,D}^{-1}(l)\mathcal{F}(f)=\sigma_{A,D}^{-1}(lf)=\sigma_{A,D}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{G}(g)h\right)=g\sigma_{A,C}^{-1}(h)

By the assumption, there is a morphism
u:\mathcal{F}(B)\rightarrow C
that makes the following diagram commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{A,C}^{-1}(h)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{B,D}^{-1}(l)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

Then the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(D)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{B,C}(u)}

Indeed, using Lemma
2.12
(i) and (iv), we have:

\sigma_{B,C}(u)f=\sigma_{A,C}\left(u\mathcal{F}(f)\right)=\sigma_{A,C}\left(\sigma_{A,C}^{-1}(h)\right)=h

and

\mathcal{G}(g)\sigma_{B,C}(u)=\sigma_{B,D}(gu)=\sigma_{B,D}\left(\sigma_{B,D}^{-1}(l)\right)=l

This shows that
f
has the left lifting property against
\mathcal{G}(g)
. The converse is similarly established.
∎

Corollary 2.14
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
be two locally small categories, and
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}):\mathcal{C}\leftrightarrows\mathcal{D}
an adjoint pair of functors. Let
\mathcal{X}
be a class of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
, and
\mathcal{Y}
a class of morphisms in
\mathcal{D}
. Then the following assertions hold:

(i)

For any morphism
f
in
\mathcal{C}
, we have
\mathcal{F}(f)\in\operatorname{LLP}(\mathcal{Y})
if and only if
f\in\operatorname{LLP}\left(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{Y})\right)
.

(ii)

For any morphism
g
in
\mathcal{D}
, we have
g\in\operatorname{RLP}\left(\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{X})\right)
if and only if
\mathcal{G}(g)\in\operatorname{RLP}\left(\mathcal{X}\right)
.

Proof.

(i): Let
f
be a morphism in
\mathcal{C}
. By Lemma
2.13
,
\mathcal{F}(f)\in\operatorname{LLP}(\mathcal{Y})
if and only if
\mathcal{F}(f)
has the left lifting property against every
g\in\mathcal{Y}
if and only if
f
has the left lifting property against every
\mathcal{G}(g)\in\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{Y})
if and only if
f\in\operatorname{LLP}\left(\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{Y})\right)
.

(ii): Similar to (i).
∎

The next proposition transfers model structures along equivalences.

Proposition 2.15
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a model category,
\mathcal{D}
a category, and
\mathcal{G}:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
a covariant equivalence of categories. Then
\mathcal{D}
becomes a model category by letting fibrations (cofibrations, or weak equivalences) be morphisms
f:A\rightarrow B
in
\mathcal{D}
such that
\mathcal{G}(f):\mathcal{G}(A)\rightarrow\mathcal{G}(B)
is a fibration (cofibration, or weak equivalence) in
\mathcal{C}
, respectively.

Proof.

One can directly check the conditions bearing in mind that
\mathcal{G}
is fully faithful.
∎

Corollary 2.16
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a cofibrantly generated model category,
\mathcal{D}
a category, and
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}):\mathcal{C}\leftrightarrows\mathcal{D}
an adjoint equivalence of categories. Then
\mathcal{D}
becomes a cofibrantly generated model category by letting fibrations (cofibrations, or weak equivalences) be morphisms
f:A\rightarrow B
in
\mathcal{D}
such that
\mathcal{G}(f):\mathcal{G}(A)\rightarrow\mathcal{G}(B)
is a fibration (cofibration, or weak equivalence) in
\mathcal{C}
, respectively.

Proof.

By Proposition
2.15
,
\mathcal{D}
is a model category with the specified classes of fibrations, cofibrations, and weak equivalences. Moreover, since
\mathcal{G}
is a covariant equivalence of categories,
\mathcal{G}
preserves direct limits. Now let
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
be sets of morphisms as in Definition
2.8
. Then
\operatorname{RLP}(\mathcal{X})
is the class of trivial fibrations and
\operatorname{RLP}(\mathcal{Y})
is the class of fibrations in
\mathcal{C}
. Since
\mathcal{C}
is a model category,
[
Hi
, Proposition 7.2.3]
implies that
\operatorname{LLP}\left(\operatorname{RLP}(\mathcal{X})\right)
is the class of cofibrations and
\operatorname{LLP}\left(\operatorname{RLP}(\mathcal{Y})\right)
is the class of trivial cofibrations in
\mathcal{C}
. By Corollary
2.14
(ii), for a given morphism
f
in
\mathcal{D}
, we have
f\in\operatorname{RLP}\left(\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{X})\right)
if and only if
\mathcal{G}(f)\in\operatorname{RLP}(\mathcal{X})
if and only if
\mathcal{G}(f)
is a trivial fibration in
C
if and only if
f
is a trivial fibration in
\mathcal{D}
. As a result,
\operatorname{RLP}\left(\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{X})\right)
is the class of trivial fibrations in
\mathcal{D}
. A similar argument shows that
\operatorname{RLP}\left(\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{Y})\right)
is the class of fibrations in
\mathcal{D}
. As
\mathcal{D}
is a model category,
\operatorname{LLP}\left(\operatorname{RLP}\left(\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{X})\right)\right)
is the class of cofibrations and
\operatorname{LLP}\left(\operatorname{RLP}\left(\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{Y})\right)\right)
is the class of trivial cofibrations in
\mathcal{D}
. By definition, if
f
is a cofibration in
\mathcal{D}
, then
\mathcal{G}(f)
is a cofibration in
\mathcal{C}
, meaning that
\mathcal{G}\left(\operatorname{LLP}\left(\operatorname{RLP}\left(\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{X})\right)\right)\right)\subseteq\operatorname{LLP}\left(\operatorname{RLP}\left(\mathcal{X}\right)\right)
. But
\mathcal{C}
is cofibrantly generated, so
\mathcal{X}
permits the small object argument, whence the source of any morphism in
\mathcal{X}
is small with respect to
\operatorname{LLP}\left(\operatorname{RLP}\left(\mathcal{X}\right)\right)
, hence with respect to
\mathcal{G}\left(\operatorname{LLP}\left(\operatorname{RLP}\left(\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{X})\right)\right)\right)
. By Lemma
2.10
,
\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{X})
permits the small object argument. A similar argument shows that
\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{Y})
permits the small object argument. Therefore,
\mathcal{D}
is cofibrantly generated with the corresponding sets
\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{X})
and
\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{Y})
.
∎

3.
Model Structure on Connective Chain Complexes

In this section, we use some ideas from
[
Ho
, Chapter 2, Section 2.3]
and
[
DS
, Section 7]
to cook up a straightforward and clear-cut proof of the model structure of connective chain complexes.

Let
R
be a ring. Recall that an
R
-complex is a
\mathbb{Z}
-indexed sequence

X:\cdots\rightarrow X_{i+1}\xrightarrow{\partial_{i+1}^{X}}X_{i}\xrightarrow{\partial_{i}^{X}}X_{i-1}\rightarrow\cdots

of left
R
-modules and
R
-homomorphisms such that
\partial_{i}^{X}\partial_{i+1}^{X}=0
for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. Given an
R
-complex
X
, we utilize the conventional notations
Z_{i}(X)=\operatorname{Ker}\left(\partial_{i}^{X}\right)
and
B_{i}(X)=\operatorname{Im}\left(\partial_{i+1}^{X}\right)
for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. An
R
-complex
X
is said to be
connective
if
X_{i}=0
for every
i<0
. The category of connective
R
-complexes is denoted by
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
which is a full subcategory of the category
\mathcal{C}(R)
of unbounded
R
-complexes. In this section, we directly show that
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
is a cofibrantly generated model category. The cofibrant generation property of this model structure allows us to transfer it along adjunctions.

We first recall the Eilenberg-MacLane complexes as follows. For any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we set
S(n):=\Sigma^{n}R
, i.e. the
R
-complex

\cdots\rightarrow 0\rightarrow R\rightarrow 0\rightarrow\cdots

where
R
is located in degree
n
. Then it is easy to verify that if
X
is an
R
-complex, then the map
\phi_{n}^{X}:\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}(R)}\left(S(n),X\right)\rightarrow Z_{n}(X)
, given by
f\mapsto f_{n}(1)
, is a natural isomorphism. Similarly, for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we set
D(n):=\Sigma^{n-1}\operatorname{Cone}\left(1^{R}\right)
, i.e. the
R
-complex

\cdots\rightarrow 0\rightarrow R\xrightarrow{1^{R}}R\rightarrow 0\rightarrow\cdots

where
R
is located in degrees
n
and
n-1
. Then it is easy to verify that if
X
is an
R
-complex, then the map
\psi^{X}_{n}:\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}(R)}\left(D(n),X\right)\rightarrow X_{n}
, given by
f\mapsto f_{n}(1)
, is a natural isomorphism. We make repeated use of these facts in the proof of the following theorem.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
R
be a ring. Then
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
is a cofibrantly generated model category with the model structure given as follows:

(i)

Fibrations are morphisms
f:X\rightarrow Y
in
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
such that
f_{i}:X_{i}\rightarrow Y_{i}
is surjective for every
i\geq 1
.

(ii)

Cofibrations are morphisms
f:X\rightarrow Y
in
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
such that
f_{i}:X_{i}\rightarrow Y_{i}
is injective and
\operatorname{Coker}(f_{i})
is a projective left
R
-module for every
i\geq 0
.

(iii)

Weak equivalences are quasi-isomorphisms, i.e. morphisms
f:X\rightarrow Y
in
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
such that
H_{i}(f):H_{i}(X)\rightarrow H_{i}(Y)
is an isomorphism for every
i\geq 0
.

Proof.

We first note that
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
is a locally small bicomplete category. Let

\mathcal{X}=\left\{\iota^{n}:S(n-1)\rightarrow D(n)\;|\;n\geq 1\right\}\cup\left\{\lambda:0\rightarrow S(0)\right\}

where
\iota^{n}
is the inclusion morphism for every
n\geq 1
. Also, let

\mathcal{Y}=\left\{\kappa^{n}:0\rightarrow D(n)\;|\;n\geq 1\right\}.

We show that
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
is a cofibrantly generated model category with the corresponding sets
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
of morphisms as in Definition
2.8
. For any
n\geq 0
, we have
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)}\left(S(n),-\right)\cong Z_{n}(-)
, so since the functor
Z_{n}(-)
preserves direct limits of direct systems, we conclude that the functor
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)}\left(S(n),-\right)
preserves direct limits of direct systems as well, thereby
S(n)
is small with respect to
\operatorname{Mor}\left(\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)\right)
. Also, the source of
\lambda
is
0
, so it is clearly small with respect to
\operatorname{Mor}\left(\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)\right)
. In particular, the sources of morphisms in
\mathcal{X}
are small with respect to
\operatorname{LLP}\left(\operatorname{RLP}(\mathcal{X})\right)
, so
\mathcal{X}
permits the small object argument. Similarly, since the source of any morphism in
\mathcal{Y}
is
0
, we see that
\mathcal{Y}
permits the small object argument.

We now show that
\operatorname{RLP}(\mathcal{X})
is the class of trivial fibrations in
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
. Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a morphism in
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
. Suppose that
f\in\operatorname{RLP}(\mathcal{X})
. We show that
f
is a trivial fibration, i.e.
f
is a quasi-isomorphism and
f_{n}
is surjective for every
n\geq 1
. Let
n\geq 0
. We first show that
H_{n}(f):H_{n}(X)\rightarrow H_{n}(Y)
is an isomorphism. To see that
H_{n}(f)
is injective, let
x\in Z_{n}(X)
be such that
H_{n}(f)\left(x+B_{n}(X)\right)=f_{n}(x)+B_{n}(Y)=0
. Then
f_{n}(x)\in B_{n}(Y)
, so
f_{n}(x)=\partial^{Y}_{n+1}(y)
for some
y\in Y_{n+1}
. Consider the natural isomorphisms
\phi_{n}^{X}:\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)}\left(S(n),X\right)\rightarrow Z_{n}(X)
and
\psi^{Y}_{n+1}:\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)}\left(D(n+1),Y\right)\rightarrow Y_{n+1}
. There is a
g\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)}\left(S(n),X\right)
such that
\phi_{n}^{X}(g)=g_{n}(1)=x
, and an
h\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)}\left(D(n+1),Y\right)
such that
\psi^{Y}_{n+1}(h)=h_{n+1}(1)=y
. Then the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(n+1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{n+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

Indeed, it is clear from the structure of
S(n)
that
\iota^{n+1}_{i}=0=g_{i}
for every
i\neq n
. Moreover, we have:

h_{n}\left(\iota_{n}^{n+1}(1)\right)=h_{n}(1)=\partial^{Y}_{n+1}\left(h_{n+1}(1)\right)=\partial^{Y}_{n+1}(y)=f_{n}(x)=f_{n}\left(g_{n}(1)\right)

By the choice of
f
, the above diagram can be completed to the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(n+1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{n+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}

Thus
\partial_{n+1}^{X}\left(l_{n+1}(1)\right)=l_{n}(1)=l_{n}\left(\iota_{n}^{n+1}(1)\right)=g_{n}(1)=x
, so
x\in B_{n}(X)
. This means that
H_{n}(f)
is injective. To see that
H_{n}(f)
is surjective, let
z\in Z_{n}(Y)
. If
n=0
, then consider the isomorphism
\phi_{0}^{Y}:\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)}\left(S(0),Y\right)\rightarrow Z_{0}(Y)
. There is an
r\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)}\left(S(0),Y\right)
such that
\phi_{0}^{Y}(r)=r_{0}(1)=z
. Then it is clear that the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}

By the choice of
f
, the above diagram can be completed to the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

Set
w=s_{0}(1)\in X_{0}=Z_{0}(X)
. Then
f_{0}(w)=f_{0}\left(s_{0}(1)\right)=r_{0}(1)=z
. If
n\geq 1
, then consider the isomorphism
\psi^{Y}_{n}:\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)}\left(D(n),Y\right)\rightarrow Y_{n}
. There is an
r\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)}\left(D(n),Y\right)
such that
\psi^{Y}_{n}(r)=r_{n}(1)=z
. Then the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S(n-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}

Indeed, it is clear that
\iota_{i}^{n}=0
for every
i\neq n-1
. Moreover, since
z\in Z_{n}(Y)
, we have
r_{n-1}\left(\iota_{n-1}^{n}(1)\right)=r_{n-1}(1)=\partial_{n}^{Y}\left(r_{n}(1)\right)=\partial_{n}^{Y}(z)=0
. By the choice of
f
, the above diagram can be completed to the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S(n-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

Set
w=s_{n}(1)\in X_{n}
. Then
f_{n}(w)=f_{n}\left(s_{n}(1)\right)=r_{n}(1)=z
. Moreover,
\partial_{n}^{X}(w)=\partial_{n}^{X}\left(s_{n}(1)\right)=s_{n-1}(1)=s_{n-1}\left(\iota_{n-1}^{n}(1)\right)=0
, so
w\in Z_{n}(X)
. In other words, for any
n\geq 0
and
z\in Z_{n}(Y)
, we have found an element
w\in Z_{n}(X)
such that
f_{n}(w)=z
. In particular, we have
H_{n}(f)\left(w+B_{n}(X)\right)=f_{n}(w)+B_{n}(Y)=z+B_{n}(Y)
. This shows that
H_{n}(f)
is surjective. Therefore,
H_{n}(f)
is an isomorphism. Next let
n\geq 1
. We show that
f_{n}
is surjective. Let
v\in Y_{n}
. Then
\partial_{n}^{Y}(v)\in B_{n-1}(Y)\subseteq Z_{n-1}(Y)
, so as noted above, there is an element
u\in Z_{n-1}(X)
such that
f_{n-1}(u)=\partial_{n}^{Y}(v)
. Consider the isomorphisms
\phi_{n-1}^{X}:\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)}\left(S(n-1),X\right)\rightarrow Z_{n-1}(X)
and
\psi^{Y}_{n}:\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)}\left(D(n),Y\right)\rightarrow Y_{n}
. There is a
p\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)}\left(S(n-1),X\right)
such that
\phi_{n-1}^{X}(p)=p_{n-1}(1)=u
, and a
q\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)}\left(D(n),Y\right)
such that
\psi^{Y}_{n}(q)=q_{n}(1)=v
. Then the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S(n-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

Indeed, it is clear that
\iota_{i}^{n}=0=p_{i}
for every
i\neq n-1
. Moreover, we have:

q_{n-1}\left(\iota_{n-1}^{n}(1)\right)=q_{n-1}(1)=\partial^{Y}_{n}\left(q_{n}(1)\right)=\partial^{Y}_{n}(v)=f_{n-1}(u)=f_{n-1}\left(p_{n-1}(1)\right)

By the choice of
f
, the above diagram can be completed to the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S(n-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

Then
f_{n}\left(t_{n}(1)\right)=q_{n}(1)=v
. Thus
f_{n}
is surjective. As a result,
f
is a trivial fibration.

Conversely, suppose that
f
is a trivial fibration, i.e.
f
is a quasi-isomorphism and
f_{n}
is surjective for every
n\geq 1
. We first show that
f_{0}
is also surjective. Consider the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{0}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{0}(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\partial_{1}^{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{0}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\partial_{1}^{Y}}}

As
f_{1}
is surjective, the above diagram implies that
B_{0}(f)
is surjective. Now consider the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{0}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{0}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{0}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{0}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{0}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{0}(f)}

Since
B_{0}(f)
is surjective and
H_{0}(f)
is an isomorphism, the Short Five Lemma implies that
f_{0}
is surjective. Therefore,
f
is a surjective quasi-isomorphism. We next show that
f\in\operatorname{RLP}(\mathcal{X})
. Let
n\geq 1
, and consider a commutative diagram as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S(n-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

Let
x=p_{n-1}(1)\in X_{n-1}
and
y=q_{n}(1)\in Y_{n}
. Then we have:

f_{n-1}(x)=f_{n-1}\left(p_{n-1}(1)\right)=q_{n-1}\left(\iota_{n-1}^{n}(1)\right)=q_{n-1}(1)=\partial_{n}^{Y}\left(q_{n}(1)\right)=\partial_{n}^{Y}(y)

Moreover,
\partial_{n-1}^{X}(x)=\partial_{n-1}^{X}\left(p_{n-1}(1)\right)=0
, so
x\in Z_{n-1}(X)
. But then
H_{n-1}(f)\left(x+B_{n-1}(X)\right)=f_{n-1}(x)+B_{n-1}(Y)=\partial_{n}^{Y}(y)+B_{n-1}(Y)=0
, so since
H_{n-1}(f)
is an isomorphism, we get
x+B_{n-1}(X)=0
, i.e.
x\in B_{n-1}(X)
, so
x=\partial_{n}^{X}(u)
for some
u\in X_{n}
. As
f
is a morphism of
R
-complexes, we have
\partial_{n}^{Y}(y)=f_{n-1}(x)=f_{n-1}\left(\partial_{n}^{X}(u)\right)=\partial_{n}^{Y}\left(f_{n}(u)\right)
, so
y-f_{n}(u)\in Z_{n}(Y)
. But
f
is a surjective quasi-isomorphism, so it is easy to see that
Z(f):Z(X)\rightarrow Z(Y)
is surjective. In particular, there is an element
v\in Z_{n}(X)
such that
f_{n}(v)=y-f_{n}(u)
. Set
z=u+v\in X_{n}
. Then
\partial_{n}^{X}(z)=\partial_{n}^{X}(u)+\partial_{n}^{X}(v)=\partial_{n}^{X}(u)=x
and
f_{n}(z)=f_{n}(u)+f_{n}(v)=y
. Consider the isomorphism
\psi^{X}_{n}:\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)}\left(D(n),X\right)\rightarrow X_{n}
. There is an
l\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)}\left(D(n),X\right)
such that
\psi^{X}_{n}(l)=l_{n}(1)=z
. Then the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S(n-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}

Indeed,
\iota_{i}^{n}=0
for every
i\neq n-1
. Furthermore, we have:

l_{n-1}\left(\iota_{n-1}^{n}(1)\right)=l_{n-1}(1)=\partial_{n}^{X}\left(l_{n}(1)\right)=\partial_{n}^{X}(z)=x=p_{n-1}(1)

On the other hand,
q_{i}=0=l_{i}
for every
i\neq n,n-1
. Moreover, we have
f_{n}\left(l_{n}(1)\right)=f_{n}(z)=y=q_{n}(1)
and

f_{n-1}\left(l_{n-1}(1)\right)=f_{n-1}\left(\partial_{n}^{X}\left(l_{n}(1)\right)\right)=f_{n-1}\left(\partial_{n}^{X}(z)\right)=f_{n-1}(x)=\partial_{n}^{Y}(y)=\partial_{n}^{Y}\left(q_{n}(1)\right)=q_{n-1}(1).

Next consider a commutative diagram as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}

By the surjectivity of
f_{0}
, there is an element
w\in X_{0}=Z_{0}(X)
such that
f_{0}(w)=r_{0}(1)
. Consider the isomorphism
\phi_{0}^{X}:\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)}\left(S(0),X\right)\rightarrow Z_{0}(X)
. There is an
s\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)}\left(S(0),X\right)
such that
\phi_{0}^{X}(s)=s_{0}(1)=w
. Then the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

Indeed, it is clear that the upper triangle is commutative. Moreover, for the lower triangle, we note that
s_{i}=0=r_{i}
for every
i\neq 0
. Also,
f_{0}\left(s_{0}(1)\right)=f_{0}(w)=r_{0}(1)
. This means that
f\in\operatorname{RLP}(\mathcal{X})
. Therefore,
\operatorname{RLP}(\mathcal{X})
is the class of trivial fibrations.

We next show that
\operatorname{RLP}(\mathcal{Y})
is the class of fibrations in
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
. Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a morphism in
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
. Suppose that
f\in\operatorname{RLP}(\mathcal{Y})
. We show that
f
is a fibration, i.e.
f_{n}
is surjective for every
n\geq 1
. Let
n\geq 1
and
v\in Y_{n}
. Consider the isomorphism
\psi^{Y}_{n}:\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)}\left(D(n),Y\right)\rightarrow Y_{n}
. There is a
q\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)}\left(D(n),Y\right)
such that
\psi^{Y}_{n}(q)=q_{n}(1)=v
. By the choice of
f
, the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

can be completed to the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

Then
f_{n}\left(t_{n}(1)\right)=q_{n}(1)=v
. This shows that
f_{n}
is surjective. As a result,
f
is a fibration.

Conversely, suppose that
f
is a fibration, i.e.
f_{n}
is surjective for every
n\geq 1
. Let
n\geq 1
, and consider a commutative diagram as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

Since
f_{n}
is surjective, there is an element
x\in X_{n}
such that
f_{n}(x)=q_{n}(1)
. Consider the isomorphism
\psi^{X}_{n}:\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)}\left(D(n),X\right)\rightarrow X_{n}
. There is an
l\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)}\left(D(n),X\right)
such that
\psi^{X}_{n}(l)=l_{n}(1)=x
. Then the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}

Indeed, it is obvious that the upper triangle is commutative. For the lower triangle, we note that
q_{i}=0=l_{i}
for every
i\neq n,n-1
. Moreover, using the fact that
f
is a morphism of
R
-complexes, we have
f_{n}\left(l_{n}(1)\right)=f_{n}(x)=q_{n}(1)
and

f_{n-1}\left(l_{n-1}(1)\right)=f_{n-1}\left(\partial_{n}^{X}\left(l_{n}(1)\right)\right)=f_{n-1}\left(\partial_{n}^{X}(x)\right)=\partial_{n}^{Y}\left(f_{n}(x)\right)=\partial_{n}^{Y}\left(q_{n}(1)\right)=q_{n-1}(1).

This implies that
f\in\operatorname{RLP}(\mathcal{Y})
. Therefore,
\operatorname{RLP}(\mathcal{Y})
is the class of fibrations.

We are now ready to check the axioms of the model category as follows:

Retract: Suppose that
f
is a retract of
g
, i.e. there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

in
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
in which
lh=1^{X}
and
qp=1^{Y}
. If
g
is a fibration, then
g_{i}
is surjective for every
i\geq 1
. On the other hand, the relation
q_{i}p_{i}=1^{Y_{i}}
implies that
q_{i}
is surjective for every
i\geq 0
. Therefore,
q_{i}g_{i}
is surjective for every
i\geq 1
. Now the relation
f_{i}l_{i}=q_{i}g_{i}
yields that
f_{i}
is surjective for every
i\geq 1
. That means that
f
is a fibration.

If
g
is a cofibration, then
g_{i}
is injective and
\operatorname{Coker}(g_{i})
is projective for every
i\geq 0
. The relation
l_{i}h_{i}=1^{X_{i}}
implies that
h_{i}
is injective for every
i\geq 0
. Therefore,
g_{i}h_{i}
is injective for every
i\geq 0
. Now the relation
p_{i}f_{i}=g_{i}h_{i}
yields that
f_{i}
is injective for every
i\geq 0
. Furthermore, we get the following induced commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Coker}(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Coker}(g)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Coker}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{q}}

It is also evident that
\bar{q}\bar{p}=1^{\operatorname{Coker}(f)}
. It follows that
\operatorname{Coker}(f)
is isomorphic to a direct summand of
\operatorname{Coker}(g)
. Therefore,
\operatorname{Coker}(f_{i})
is projective for every
i\geq 0
. Thus
f
is a cofibration.

Finally, if
g
is a weak equivalence, then
g
is a quasi-isomorphism. Fix
i\geq 0
. The functoriality of homology yields the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i}(Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i}(W)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i}(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i}(h)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i}(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i}(l)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i}(p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i}(q)}

Setting
\phi=H_{i}(l)H_{i}(g)^{-1}H_{i}(p):H_{i}(Y)\rightarrow H_{i}(X)
, we observe from the above commutative diagram that
\phi
is the inverse of
H_{i}(f)
, so
H_{i}(f)
is an isomorphism. That is to say,
f
is a quasi-isomorphism, hence a weak equivalence.

Two-Out-of-Three: Suppose that we have morphisms
f=gh
in
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
in which two of
f
,
g
, or
h
are quasi-isomorphisms. Then by the functoriality of homology, it is clear that the third morphism must be a quasi-isomorphism as well.

First Half of Lifting: Consider a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}

in
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
in which
f
is a cofibration and
g
is a trivial fibration. By definition,
f_{i}:X_{i}\rightarrow Y_{i}
is injective and
\operatorname{Coker}(f_{i})
is projective for every
i\geq 0
. As a result, for any
i\geq 0
, the short exact sequence

0\rightarrow X_{i}\xrightarrow{f_{i}}Y_{i}\rightarrow\operatorname{Coker}(f_{i})\rightarrow 0

is split, resulting in a decomposition
Y_{i}=\operatorname{Im}(f_{i})\oplus P_{i}
for some projective left
R
-module
P_{i}\cong\operatorname{Coker}(f_{i})
. Moreover, as noted before,
g:X\rightarrow Y
is a surjective quasi-isomorphism, so there is a short exact sequence

0\rightarrow\operatorname{Ker}(g)\xrightarrow{\iota}Z\xrightarrow{g}W\rightarrow 0

of
R
-complexes in which
\operatorname{Ker}(g)
is exact. Now we construct a morphism
\phi=(\phi_{i})_{i\geq 0}:Y\rightarrow Z
of
R
-complexes inductively. Let
i=0
, and consider the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{0}}

Since
g_{0}
is surjective and
P_{0}
is projective, there is an
R
-homomorphism
\rho_{0}:P_{0}\rightarrow Z_{0}
that makes the following diagram commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{0}\mid_{P_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{0}}

If
y\in Y_{0}=\operatorname{Im}(f_{0})\oplus P_{0}
, then we can uniquely write
y=f_{0}(x)+z
for some
x\in X_{0}
and
z\in P_{0}
. Define a map
\phi_{0}:Y_{0}\rightarrow Z_{0}
by setting
\phi_{0}(y)=h_{0}(x)+\rho_{0}(z)
. Then it is clear that
\phi_{0}
is an
R
-homomorphism. Moreover, the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{0}}

Indeed, if
x\in X_{0}
, then using the definition, we have
\phi_{0}\left(f_{0}(x)\right)=h_{0}(x)
. Further, if
y=f_{0}(x)+z\in Y_{0}=\operatorname{Im}(f_{0})\oplus P_{0}
, then we have:

g_{0}\left(\phi_{0}(y)\right)=g_{0}\left(h_{0}(x)\right)+g_{0}\left(\rho_{0}(z)\right)=l_{0}\left(f_{0}(x)\right)+l_{0}(z)=l_{0}\left(f_{0}(x)+z\right)=l_{0}(y)

Now suppose that
n\geq 1
, and for any
0\leq i\leq n-1
, the
R
-homomorphism
\phi_{i}:Y_{i}\rightarrow Z_{i}
is constructed in a way that the following diagrams are commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{i}}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{i-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{i-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{i}^{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{i-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{i}^{Z}}

That is,
\phi_{i}f_{i}=h_{i}
,
g_{i}\phi_{i}=l_{i}
, and
\partial_{i}^{Z}\phi_{i}=\phi_{i-1}\partial_{i}^{Y}
for every
0\leq i\leq n-1
. By the same argument as in the case
i=0
, we can obtain an
R
-homomorphism
\rho_{n}:P_{n}\rightarrow Z_{n}
that makes the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{n}\mid_{P_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{n}}

commutative, and we can construct an
R
-homomorphism
\psi_{n}:Y_{n}\rightarrow Z_{n}
, given by
\psi_{n}(y)=h_{n}(x)+\rho_{n}(z)
for every
y=f_{n}(x)+z\in Y_{n}=\operatorname{Im}(f_{n})\oplus P_{n}
, that makes the following diagram commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{n}}
