{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{n}}

That is,
\psi_{n}f_{n}=h_{n}
and
g_{n}\psi_{n}=l_{n}
. However, the following diagram need not be commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{n}^{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{n}^{Z}}

To fix this, we set
\zeta_{n}=\partial_{n}^{Z}\psi_{n}-\phi_{n-1}\partial_{n}^{Y}:Y_{n}\rightarrow Z_{n-1}
. Using the fact that
l:Y\rightarrow W
and
g:Z\rightarrow W
are morphisms of
R
-complexes, we have for every
z\in P_{n}\subseteq Y_{n}
:

\begin{split}g_{n-1}\left(\zeta_{n}(z)\right)&=g_{n-1}\left(\partial_{n}^{Z}\left(\psi_{n}(z)\right)\right)-g_{n-1}\left(\phi_{n-1}\left(\partial_{n}^{Y}(z)\right)\right)=g_{n-1}\left(\partial_{n}^{Z}\left(\rho_{n}(z)\right)\right)-l_{n-1}\left(\partial_{n}^{Y}(z)\right)\\
&=\partial_{n}^{W}\left(g_{n}\left(\rho_{n}(z)\right)\right)-\partial_{n}^{W}\left(l_{n}(z)\right)=\partial_{n}^{W}\left(l_{n}(z)\right)-\partial_{n}^{W}\left(l_{n}(z)\right)=0\end{split}

That is,
\zeta_{n}(z)\in\operatorname{Ker}(g_{n-1})
. Therefore, we have for every
z\in P_{n}\subseteq Y_{n}
:

\begin{split}\partial_{n-1}^{\operatorname{Ker}(g)}\left(\zeta_{n}(z)\right)&=\partial_{n-1}^{Z}\left(\zeta_{n}(z)\right)=\partial_{n-1}^{Z}\left(\partial_{n}^{Z}\left(\psi_{n}(z)\right)\right)-\partial_{n-1}^{Z}\left(\phi_{n-1}\left(\partial_{n}^{Y}(z)\right)\right)\\
&=\partial_{n-1}^{Z}\left(\partial_{n}^{Z}\left(\psi_{n}(z)\right)\right)-\phi_{n-2}\left(\partial_{n-1}^{Y}\left(\partial_{n}^{Y}(z)\right)\right)=0\end{split}

That is,
\zeta_{n}(z)\in Z_{n-1}\left(\operatorname{Ker}(g)\right)
. But
\operatorname{Ker}(g)
is exact, so
\zeta_{n}(z)\in B_{n-1}\left(\operatorname{Ker}(g)\right)
. Therefore,
\zeta_{n}
induces an
R
-homomorphism
\overline{\zeta_{n}\mid_{P_{n}}}:P_{n}\rightarrow B_{n-1}\left(\operatorname{Ker}(g)\right)
. By the projectivity of
P_{n}
, there is an
R
-homomorphism
\mu_{n}:P_{n}\rightarrow\operatorname{Ker}(g_{n})
that makes the following diagram commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ker}(g_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{n-1}\left(\operatorname{Ker}(g)\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\zeta_{n}\mid_{P_{n}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\partial_{n}^{\operatorname{Ker}(g)}}}

Let
\pi_{n}:Y_{n}=\operatorname{Im}(f_{n})\oplus P_{n}\rightarrow P_{n}
be the canonical projection. Also, let
\chi_{n}:Y_{n}\rightarrow Z_{n}
be the following composition:

Y_{n}\xrightarrow{\pi_{n}}P_{n}\xrightarrow{\mu_{n}}\operatorname{Ker}(g_{n})\xrightarrow{\iota_{n}}Z_{n}

Set
\phi_{n}=\psi_{n}-\chi_{n}:Y_{n}\rightarrow Z_{n}
. Then the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{n}}

Indeed, we have for every
x\in X_{n}
:

\phi_{n}\left(f_{n}(x)\right)=\psi_{n}\left(f_{n}(x)\right)-\chi_{n}\left(f_{n}(x)\right)=h_{n}(x)-\iota_{n}\left(\mu_{n}\left(\pi_{n}\left(f_{n}(x)\right)\right)\right)=h_{n}(x)

Also, we have for every
y=f_{n}(x)+z\in Y_{n}=Im(f_{n})\oplus P_{n}
:

g_{n}\left(\phi_{n}(y)\right)=g_{n}\left(\psi_{n}(y)\right)-g_{n}\left(\chi_{n}(y)\right)=l_{n}(y)-g_{n}\left(\iota_{n}\left(\mu_{n}\left(\pi_{n}(y)\right)\right)\right)=l_{n}(y)-g_{n}\left(\mu_{n}(z)\right)=l_{n}(y)

In addition, the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{n}^{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{n}^{Z}}

Indeed, noting that
f:X\rightarrow Y
and
h:X\rightarrow Z
are morphisms of
R
-complexes, we have for every
y=f_{n}(x)+z\in Y_{n}=\operatorname{Im}(f_{n})\oplus P_{n}
:

\begin{split}\partial_{n}^{Z}\left(\phi_{n}(y)\right)&=\partial_{n}^{Z}\left(\psi_{n}(y)\right)-\partial_{n}^{Z}\left(\chi_{n}(y)\right)\\
&=\partial_{n}^{Z}\left(\psi_{n}(y)\right)-\partial_{n}^{Z}\left(\iota_{n}\left(\mu_{n}\left(\pi_{n}(y)\right)\right)\right)\\
&=\partial_{n}^{Z}\left(\psi_{n}(y)\right)-\partial_{n}^{Z}\left(\mu_{n}(z)\right)\\
&=\partial_{n}^{Z}\left(\psi_{n}(y)\right)-\partial_{n}^{\operatorname{Ker}(g)}\left(\mu_{n}(z)\right)\\
&=\partial_{n}^{Z}\left(\psi_{n}(y)\right)-\zeta_{n}(z)\\
&=\partial_{n}^{Z}\left(\psi_{n}(y)\right)-\partial_{n}^{Z}\left(\psi_{n}(z)\right)+\phi_{n-1}\left(\partial_{n}^{Y}(z)\right)\\
&=\partial_{n}^{Z}\left(\psi_{n}(y-z)\right)+\phi_{n-1}\left(\partial_{n}^{Y}\left(y-f_{n}(x)\right)\right)\\
&=\partial_{n}^{Z}\left(\psi_{n}\left(f_{n}(x)\right)\right)+\phi_{n-1}\left(\partial_{n}^{Y}(y)\right)-\phi_{n-1}\left(\partial_{n}^{Y}\left(f_{n}(x)\right)\right)\\
&=\partial_{n}^{Z}\left(\psi_{n}\left(f_{n}(x)\right)\right)+\phi_{n-1}\left(\partial_{n}^{Y}(y)\right)-\phi_{n-1}\left(f_{n-1}\left(\partial_{n}^{X}(x)\right)\right)\\
&=\partial_{n}^{Z}\left(h_{n}(x)\right)+\phi_{n-1}\left(\partial_{n}^{Y}(y)\right)-h_{n-1}\left(\partial_{n}^{X}(x)\right)\\
&=\partial_{n}^{Z}\left(h_{n}(x)\right)+\phi_{n-1}\left(\partial_{n}^{Y}(y)\right)-\partial_{n}^{Z}\left(h_{n}(x)\right)\\
&=\phi_{n-1}\left(\partial_{n}^{Y}(y)\right)\end{split}

As a consequence, we can continue this construction inductively and find a morphism
\phi=(\phi_{i})_{i\geq 0}:Y\rightarrow Z
of
R
-complexes that makes the following diagram commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

First Half of Factorization: Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a morphism in
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
. For any
n\geq 1
, consider the isomorphism
\psi_{n}^{Y}:\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)}\left(D(n),Y\right)\rightarrow Y_{n}
. For any
n\geq 1
and
x\in Y_{n}
, there is a
p^{n,x}\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)}\left(D(n),Y\right)
such that
\psi_{n}^{Y}\left(p^{n,x}\right)=p^{n,x}_{n}(1)=x
. Let
P=\bigoplus_{n=1}^{\infty}\bigoplus_{x\in Y_{n}}D(n)
. By the universal property of direct sum, there is a unique morphism
p:P\rightarrow Y
of
R
-complexes that makes the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{n,x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{n,x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

commutative in which
\iota^{n,x}
is the canonical injection for every
n\geq 1
and
x\in Y_{n}
. Thus if
n\geq 1
and
x\in Y_{n}
, then
p_{n}\left(\iota_{n}^{n,x}(1)\right)=p_{n}^{n,x}(1)=x
. This shows that
p_{n}
is surjective for every
n\geq 1
. In addition, there is a unique morphism
\eta:X\oplus P\rightarrow U
of
R
-complexes that makes the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\oplus P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

commutative in which
\kappa
and
\nu
are canonical injections. For any
i\geq 1
,
\eta_{i}\nu_{i}=p_{i}
is surjective, so
\eta_{i}
is surjective. This implies that
\eta
is a fibration. On the other hand, for any
i\geq 0
,
\kappa_{i}
is injective, and
\operatorname{Coker}(\kappa_{i})\cong P_{i}
is a direct sum of copies of
R
, so
\operatorname{Coker}(\kappa_{i})
is projective. That is,
\kappa
is a cofibration. Also, for any
i\geq 0
, we have

H_{i}(P)=H_{i}\left(\textstyle\bigoplus_{n=1}^{\infty}\textstyle\bigoplus_{x\in Y_{n}}D(n)\right)\cong\textstyle\bigoplus_{n=1}^{\infty}\bigoplus_{x\in Y_{n}}H_{i}\left(D(n)\right)=0,

which implies that
H_{i}(\kappa):H_{i}(X)\rightarrow H_{i}(X\oplus P)
is an isomorphism, so
\kappa
is a quasi-isomorphism. Therefore,
\kappa
is a trivial cofibration. Now
f=\eta\kappa
is a factorization of
f
in which
\eta
is a fibration and
\kappa
is a trivial cofibration. We further show that
\kappa
has the left lifting property against all fibrations. To this end, let
t:U\rightarrow V
be a fibration in
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
. Consider a commutative diagram as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\oplus P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

For any
n\geq 1
and
x\in Y_{n}
, let
u^{n,x}:D(n)\rightarrow V
be the following composition:

D(n)\xrightarrow{\iota^{n,x}}P\xrightarrow{\nu}X\oplus P\xrightarrow{s}V

As we observed above,
\operatorname{RLP}(\mathcal{Y})
is the class of fibrations, so
t\in\operatorname{RLP}(\mathcal{Y})
. It follows that for any
n\geq 1
and
x\in Y_{n}
, there is a morphism
q^{n,x}:D(n)\rightarrow U
that makes the following diagram commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u^{n,x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{n,x}}

By the universal property of direct sum, there is a unique morphism
q:P\rightarrow U
of
R
-complexes that makes the following diagram commutative for every
n\geq 1
and
x\in Y_{n}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{n,x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{n,x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

In addition, there is a unique morphism
\theta:X\oplus P\rightarrow U
of
R
-complexes that makes the following diagram commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\oplus P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

Then the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\oplus P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}

Clearly,
\theta\kappa=r
. Also,
tq\iota^{n,x}=tq^{n,x}=u^{n,x}=s\nu\iota^{n,x}
for every
n\geq 1
and
x\in Y_{n}
, implying that
tq=s\nu
. Further,
t\theta\kappa=tr=s\kappa
and
t\theta\nu=tq=s\nu
, showing that
t\theta=s
. As a consequence,
\kappa
has the left lifting property against all fibrations.

Second Half of Lifting: Consider a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}

in
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
in which
f
is a trivial cofibration and
g
is a fibration. Consider the factorization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\oplus P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

of
f
that was established above in which
P=\bigoplus_{n=1}^{\infty}\bigoplus_{x\in Y_{n}}D(n)
,
\eta
is a fibration, and
\kappa
is a trivial cofibration that further has the left lifting property against all fibrations. Since
f
and
\kappa
are quasi-isomorphisms, we deduce that
\eta
is also a quasi-isomorphism, so
\eta
is a trivial fibration. Consider the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\oplus P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1^{Y}}

As
f
is a cofibration and
\eta
is a trivial fibration, the first half of lifting that was established above provides a morphism
r:Y\rightarrow X\oplus P
that makes the following diagram commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\oplus P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1^{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}

This yields the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\oplus P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1^{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1^{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

This shows that
f
is a retract of
\kappa
. But
\kappa
has the left lifting property against all fibrations, so the proof of
[
Hi
, Lemma 7.2.8]
that makes no use of the model category assumption, shows that
f
has the left lifting property against all fibrations. This implies that the above diagram can be completed as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}

Second Half of Factorization: Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a morphism in
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
. We construct the desired factorization inductively. Let
\rho_{0}:P_{0}\rightarrow Y_{0}
be an
R
-epimorphism in which
P_{0}
is a projective left
R
-module, and set
Q_{0}=X_{0}\oplus P_{0}
. By the universal property of direct sum, there is a unique morphism
\eta_{0}:Q_{0}\rightarrow Y_{0}
of
R
-complexes that makes the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{0}}

commutative in which
\kappa_{0}
and
\nu_{0}
are canonical injections. Then
f_{0}=\eta_{0}\kappa_{0}
and
\rho_{0}=\eta_{0}\nu_{0}
. It is clear that
\kappa_{0}
is injective whose
\operatorname{Coker}(\kappa_{0})\cong P_{0}
is projective. On the other hand, since
\rho_{0}
is surjective, we infer that
\eta_{0}
is surjective. Now suppose that
n\geq 1
, and for any
0\leq i\leq n-1
, the
R
-module
Q_{i}
and the
R
-homomorphisms
\partial_{i}^{Q}:Q_{i}\rightarrow Q_{i-1}
,
\kappa_{i}:X_{i}\rightarrow Q_{i}
, and
\eta_{i}:Q_{i}\rightarrow Y_{i}
are constructed in a way that
\partial_{i-1}^{Q}\partial_{i}^{Q}=0
,
f_{i}=\eta_{i}\kappa_{i}
,
\kappa_{i}
is injective whose
\operatorname{Coker}(\kappa_{i})
is projective,
\eta_{i}
is surjective with
H_{i-1}(\eta)
an isomorphism, and the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{i-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{i-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{i-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{i}^{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{i-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{i}^{Q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{i-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{i}^{Y}}

Using the above diagram, we see that if
x\in X_{n}
, then
\partial_{n-1}^{Q}\left(\kappa_{n-1}\left(\partial_{n}^{X}(x)\right)\right)=\kappa_{n-2}\left(\partial_{n-1}^{X}\left(\partial_{n}^{X}(x)\right)\right)=0
, so
\kappa_{n-1}\left(\partial_{n}^{X}(x)\right)\in Z_{n-1}(Q)
. Therefore, we get an induced
R
-homomorphism
\overline{\kappa_{n-1}\partial_{n}^{X}}:X_{n}\rightarrow Z_{n-1}(Q)
. Consider the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{n-1}(Q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{n-1}(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\kappa_{n-1}\partial_{n}^{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\partial_{n}^{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{n-1}(\eta)}

Indeed, using the fact that
f
is a morphism of
R
-complexes, we have
\eta_{n-1}\left(\kappa_{n-1}\left(\partial_{n}^{X}(x)\right)\right)=f_{n-1}\left(\partial_{n}^{X}(x)\right)=\partial_{n}^{Y}\left(f_{n}(x)\right)
for every
x\in X_{n}
. Set:

L_{n}=Z_{n-1}(Q)\textstyle\mathop{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\textstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}}_{Z_{n-1}(Y)}Y_{n}=\left\{(x,y)\in Z_{n-1}(Q)\oplus Y_{n}\;|\;\eta_{n-1}(x)=\partial_{n}^{Y}(y)\right\}

Then there is a unique morphism
\varrho_{n}:X_{n}\rightarrow L_{n}
that makes the following pullback diagram commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{n-1}(Q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{n-1}(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\kappa_{n-1}\partial_{n}^{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\partial_{n}^{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{n-1}(\eta)}

As in the case
i=0
, let
\rho_{n}:P_{n}\rightarrow L_{n}
be an
R
-epimorphism in which
P_{n}
is a projective left
R
-module, and set
Q_{n}=X_{n}\oplus P_{n}
. By the universal property of direct sum, there is a unique morphism
\theta_{n}:Q_{n}\rightarrow L_{n}
of
R
-complexes that makes the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{n}}

commutative in which
\kappa_{n}
and
\nu_{n}
are canonical injections. Set
\eta_{n}=\zeta_{n}\theta_{n}:Q_{n}\rightarrow Y_{n}
. Then
f_{n}=\zeta_{n}\varrho_{n}=\zeta_{n}\theta_{n}\kappa_{n}=\eta_{n}\kappa_{n}
. It is also clear that
\kappa_{n}
is injective whose
\operatorname{Coker}(\kappa_{n})\cong P_{n}
is projective.

Consider the following compositions of
R
-homomorphisms:

X_{n}\xrightarrow{\partial_{n}^{X}}X_{n-1}\xrightarrow{\kappa_{n-1}}Q_{n-1}\quad\text{and}\quad P_{n}\xrightarrow{\rho_{n}}L_{n}\xrightarrow{\xi_{n}}Z_{n-1}(Q)\xrightarrow{\iota_{n-1}^{Q}}Q_{n-1}

By the universal property of direct sum, there is a unique morphism
\partial_{n}^{Q}:Q_{n}\rightarrow Q_{n-1}
of
R
-complexes that makes the following diagram commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{n-1}\partial_{n}^{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{n}^{Q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{n-1}^{Q}\xi_{n}\rho_{n}}

Then we have
\partial_{n-1}^{Q}\partial_{n}^{Q}\kappa_{n}=\partial_{n-1}^{Q}\kappa_{n-1}\partial_{n}^{X}=\kappa_{n-2}\partial_{n-1}^{X}\partial_{n}^{X}=0
and
\partial_{n-1}^{Q}\partial_{n}^{Q}\nu_{n}=\partial_{n-1}^{Q}\iota_{n-1}^{Q}\xi_{n}\rho_{n}=0
as
\operatorname{Im}(\xi_{n})\subseteq Z_{n-1}(Q)
. It follows that
\partial_{n-1}^{Q}\partial_{n}^{Q}=0
.

We next show that the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{n}^{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{n}^{Q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{n}^{Y}}

Indeed, we have
\partial_{n}^{Q}\kappa_{n}=\kappa_{n-1}\partial_{n}^{X}
from the previous commutative diagram. Moreover, we have:

\partial_{n}^{Y}\eta_{n}\kappa_{n}=\partial_{n}^{Y}\zeta_{n}\theta_{n}\kappa_{n}=\partial_{n}^{Y}\zeta_{n}\varrho_{n}=\partial_{n}^{Y}f_{n}=f_{n-1}\partial_{n}^{X}=\eta_{n-1}\kappa_{n-1}\partial_{n}^{X}=\eta_{n-1}\partial_{n}^{Q}\kappa_{n}

and

\partial_{n}^{Y}\eta_{n}\nu_{n}=\partial_{n}^{Y}\zeta_{n}\theta_{n}\nu_{n}=\partial_{n}^{Y}\zeta_{n}\rho_{n}=\iota_{n-1}^{Y}\overline{\partial_{n}^{Y}}\zeta_{n}\rho_{n}=\iota_{n-1}^{Y}Z_{n-1}(\eta)\xi_{n}\rho_{n}=\eta_{n-1}\iota_{n-1}^{Q}\xi_{n}\rho_{n}=\eta_{n-1}\partial_{n}^{Q}\nu_{n}

It follows that
\partial_{n}^{Y}\eta_{n}=\eta_{n-1}\partial_{n}^{Q}
.

We now show that
H_{n-1}(\eta)
is injective. Suppose that
H_{n-1}(\eta)\left(x+B_{n-1}(Q)\right)=\eta_{n-1}(x)+B_{n-1}(Y)=0
for some
x\in Z_{n-1}(Q)
. Then
\eta_{n-1}(x)\in B_{n-1}(Y)
, so
\eta_{n-1}(x)=\partial_{n}^{Y}(y)
for some
y\in Y_{n}
. This implies that
(x,y)\in L_{n}
. On the other hand,
\rho_{n}=\theta_{n}\nu_{n}
and
\rho_{n}
is surjective, so
\theta_{n}:Q_{n}\rightarrow L_{n}
is surjective. Therefore, there is an element
(z,w)\in Q_{n}=X_{n}\oplus P_{n}
such that
\theta_{n}(z,w)=(x,y)
. Then we have:

\begin{split}x&=\xi_{n}(x,y)=\xi_{n}\left(\theta_{n}(z,w)\right)=\xi_{n}\left(\varrho_{n}(z)\right)+\xi_{n}\left(\rho_{n}(w)\right)=\kappa_{n-1}\left(\partial_{n}^{X}(z)\right)+\partial_{n}^{Q}\left(\nu_{n}(w)\right)\\
&=\partial_{n}^{Q}\left(\kappa_{n}(z)\right)+\partial_{n}^{Q}\left(\nu_{n}(w)\right)=\partial_{n}^{Q}\left(\kappa_{n}(z)+\nu_{n}(w)\right)=\partial_{n}^{Q}(z,w)\end{split}

Hence
x\in B_{n-1}(Q)
, so
x+B_{n-1}(Q)=0
. This shows that
H_{n-1}(\eta)
is injective.

We next show that
H_{n-1}(\eta)
is surjective. Let
y\in Z_{n}(Y)
. Then
\eta_{n-1}(0)=0=\partial_{n}^{Y}(y)
, so
(0,y)\in L_{n}
. By the surjectivity of
\theta_{n}:Q_{n}\rightarrow L_{n}
, there is an element
(z,w)\in Q_{n}=X_{n}\oplus P_{n}
such that
\theta_{n}(z,w)=(0,y)
. Then
y=\zeta_{n}(0,y)=\zeta_{n}\left(\theta_{n}(z,w)\right)=\eta_{n}(z,w)
. Also, a similar calculation as in the previous paragraph shows that
\partial_{n}^{Q}(z,w)=0
, so
(z,w)\in Z_{n}(Q)
. This shows that
Z_{n}(\eta):Z_{n}(Q)\rightarrow Z_{n}(Y)
is surjective. Since we have constructed all these
R
-homomorphisms in the same way in each step, we can infer that
Z_{n-1}(\eta)
is surjective. It follows that
H_{n-1}(\eta)
is surjective. Therefore,
H_{n-1}(\eta)
is an isomorphism.

We finally show that
\eta_{n}
is surjective. Let
y\in Y_{n}
. Then
\partial_{n}^{Y}(y)\in B_{n-1}(Y)\subseteq Z_{n-1}(Y)
, so by the surjectivity of
Z_{n-1}(\eta)
, there is an element
x\in Z_{n-1}(Q)
such that
\eta_{n-1}(x)=\partial_{n}^{Y}(y)
. Then
(x,y)\in L_{n}
, so by the surjectivity of
\theta_{n}
, there is an element
(z,w)\in Q_{n}=X_{n}\oplus P_{n}
such that
\theta_{n}(z,w)=(x,y)
. It follows that
y=\zeta_{n}(x,y)=\zeta_{n}\left(\theta_{n}(z,w)\right)=\eta_{n}(z,w)
. Thus
\eta_{n}
is surjective.

As a consequence, we can inductively construct the morphisms
\kappa:X\rightarrow Q
and
\eta:Q\rightarrow Y
of
R
-complexes such that
f=\eta\kappa
,
\eta
is a trivial fibration, and
\kappa
is a cofibration.

All in all, we have a cofibrantly generated model structure on
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
.
∎

4.
Simplicial Objects and Dold-Kan Correspondence Theorem

Simplicial objects are nowadays widely used in modern mathematics. Simplicial sets in particular are combinatorial models for topological spaces. They also serve as the bedrock of the language of
\infty
-categories. Our central focus of attention is on simplicial modules and simplicial commutative algebras which can be thought of as generalizations as well as non-abelian analogues of chain complexes. In this section, we recall the basic definitions and examples of simplicial objects. Moreover, we fix our notations on the normalization and Dld-Kan functors. Deploying the Dold-Kan Correspondence, we transfer the model structure on connective chain complexes to simplicial modules.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category. A
simplicial object
over
\mathcal{C}
is a family
A=\{A_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
of objects of
\mathcal{C}
together with
face morphisms

d_{n,i}^{A}:A_{n}\rightarrow A_{n-1}
for every
n\geq 1
and
0\leq i\leq n
, and
degeneracy morphisms

s_{n,i}^{A}:A_{n}\rightarrow A_{n+1}
for every
n\geq 0
and
0\leq i\leq n
, that satisfy the following relations for every
n\geq 0
and
0\leq i,j\leq n
:

(i)

d_{n,i}^{A}d_{n+1,j}^{A}=d_{n,j-1}^{A}d_{n+1,i}^{A}
if
i<j
.

(ii)

s_{n,i}^{A}s_{n-1,j}^{A}=s_{n,j+1}^{A}s_{n-1,i}^{A}
if
i\leq j
.

(iii)

d_{n,i}^{A}s_{n-1,j}^{A}=\begin{cases}s_{n-2,j-1}^{A}d_{n-1,i}^{A}&\text{if }i<j\\
1^{A_{n-1}}&\text{if }i=j\text{ or }j+1\\
s_{n-2,j}^{A}d_{n-1,i-1}^{A}&\text{if }i>j+1\end{cases}

Given two simplicial objects
A=\{A_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
and
B=\{B_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
over
\mathcal{C}
, a
morphism

\tau:A\rightarrow B

of simplicial objects
is a collection
\tau=(\tau_{n})_{n\geq 0}
of morphisms
\tau_{n}:A_{n}\rightarrow B_{n}
of
\mathcal{C}
for every
n\geq 0
, such that the following diagram is commutative for every
n\geq 1
and
0\leq i\leq n
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{n-1,i}^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{n,i}^{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{n-1,i}^{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{n,i}^{B}}

We speak of simplicial sets, simplicial modules, or simplicial commutative algebras when we consider simplicial objects over the categories of sets, modules, or commutative algebras.

Remark 4.2
.

It is immediate from Definition
4.1
that we have a category
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{C}
of simplicial objects over
\mathcal{C}
. As a matter of fact,
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{C}
is a functor category. Let
\Delta
denote the
simplex category
described by

\operatorname{Obj}(\Delta)=\left\{[n]=\{0,1,2,...,n\}\;|\;n\geq 0\right\}

and

\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[m]\right)=\left\{f\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}\left([n],[m]\right)\;|\;f\textrm{ is order-preserving}\right\}

for every
[n],[m]\in\operatorname{Obj}(\Delta)
. Then it is folklore that
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{\operatorname{op}}}
. In other words, a simplicial object over
\mathcal{C}
is nothing but a contravariant functor
\Delta\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
. Accordingly, we sometimes use the functorial point of view when defining a simplicial object.

For any
n\geq 0
and
0\leq i\leq n
, the
face map

\delta_{n,i}:[n-1]\rightarrow[n]
is the unique injective map that ”misses”
i
, i.e.

\delta_{n,i}(k)=\begin{cases}k&\textrm{if }k<i\\
k+1&\textrm{if }k\geq i\end{cases}

for every
0\leq k\leq n-1
, and the
degeneracy map

\sigma_{n,i}:[n+1]\rightarrow[n]
is the unique surjective map that ”hits”
i
twice, i.e.

\sigma_{n,i}(k)=\begin{cases}k&\textrm{if }k\leq i\\
k-1&\textrm{if }k>i\end{cases}

for every
0\leq k\leq n+1
. It is clear that the face and the degeneracy maps are morphisms in the simplex category. Moreover,
\delta_{n,n}
is the inclusion map for every
n\geq 0
. One can show by brute force that the following face-degeneracy relations hold for every
0\leq i,j\leq n
:

(i)

\delta_{n+1,j}\delta_{n,i}=\delta_{n+1,i}\delta_{n,j-1}
if
i<j
.

(ii)

\sigma_{n-1,j}\sigma_{n,i}=\sigma_{n-1,i}\sigma_{n,j+1}
if
i\leq j
.

(iii)

\sigma_{n-1,j}\delta_{n,i}=\begin{cases}\delta_{n-1,i}\sigma_{n-2,j-1}&\text{if }i<j\\
1^{[n-1]}&\text{if }i=j\text{ or }j+1\\
\delta_{n-1,i-1}\sigma_{n-2,j}&\text{if }i>j+1.\end{cases}

Suppose that
n,m\geq 0
and
f\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[m]\right)
. If
f
is bijective, then
n=m
and
f=1^{[n]}
. Now assume that
f
is not bijective. If
f
is injective, then there is a unique factorization
f=\delta_{m,i_{1}}\cdots\delta_{n+1,i_{m-n}}
where
0\leq i_{m-n}<\cdots<i_{1}\leq m
are the elements of
[m]
that are not in the image of
f
. If
f
is surjective, then there is a unique factorization
f=\sigma_{m,j_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{n-1,j_{n-m}}
where
0\leq j_{1}<\cdots<j_{n-m}<n
are the elements of
[n]
for which
f(j)=f(j+1)
. In general, there is a unique factorization
f=gh
where
g\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([k],[m]\right)
is injective and
h\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[k]\right)
is surjective; See
[
We
, Lemma 8.1.2]
.

A trivial example of a simplicial object is the constant one in which we have the same object in each degree and the face and the degeneracy morphisms are all identity morphisms. We next look at some non-trivial examples.

Example 4.3
.

Fix
n\geq 0
. We have the contravariant functor
\Delta^{n}=\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left(-,[n]\right):\Delta\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}
, so
\Delta^{n}
is a simplicial set called the
combinatorial

n
-
simplex
. If
m\geq 0
and
f\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[m]\right)
, then
\Delta^{f}=\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}(-,f):\Delta^{n}=\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left(-,[n]\right)\rightarrow\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left(-,[m]\right)=\Delta^{m}
is a natural transformation of functors, i.e. a morphism of simplicial sets. For any
m\geq 0
, considering the images of order-preserving maps
[m]\rightarrow[n]
, we can make the following identification:

\Delta_{m}^{n}=\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([m],[n]\right)\cong\left\{(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{m})\in\mathbb{N}^{m+1}\;|\;0\leq\alpha_{0}\leq\alpha_{1}\leq\cdots\leq\alpha_{m}\leq n\right\}

The above identification shows that
|\Delta_{m}^{n}|=\binom{n+m+1}{n}
. On the other hand, under this identification, for any
m\geq 0
and
0\leq i\leq m
, the face morphism
d_{m,i}^{\Delta^{n}}:\Delta_{m}^{n}\rightarrow\Delta_{m-1}^{n}
is given by
d_{m,i}^{\Delta^{n}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{m})=(\alpha_{0},...,\alpha_{i-1},\alpha_{i+1},...,\alpha_{m})
, and the degeneracy morphism
s_{m,i}^{\Delta^{n}}:\Delta_{m}^{n}\rightarrow\Delta_{m+1}^{n}
is given by
s_{m,i}^{\Delta^{n}}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{m})=(\alpha_{0},...,\alpha_{i-1},\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i+1},...,\alpha_{m})
. Given
m\geq 0
, any element of
\Delta_{m}^{n}
is called an
m
-
cell
. The
m
-cells that have repeated coordinates, i.e. the ones that belong to the images of degeneracy morphisms, are called
degenerate
, and the rest are called
non-degenerate
.

We further have the contravariant functor
\partial\Delta^{n}:\Delta\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}
, given by

\partial\Delta^{n}_{m}=\left\{f\in\Delta_{m}^{n}=\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([m],[n]\right)\;|\;f\textrm{ is not surjective}\right\}

for every
m\geq 0
, and
(\partial\Delta^{n})(\eta):=\Delta^{n}(\eta)=\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left(\eta,[n]\right)
for every
\eta\in\operatorname{Mor}(\Delta)
. Thus
\partial\Delta^{n}
is a simplicial set called the
boundary
of
\Delta^{n}
. If
\iota_{m}:\partial\Delta_{m}^{n}\rightarrow\Delta_{m}^{n}
is the inclusion map for every
m\geq 0
, then
\iota=(\iota_{m})_{m\geq 0}:\partial\Delta^{n}\rightarrow\Delta^{n}
is a morphism of simplicial sets. Under the above identification, we have for every
m\geq 0
:

\partial\Delta_{m}^{n}\cong\left\{(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{m})\in\Delta_{m}^{n}\;|\;\{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{m}\}\subset[n]\right\}

For any
m\geq 0
, we notice that
\partial\Delta_{m}^{1}=\{(\underbrace{0,...,0}_{m+1\textrm{ times}}),(\underbrace{1,...,1}_{m+1\textrm{ times}})\}
, so in light of
\Delta_{m}^{0}=\{(\underbrace{0,...,0}_{m+1\textrm{ times}})\}
, we see that there is an isomorphism
\partial\Delta^{1}\cong\Delta^{0}\sqcup\Delta^{0}
of simplicial sets.

We next recall some basic constructions of simplicial objects which will be needed later.

Remark 4.4
.

If
R
is a ring,
M
is a simplicial right
R
-module, and
N
is a simplicial left
R
-module, then the
simplicial tensor product

M\otimes_{R}N
is defined as follows. If
n\geq 0
, then
(M\otimes_{R}N)_{n}=M_{n}\otimes_{R}N_{n}
. If
\eta\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[m]\right)
, then
(M\otimes_{R}N)(\eta)=M(\eta)\otimes_{R}N(\eta):M_{m}\otimes_{R}N_{m}\rightarrow M_{n}\otimes_{R}N_{n}
. This construction is functorial in each argument.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a locally small bicomplete category,
A
is a simplicial object over
\mathcal{C}
, and
U
is a simplicial set, then the
copower simplicial object

A^{(U)}
is defined as follows. If
n\geq 0
, then
(A^{(U)})_{n}=A_{n}^{(U_{n})}
. If
\eta\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[m]\right)
, then we consider the morphism
A(\eta):A_{m}\rightarrow A_{n}
in
\mathcal{C}
as well as the map
U(\eta):U_{m}\rightarrow U_{n}
, and then define
A^{(U)}(\eta):A_{m}^{(U_{m})}\rightarrow A_{n}^{(U_{n})}
to be the diagonal of the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{m}^{(U_{m})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{n}^{(U_{m})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{m}^{(U_{n})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{n}^{(U_{n})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(\eta)^{(U_{m})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{U(\eta)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{U(\eta)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(\eta)^{(U_{n})}}

This construction is functorial in each argument. Moreover, if
R
is a ring,
M
is a simplicial left
R
-module, and
U
is a simplicial set, then there is a natural isomorphism
M^{(U)}\cong R^{(U)}\otimes_{R}M
of simplicial left
R
-modules.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a locally small bicomplete category, and
A
and
B
are two simplicial objects over
\mathcal{C}
, then the
mapping simplicial set

\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{C}}(A,B)
is defined as follows. If
n\geq 0
, then
\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{C}}(A,B)_{n}=\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{C}}\left(A^{(\Delta^{n})},B\right)
. If
\eta\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[m]\right)
and
f\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{C}}\left(A^{(\Delta^{m})},B\right)
, then we consider the morphisms

A^{(\Delta^{n})}\xrightarrow{A^{(\Delta^{\eta})}}A^{(\Delta^{m})}\xrightarrow{f}B

and define the map
\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{C}}(A,B)(\eta):\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{C}}\left(A^{(\Delta^{m})},B\right)\rightarrow\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{C}}\left(A^{(\Delta^{n})},B\right)
by setting
\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{C}}(A,B)(\eta)(f)=fA^{(\Delta^{\eta})}
. This construction is functorial in each argument. Moreover, if
R
is a ring,
M
and
N
are two simplicial left
R
-modules, and
U
is a simplicial set, then there is a natural bijection of sets as follows:

\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)}\left(M^{(U)},N\right)\cong\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)}\left(M,\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,N)\right)

Let
U
be a simplicial set. If
R
is a ring and
M
is a simplicial left
R
-module, then we can forget the module structure of
M
, consider it as a simplicial set, and then form
\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,M)
. Then
\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,M)
is a simplicial set which becomes a simplicial left
R
-module with the module structure inherited from
M
by pointwise operations. Similarly, if
R
is a commutative ring and
A
is a simplicial commutative
R
-algebra, then we can forget the algebra structure of
A
, consider it as a simplicial set, and then form
\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,A)
. Then
\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,A)
is a simplicial set which becomes a simplicial commutative
R
-algebra with the algebra structure inherited from
A
by pointwise operations.

We now recall the normalization functor; see
[
We
, Definition 8.3.6]
.

Definition 4.5
.

Let
R
be a ring. The
normalization functor

\operatorname{Nor}:\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
is defined as follows:

(i)

\operatorname{Nor}
assigns to each simplicial left
R
-module
M
, the normalized
R
-complex
\operatorname{Nor}(M)
, whose terms are
\operatorname{Nor}(M)_{0}=M_{0}
and
\operatorname{Nor}(M)_{n}=\bigcap_{i=0}^{n-1}\operatorname{Ker}\left(d_{n,i}^{M}\right)\subseteq M_{n}
for every
n\geq 1
, and whose differential
\partial_{n}^{\operatorname{Nor}(M)}:\operatorname{Nor}(M)_{n}\rightarrow\operatorname{Nor}(M)_{n-1}
is given by
\partial_{n}^{\operatorname{Nor}(M)}(x)=(-1)^{n}d_{n,n}^{M}(x)
for every
n\geq 1
and
x\in\operatorname{Nor}(M)_{n}
.

(ii)

\operatorname{Nor}
assigns to each morphism
f:M\rightarrow N
of simplicial left
R
-modules, the morphism
\operatorname{Nor}(f):\operatorname{Nor}(M)\rightarrow\operatorname{Nor}(N)
of
R
-complexes, where
\operatorname{Nor}(f)_{n}:\operatorname{Nor}(M)_{n}\rightarrow\operatorname{Nor}(N)_{n}
is given by
\operatorname{Nor}(f)_{n}(x)=f_{n}(x)
for every
n\geq 0
and
x\in\operatorname{Nor}(M)_{n}
.

Remark 4.6
.

Let
R
be a ring, and
M
a simplicial left
R
-module. The
Moore complex

C(M)
is defined as follows. If
n\geq 0
, then
C(M)_{n}=M_{n}
. If
n\geq 1
, then
\partial_{n}^{C(M)}=\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}d_{n,i}^{M}:C(M)_{n}\rightarrow C(M)_{n-1}
. It can be verified by inspection that
C(M)
is a connective
R
-complex. Moreover, if we set
D(M)_{0}=0
and
D(M)_{n}=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\operatorname{Im}\left(s_{n-1,i}^{M}\right)
for every
n\geq 1
, and also
\partial_{n}^{D(M)}(x)=\partial_{n}^{C(M)}(x)
for every
n\geq 1
and
x\in D(M)_{n}
, then
D(M)
turns out to be a subcomplex of
C(M)
. Moreover, we have
C(M)=\operatorname{Nor}(M)\oplus D(M)
and
\operatorname{Nor}(M)\cong C(M)/D(M)
naturally in
M
. See
[
We
, Lemma 8.3.7]
.

We recall a quintessential example of simplicial sets.

Example 4.7
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a small category. The
nerve
of
\mathcal{C}
is a simplicial set defined as follows. If
[n]\in\operatorname{Obj}(\Delta)
, then viewing the poset
[n]
as a small category, we set
\operatorname{nrv}_{n}(\mathcal{C})=\operatorname{Fun}\left([n],\mathcal{C}\right)
, i.e. the set of covariant functors
[n]\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
. If
\eta\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[m]\right)
, then the map
\operatorname{nrv}(\mathcal{C})(\eta):\operatorname{Fun}\left([m],\mathcal{C}\right)\rightarrow\operatorname{Fun}\left([n],\mathcal{C}\right)
is defined by
\operatorname{nrv}(\mathcal{C})(\eta)(\theta)=\theta\eta
for every
\theta\in\operatorname{Fun}\left([m],\mathcal{C}\right)
. Then
\operatorname{nrv}(\mathcal{C}):\Delta\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}
is a contravariant functor, so it is a simplicial set. More specifically, we have
\operatorname{nrv}_{0}(\mathcal{C})=\operatorname{Obj}(\mathcal{C})
, and for any
n\geq 1
,
\operatorname{nrv}_{n}(\mathcal{C})
is the set of
n
-tuples of composable morphisms

A_{0}\rightarrow A_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow A_{n-1}\rightarrow A_{n}

in
\mathcal{C}
. Moreover, the face morphism
d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{nrv}(\mathcal{C})}:\operatorname{nrv}_{n}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\operatorname{nrv}_{n-1}(\mathcal{C})
is given by

d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{nrv}(\mathcal{C})}\left(A_{0}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow A_{i-1}\xrightarrow{f_{i-1}}A_{i}\xrightarrow{f_{i}}A_{i+1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow A_{n}\right)=\left(A_{0}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow A_{i-1}\xrightarrow{f_{i}f_{i-1}}A_{i+1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow A_{n}\right)

and the degeneracy morphism
s_{n,i}^{\operatorname{nrv}(\mathcal{C})}:\operatorname{nrv}_{n}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\operatorname{nrv}_{n+1}(\mathcal{C})
is given by

s_{n,i}^{\operatorname{nrv}(\mathcal{C})}\left(A_{0}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow A_{i-1}\rightarrow A_{i}\rightarrow A_{i+1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow A_{n}\right)=\left(A_{0}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow A_{i-1}\rightarrow A_{i}\xrightarrow{1^{A_{i}}}A_{i}\rightarrow A_{i+1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow A_{n}\right)

for every
0\leq i\leq n
.

We need the next lemma in the sequel.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
R
be a ring, and
\mathcal{P}
a poset with a least element
e
. View
\mathcal{P}
as a small category, and let
\zeta:\Delta^{0}\rightarrow\operatorname{nrv}(\mathcal{P})
be the morphism of simplicial sets given by
\zeta_{m}(0,...,0)=(e\leq\cdots\leq e)
for every
m\geq 0
. Then
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\zeta)}\right):\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{0})}\right)\rightarrow\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{\left(\operatorname{nrv}(\mathcal{P})\right)}\right)
is a homotopy equivalence of
R
-complexes. As a consequence, we have:

H_{i}\left(\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{\left(\operatorname{nrv}(\mathcal{P})\right)}\right)\right)\cong\begin{cases}R&\textrm{if }i=0\\
0&\textrm{if }i>0\end{cases}

Proof.

Set
U=\operatorname{nrv}(\mathcal{P})
. Since
\Delta_{m}^{0}=\{(\underbrace{0,...,0}_{m+1\textrm{ times}})\}
for every
m\geq 0
, we can define a morphism
\zeta:\Delta^{0}\rightarrow U
of simplicial sets by setting
\zeta_{m}(0,...,0)=(e\leq\cdots\leq e)
for every
m\geq 0
. We show that the induced morphism

\textstyle\overline{R^{(\zeta)}}:\frac{C\left(R^{(\Delta^{0})}\right)}{D\left(R^{(\Delta^{0})}\right)}\rightarrow\frac{C\left(R^{(U)}\right)}{D\left(R^{(U)}\right)}

is a homotopy equivalence. Since
\Delta^{0}
is a terminal object in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}
, there exists a unique morphism
\varepsilon:U\rightarrow\Delta^{0}
of simplicial sets. It is clear that
\varepsilon\zeta=1^{\Delta^{0}}
, so
R^{(\varepsilon)}R^{(\zeta)}=1^{R^{(\Delta^{0})}}
, whence

\overline{R^{(\varepsilon)}}\overline{R^{(\zeta)}}=1^{\frac{C\left(R^{(\Delta^{0})}\right)}{D\left(R^{(\Delta^{0})}\right)}}.

Now let
m\geq 0
. Then
R^{(U_{m})}
is a free left
R
-module with a basis that is in 1-1 correspondence with the set
U_{m}
. We identify this basis with elements of
U_{m}
in what follows. Define an
R
-homomorphism

t_{m}:C\left(R^{(U)}\right)_{m}=R^{(U_{m})}\rightarrow R^{(U_{m+1})}=C\left(R^{(U)}\right)_{m+1}

by setting

t_{m}(a_{0}\leq\cdots\leq a_{m})=(e\leq a_{0}\leq\cdots\leq a_{m})

for every
(a_{0}\leq\cdots\leq a_{m})\in U_{m}
, and then extending linearly. We note that
(a_{0}\leq\cdots\leq a_{m})\in\operatorname{Im}\left(s_{m-1,i}^{U}\right)
for some
0\leq i\leq m-1
if and only if
a_{i}=a_{i+1}
. This implies that if
(a_{0}\leq\cdots\leq a_{m})\in\operatorname{Im}\left(s_{m-1,i}^{U}\right)
, then
(e\leq a_{0}\leq\cdots\leq a_{m})\in\operatorname{Im}\left(s_{m,i+1}^{U}\right)
. It follows that
t_{m}\left(D\left(R^{(U)}\right)_{m}\right)\subseteq D\left(R^{(U)}\right)_{m+1}
, so
t_{m}
induces an
R
-homomorphism

\textstyle\overline{t_{m}}:\frac{C\left(R^{(U)}\right)_{m}}{D\left(R^{(U)}\right)_{m}}\rightarrow\frac{C\left(R^{(U)}\right)_{m+1}}{D\left(R^{(U)}\right)_{m+1}}.

We also set
\overline{t_{m}}=0
for every
m<0
. We show that

\textstyle 1^{\frac{C\left(R^{(U)}\right)_{m}}{D\left(R^{(U)}\right)_{m}}}-\overline{R^{(\zeta_{m})}}\overline{R^{(\varepsilon_{m})}}=\partial_{m+1}^{\frac{C\left(R^{(U)}\right)}{D\left(R^{(U)}\right)}}\overline{t_{m}}+\overline{t_{m-1}}\partial_{m}^{\frac{C\left(R^{(U)}\right)}{D\left(R^{(U)}\right)}}

every
m\geq 0
. To this end, it suffices to establish the equality for the elements of
U_{m}
. Let
(a_{0}\leq\cdots\leq a_{m})\in U_{m}
. We consider two case:

Case I:
m=0

In this case, we have:

(a_{0})-\overline{R^{(\zeta_{0})}}\left(\overline{R^{(\varepsilon_{0})}}(a_{0})\right)=(a_{0})-\overline{R^{(\zeta_{0})}}(0)=(a_{0})-(e)

On the other hand, since
\overline{t_{-1}}=0
, we have:

\begin{split}\partial_{1}^{\frac{C\left(R^{(U)}\right)}{D\left(R^{(U)}\right)}}\left(\overline{t_{0}}(a_{0})\right)+\overline{t_{-1}}\left(\partial_{0}^{\frac{C\left(R^{(U)}\right)}{D\left(R^{(U)}\right)}}(a_{0})\right)&=\partial_{1}^{\frac{C\left(R^{(U)}\right)}{D\left(R^{(U)}\right)}}\left((e\leq a_{0})+D\left(R^{(U)}\right)_{1}\right)=\partial_{1}^{C\left(R^{(U)}\right)}(e\leq a_{0})\\
&=d_{1,0}^{R^{(U)}}(e\leq a_{0})-d_{1,1}^{R^{(U)}}(e\leq a_{0})=(a_{0})-(e)\end{split}

Consequently, we see that the desired equality holds in this case.

Case II:
m>0

In this case, since
(e\leq\cdots\leq e)\in D\left(R^{(U)}\right)_{m}
, we have:

\begin{split}(a_{0}\leq\cdots\leq a_{m})-\overline{R^{(\zeta_{m})}}\left(\overline{R^{(\varepsilon_{m})}}\left((a_{0}\leq\cdots\leq a_{m})+D\left(R^{(U)}\right)_{m}\right)\right)&=(a_{0}\leq\cdots\leq a_{m})-\overline{R^{(\zeta_{m})}}\left((0,...,0)+D\left(R^{(\Delta^{0})}\right)_{m}\right)\\
&=(a_{0}\leq\cdots\leq a_{m})-(e\leq\cdots\leq e)+D\left(R^{(U)}\right)_{m}\\
&=(a_{0}\leq\cdots\leq a_{m})+D\left(R^{(U)}\right)_{m}\end{split}

On the other hand, we have:

\begin{split}\partial_{m+1}^{\frac{C\left(R^{(U)}\right)}{D\left(R^{(U)}\right)}}\left(\overline{t_{m}}\left((a_{0}\leq\cdots\leq a_{m})+D\left(R^{(U)}\right)_{m}\right)\right)&=\partial_{m+1}^{\frac{C\left(R^{(U)}\right)}{D\left(R^{(U)}\right)}}\left((e\leq a_{0}\leq\cdots\leq a_{m})+D\left(R^{(U)}\right)_{m+1}\right)\\
&=\partial_{m+1}^{C\left(R^{(U)}\right)}(e\leq a_{0}\leq\cdots\leq a_{m})+D\left(R^{(U)}\right)_{m}\\
&=\sum_{i=0}^{m+1}(-1)^{i}d_{m+1,i}^{R^{(U)}}(e\leq a_{0}\leq\cdots\leq a_{m})+D\left(R^{(U)}\right)_{m}\\
&=d_{m+1,0}^{R^{(U)}}(e\leq a_{0}\leq\cdots\leq a_{m})+\sum_{i=1}^{m+1}(-1)^{i}d_{m+1,i}^{R^{(U)}}(e\leq a_{0}\leq\cdots\leq a_{m})+D\left(R^{(U)}\right)_{m}\\
&=(a_{0}\leq\cdots\leq a_{m})+\sum_{i=1}^{m+1}(-1)^{i}(e\leq a_{0}\leq\cdots\leq a_{i-2}\leq a_{i}\leq\cdots\leq a_{m})+D\left(R^{(U)}\right)_{m}\end{split}

and

\begin{split}\overline{t_{m-1}}\left(\partial_{m}^{\frac{C\left(R^{(U)}\right)}{D\left(R^{(U)}\right)}}\left((a_{0}\leq\cdots\leq a_{m})+D\left(R^{(U)}\right)_{m}\right)\right)&=\overline{t_{m-1}}\left(\partial_{m}^{C\left(R^{(U)}\right)}(a_{0}\leq\cdots\leq a_{m})+D\left(R^{(U)}\right)_{m-1}\right)\\
&=\overline{t_{m-1}}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{m}(-1)^{i}d_{m,i}^{R^{(U)}}(a_{0}\leq\cdots\leq a_{m})+D\left(R^{(U)}\right)_{m-1}\right)\\
&=\overline{t_{m-1}}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{m}(-1)^{i}(a_{0}\leq\cdots\leq a_{i-1}\leq a_{i+1}\leq\cdots\leq a_{m})+D\left(R^{(U)}\right)_{m-1}\right)\\
&=\sum_{i=0}^{m}(-1)^{i}(e\leq a_{0}\leq\cdots\leq a_{i-1}\leq a_{i+1}\leq\cdots\leq a_{m})+D\left(R^{(U)}\right)_{m}\\
&=-\sum_{i=1}^{m+1}(-1)^{i}(e\leq a_{0}\leq\cdots\leq a_{i-2}\leq a_{i}\leq\cdots\leq a_{m})+D\left(R^{(U)}\right)_{m}\end{split}

Adding them up, we get:

\partial_{m+1}^{\frac{C\left(R^{(U)}\right)}{D\left(R^{(U)}\right)}}\left(\overline{t_{m}}\left((a_{0}\leq\cdots\leq a_{m})+D\left(R^{(U)}\right)_{m}\right)\right)+\overline{t_{m-1}}\left(\partial_{m}^{\frac{C\left(R^{(U)}\right)}{D\left(R^{(U)}\right)}}\left((a_{0}\leq\cdots\leq a_{m})+D\left(R^{(U)}\right)_{m}\right)\right)=

(a_{0}\leq\cdots\leq a_{m})+D\left(R^{(U)}\right)_{m}

Consequently, we see that the desired equality holds in this case. This means that
\overline{R^{(\zeta)}}\overline{R^{(\varepsilon)}}\sim 1^{\frac{C\left(R^{(U)}\right)}{D\left(R^{(U)}\right)}}
. As a result,
\overline{R^{(\zeta)}}
is a homotopy equivalence.

Now the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{0})}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(U)}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{C\left(R^{(\Delta^{0})}\right)}{D\left(R^{(\Delta^{0})}\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{C\left(R^{(U)}\right)}{D\left(R^{(U)}\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\zeta)}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{R^{(\zeta)}}}

implies that
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\zeta)}\right)
is a homotopy equivalence, hence a quasi-isomorphism.

Next let
n\geq 0
. Recall that we have an identification

\Delta_{m}^{n}=\left\{(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{m})\in\mathbb{N}^{m+1}\;|\;0\leq\alpha_{0}\leq\alpha_{1}\leq\cdots\leq\alpha_{m}\leq n\right\}

for every
m\geq 0
. The simplicial left
R
-module
R^{(\Delta^{n})}
is in degree
m
equal to the free left
R
-module
R^{(\Delta^{n}_{m})}
with a basis
e_{1},...,e_{k}
where
k=|\Delta_{m}^{n}|=\binom{n+m+1}{n}
. If an element of
\Delta^{n}_{m}
is degenerate, then it belongs to the image of some degeneracy morphism
s_{m-1,i}^{\Delta^{n}}
where
0\leq i\leq m-1
, so the corresponding basis element belongs to the image of the degeneracy morphism
R^{\left(s_{m-1,i}^{\Delta^{n}}\right)}=s_{m-1,i}^{R^{(\Delta^{n})}}
. In light of Remark
4.6
, we have the isomorphism

\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right)_{m}\cong\frac{R^{(\Delta_{m}^{n})}}{\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}\operatorname{Im}\left(s_{m-1,i}^{R^{(\Delta^{n})}}\right)}

and we know that
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right)_{m}
is a direct summand of
R^{(\Delta_{m}^{n})}
, so we conclude that
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right)_{m}
is a free left
R
-module with a basis that corresponds to the non-degenerate elements of
\Delta^{n}_{m}
for every
m\geq 0
. As a result, since
\Delta_{n}^{n}=\{(0,1,...,n)\}
, we get
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right)_{n}\cong R^{(\{(0,1,...,n)\})}=R
. Moreover, if
m>n
, then every element of
\Delta^{n}_{m}
is degenerate, so
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right)_{m}=0
. In particular, we have
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{0})}\right)_{0}=R^{(\Delta_{0}^{0})}=R^{(\{0\})}=R
and
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{0})}\right)_{m}=0
for every
m\geq 1
. As a consequence,
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{0})}\right)
is the
R
-complex

0\rightarrow R\rightarrow 0

with
R
in degree
0
. Therefore, we have:

H_{i}\left(\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(U)}\right)\right)\cong H_{i}\left(\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{0})}\right)\right)=\begin{cases}R&\textrm{if }i=0\\
0&\textrm{if }i>0\end{cases}

∎

We next recall the Dold-Kan functor. We use the functorial point of view for simplicial modules in the next definition; see
[
We
, 8.4.4]
.

Definition 4.9
.

Let
R
be a ring. The
Dold-Kan functor

\operatorname{DK}:\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)\rightarrow\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
is defined as follows:

(i)

\operatorname{DK}
assigns to each connective
R
-complex
X
, the contravariant functor
\operatorname{DK}(X):\Delta\rightarrow\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
defined as follows. If
[n]\in\operatorname{Obj}(\Delta)
, then we set

\operatorname{DK}(X)\left([n]\right)=\textstyle\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}{f\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[k]\right)}\\
{f\textrm{ surjective}}\end{subarray}}X_{k}

which is a finite direct sum since there is no surjective map
[n]\rightarrow[k]
if
k>n
. If
\eta\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[m]\right)
, then the
R
-homomorphism

\operatorname{DK}(X)(\eta):\operatorname{DK}(X)\left([m]\right)=\textstyle\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}{g\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([m],[l]\right)}\\
{g\textrm{ surjective}}\end{subarray}}X_{l}\rightarrow\textstyle\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}{f\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[k]\right)}\\
{f\textrm{ surjective}}\end{subarray}}X_{k}=\operatorname{DK}(X)\left([n]\right)

is given by a matrix
\left(\operatorname{DK}(X)(\eta)_{f,g}:X_{l}\rightarrow X_{k}\right)
, where we consider the unique factorization of
g\eta
as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[k]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[l]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}

in which
\iota
is injective and
f
is surjective (see Remark
4.2
), and then we set:

\operatorname{DK}(X)(\eta)_{f,g}=\begin{cases}1^{X_{l}}&\textrm{if }k=l\textrm{ and }\iota=1^{[l]}\\
(-1)^{l}\partial_{l}^{X}&\textrm{if }k=l-1\textrm{ and }\iota=\delta_{l,l}\\
0&\textrm{otherwise}\end{cases}

(ii)

\operatorname{DK}
assigns to each morphism
\phi:X\rightarrow Y
of connective
R
-complexes, the natural transformation
\operatorname{DK}(\phi):\operatorname{DK}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{DK}(Y)
, where for any
[n]\in\operatorname{Obj}(\Delta)
, the
R
-homomorphism

\operatorname{DK}(X)(\phi)^{[n]}:\operatorname{DK}(X)\left([n]\right)=\textstyle\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}{g\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[l]\right)}\\
{g\textrm{ surjective}}\end{subarray}}X_{l}\rightarrow\textstyle\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}{f\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[k]\right)}\\
{f\textrm{ surjective}}\end{subarray}}Y_{k}=\operatorname{DK}(Y)\left([n]\right)

is given by a matrix
\left(\operatorname{DK}(\phi)_{f,g}^{[n]}:X_{l}\rightarrow Y_{k}\right)
where we have:

\operatorname{DK}(X)(\phi)_{f,g}^{[n]}=\begin{cases}\phi_{k}&\textrm{if }f=g\\
0&\textrm{otherwise}\end{cases}

In order to get a flavor of the Dold-Kan functor, we give the following example.

Example 4.10
.

Let
R
be a ring, and
X
a connective
R
-complex. Let
\eta\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([1],[2]\right)
be given by
\eta(0)=0
and
\eta(1)=1
. Consider the following
R
-homomorphism:

\operatorname{DK}(X)(\eta):\operatorname{DK}(X)\left([2]\right)=\textstyle\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}{g\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([2],[l]\right)}\\
{g\textrm{ surjective}}\end{subarray}}X_{l}\rightarrow\textstyle\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}{f\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([1],[k]\right)}\\
{f\textrm{ surjective}}\end{subarray}}X_{k}=\operatorname{DK}(X)\left([1]\right)

We first determine all the surjective maps
g
in
\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([2],[l]\right)
for
0\leq l\leq 2
. We have:

Case I:
l=0
;
g_{1}:[2]\rightarrow[0]
given by
g_{1}(0)=g_{1}(1)=g_{1}(2)=0
.

Case II:
l=1
;
g_{2}:[2]\rightarrow[1]
given by
g_{2}(0)=0
,
g_{2}(1)=1
, and
g_{2}(2)=1
; and
g_{3}:[2]\rightarrow[1]
given by
g_{3}(0)=0
,
g_{3}(1)=0
, and
g_{3}(2)=1
.

Case III:
l=2
; If
g_{4}:[2]\rightarrow[2]
is surjective, then it must be injective as well, so
g_{4}=1^{[2]}
.

We next determine all the surjective maps
f
in
\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([1],[k]\right)
for
0\leq k\leq 1
. We have:

Case I:
k=0
;
f_{1}:[1]\rightarrow[0]
given by
f_{1}(0)=f_{1}(1)=0
.

Case II:
k=1
; If
f_{2}:[1]\rightarrow[1]
is surjective, then it must be injective as well, so
f_{2}=1^{[1]}
.

By direct inspection, we observe that the only possible commutative diagrams are as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[2]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[0]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[0]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1^{[0]}}

,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[2]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1^{[1]}}

,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[2]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[0]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{1,1}}

,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[2]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[2]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{4}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{2,2}}

It follows that

\operatorname{DK}(X)(\eta):\operatorname{DK}(X)\left([2]\right)=X_{0}\oplus X_{1}\oplus X_{1}\oplus X_{2}\rightarrow X_{0}\oplus X_{1}=\operatorname{DK}(X)\left([1]\right)

can be represented by the following matrix:

\begin{pmatrix}1^{X_{0}}&0&-\partial_{1}^{X}&0\\
0&1^{X_{1}}&0&\partial_{2}^{X}\end{pmatrix}

The following proposition describes how the Dold-Kan functor interacts with surjections.

Proposition 4.11
.

Let
R
be a ring, and
X
an
R
-complex. Let
\lambda_{f}^{X}:X_{k}\rightarrow\operatorname{DK}(X)\left([n]\right)
be the canonical injection corresponding to the index
f\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[k]\right)
. If
\eta\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[m]\right)
,
g\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([m],[l]\right)
is surjective, and
x\in X_{l}
, then we have:

\operatorname{DK}(X)(\eta)\left(\lambda_{g}^{X}(x)\right)=\begin{cases}\lambda_{g\eta}^{X}(x)&\textrm{if }g\eta\textrm{ is surjective}\\
0&\textrm{if }g\eta\textrm{ is not surjective but }m\in\operatorname{Im}(\eta)\end{cases}

Proof.

Let
A
denote the matrix representing
\operatorname{DK}(X)(\eta)
as in Definition
4.9
. First suppose that
g\eta
is surjective. We focus on the column
g
of
A
. By the uniqueness of the face-degeneracy factorization in Remark
4.2
, we can only have the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[l]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[l]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1^{[l]}}

This means that the column
g
of
A
has
1^{X_{l}}
in the position
(g\eta,g)
and zero elsewhere. We note that
\lambda_{g}^{X}(x)
has
x
in its coordinate corresponding to
g
and zero elsewhere. Considering
\lambda_{g}^{X}(x)
as a column matrix, we see that the matrix product
A\lambda_{g}^{X}(x)
is equal to the column matrix with only one non-zero coordinate
1^{X_{l}}(x)=x
in position
g\eta
. It follows that
\operatorname{DK}(X)(\eta)\left(\lambda_{g}^{X}(x)\right)=A\lambda_{g}^{X}(x)=\lambda_{g\eta}^{X}(x)
.

Next suppose that
g\eta
is not surjective but
m\in\operatorname{Im}(\eta)
. We focus on the column
g
of
A
. If there is a commutative diagram as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[l]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[l]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1^{[l]}}

in which
f
is surjective, then
g\eta=f
is surjective which contradicts our assumption. Therefore, there is no such diagram. On the other hand, if there is a commutative diagram as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[l-1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[l]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{l,l}}

in which
f
is surjective, then
g\eta=\delta_{l,l}f
. Let
g(m)=p\in[l]
. If
p\neq l
, then
p<l
. Since
g
is surjective, there is an element
r\in[m]
such that
g(r)=l
. But then
g(m)=p<l=g(r)
which is a contradiction since
r\leq m
. Therefore,
g(m)=p=l
. Since
m\in\operatorname{Im}(\eta)
, there is an element
s\in[n]
such that
\eta(s)=m
. But then
\delta_{l,l}\left(f(s)\right)=g\left(\eta(s)\right)=g(m)=l
, so
l\in\operatorname{Im}(\delta_{l,l})
which is a contradiction. As a consequence, there is no such diagram either. This means that the column
g
of
A
is entirely zero. Considering
\lambda_{g}^{X}(x)
as a column matrix, we see that the matrix product
A\lambda_{g}^{X}(x)
is equal to the zero column matrix. It follows that
\operatorname{DK}(X)(\eta)\left(\lambda_{g}^{X}(x)\right)=A\lambda_{g}^{X}(x)=0
.
∎

Corollary 4.12
.

Let
R
be a ring, and
X
an
R
-complex. Let
\lambda_{f}^{X}:X_{k}\rightarrow\operatorname{DK}(X)\left([n]\right)
be the canonical injection corresponding to the index
f\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[k]\right)
. If
g\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[l]\right)
is surjective and
x\in X_{l}
, then we have

d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{g}^{X}(x)\right)=\begin{cases}\lambda_{g\delta_{n,i}}^{X}(x)&\textrm{if }0\leq i\leq n\textrm{ and }g\delta_{n,i}\textrm{ is surjective}\\
0&\textrm{if }0\leq i\leq n-1\textrm{ and }g\delta_{n,i}\textrm{ is not surjective}\\
(-1)^{l}\lambda_{\overline{g}}^{X}\left(\partial_{l}^{X}(x)\right)&\textrm{if }i=n\textrm{ and }g\delta_{n,n}\textrm{ is not surjective}\end{cases}

for every
0\leq i\leq n
, where
\overline{g}\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n-1],[l-1]\right)
is induced by
g
.

Proof.

Let
0\leq i\leq n
. We note that
\delta_{n,i}\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n-1],[n]\right)
and
d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}=\operatorname{DK}(X)(\delta_{n,i})
. Let
A_{n,i}
denote the matrix representing
d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}
as in Definition
4.9
. If
g\delta_{n,i}
is surjective, then by Proposition
4.11
, we have
d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{g}^{X}(x)\right)=\operatorname{DK}(X)(\delta_{n,i})\left(\lambda_{g}^{X}(x)\right)=\lambda_{g\delta_{n,i}}^{X}(x)
. If
0\leq i\leq n-1
, then
n\in\operatorname{Im}(\delta_{n,i})
, so if in addition,
g\delta_{n,i}
is not surjective, then Proposition
4.11
implies that
d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{g}^{X}(x)\right)=\operatorname{DK}(X)(\delta_{n,i})\left(\lambda_{g}^{X}(x)\right)=0
. Now suppose that
g\delta_{n,n}
is not surjective. We focus on the column
g
of
A_{n,i}
. If there is a commutative diagram as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n-1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[l]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[l]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{n,n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1^{[l]}}

in which
f
is surjective, then then
g\delta_{n,n}=f
is surjective which contradicts our assumption. Therefore, there is no such diagram. On the other hand, as we saw in the proof of Proposition
4.11
, since
g
is surjective, we must have
g(n)=l
. But
g\delta_{n,n}
is not surjective which means that if we omit
n
from the domain of
g
, then it will no longer remain surjective. That is to say,
l
is assumed by
g
only at
n
. It follows that
g
induces a surjective map
\overline{g}\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n-1],[l-1]\right)
given by
\overline{g}(i)=g(i)
for every
i\in[n-1]
. Therefore, we get the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n-1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[l-1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[l]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{n,n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{l,l}}

This implies that the column of the matrix
A_{n,i}
corresponding to
g
has all its entries zero except for the entry in the position
(\overline{g},g)
which is equal to
(-1)^{l}\partial_{l}^{X}
. As a result, we have
d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{g}^{X}(x)\right)=A_{n,i}\lambda_{g}^{X}(x)=(-1)^{l}\lambda_{\overline{g}}^{X}\left(\partial_{l}^{X}(x)\right)
.
∎

Corollary 4.13
.

Let
R
be a ring, and
X
an
R
-complex. Let
\lambda_{f}^{X}:X_{k}\rightarrow\operatorname{DK}(X)\left([n]\right)
be the canonical injection corresponding to the index
f\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[k]\right)
. If
g\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[l]\right)
is surjective and
x\in X_{l}
, then we have

s_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{g}^{X}(x)\right)=\lambda_{g\sigma_{n,i}}^{X}(x)

for every
0\leq i\leq n
.

Proof.

Let
0\leq i\leq n
. We note that
\sigma_{n,i}\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n+1],[n]\right)
and
s_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}=\operatorname{DK}(X)(\sigma_{n,i})
. Since
\sigma_{n,i}
is surjective, we conclude that
g\sigma_{n,i}
is surjective, so by Proposition
4.11
, we have
s_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{g}^{X}(x)\right)=\operatorname{DK}(X)(\sigma_{n,i})\left(\lambda_{g}^{X}(x)\right)=\lambda_{g\sigma_{n,i}}^{X}(x)
.
∎

Here is the celebrated Dold-Kan Correspondence.

Theorem 4.14
.

Let
R
be a ring. Then there is an adjoint equivalence of categories as follows:

(\operatorname{DK},\operatorname{Nor}):\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)\leftrightarrows\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)

Proof.

See
[
We
, Theorem 8.4.1 and Exercise 8.4.2]
.
∎

It is worth noting that under the Dold-Kan Correspondence, the connective chain complex concentrated in degree zero corresponds to the constant simplicial module.

The cofibrant generation property of the model structure of connective chain complexes in conjunction with the Dold-Kan Correspondence allow us to transfer the model structure of connective chain complexes to simplicial modules.

Theorem 4.15
.

Let
R
be a ring. Then
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
is a cofibrantly generated model category with the model structure given as follows:

(i)

Fibrations are morphisms
f:M\rightarrow N
in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
such that
\operatorname{Nor}(f)_{i}:\operatorname{Nor}(M)_{i}\rightarrow\operatorname{Nor}(N)_{i}
is surjective for every
i\geq 1
.

(ii)

Cofibrations are morphisms
f:M\rightarrow N
in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
such that
\operatorname{Nor}(f)_{i}:\operatorname{Nor}(M)_{i}\rightarrow\operatorname{Nor}(N)_{i}
is injective and
\operatorname{Coker}\left(\operatorname{Nor}(f)_{i}\right)
is a projective left
R
-module for every
i\geq 0
.

(iii)

Weak equivalences are morphisms
f:M\rightarrow N
in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
such that
\operatorname{Nor}(f):\operatorname{Nor}(M)\rightarrow\operatorname{Nor}(N)
is a quasi-isomorphism in
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
.

Proof.

Follows from Corollary
2.16
, Theorem
3.1
, and Theorem
4.14
.
∎

5.
Shuffle Product and a Variant of Eilenberg-Zilber Theorem

In this section, we introduce the notion of shuffle product of connective chain complexes and prove a variant of the Eilenberg-Zilber Theorem. This will equip us with a formidable tool to overcome the acyclicity condition in Theorem
2.9
.

We first review the concept of a shuffle. For any
p,q\geq 0
, let
S_{p+q}
denote the symmetric group on the set
\{1,2,...,p+q\}
, and
\operatorname{Sh}(p,q)
denote the set of
(p,q)
-shuffles in
S_{p+q}
, i.e.

\operatorname{Sh}(p,q)=\left\{\nu\in S_{p+q}\mid\nu(1)<\cdots<\nu(p)\textrm{ and }\nu(p+1)<\cdots<\nu(p+q)\right\}.

We note that
|\operatorname{Sh}(p,q)|=\binom{p+q}{p}
. For any
\nu\in\operatorname{Sh}(p,q)
, consider the set:

A_{\nu}=\left\{\left(\nu(\alpha),\nu(\beta)\right)\mid 1\leq\alpha\leq p\textrm{, }p+1\leq\beta\leq p+q,\textrm{ and }\nu(\alpha)>\nu(\beta)\right\}

Then
\operatorname{sgn}(\nu)=(-1)^{|A_{\nu}|}
. Indeed, we fix the elements
\nu(p+1)<\cdots<\nu(p+q)
, and then for each
\alpha=p,p-1,...,1
, we compare
\nu(\alpha)
with these elements to see how many moves to the right are needed in order to put
\nu(\alpha)
in the right place.

We next fix some notations in the next remark.

Remark 5.1
.

Let
n\geq 0
and
0\leq k,l\leq n
. Moreover, let
f\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[k]\right)
and
g\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[l]\right)
be surjective. Define a map
\phi_{f,g}:[n]\rightarrow[k]\times[l]
by setting
\phi_{f,g}(i)=\left(f(i),g(i)\right)
for every
i\in[n]
. Suppose that
f
and
g
are not bijective. As in Remark
4.2
, let
0\leq j_{1}<\cdots<j_{n-k}<n
be the elements of
[n]
for which
f(j)=f(j+1)
so that
f=\sigma_{k,j_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{n-1,j_{n-k}}
, and
0\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{n-l}<n
the elements of
[n]
for which
g(i)=g(i+1)
so that
g=\sigma_{l,i_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{n-1,i_{n-l}}
. If
\phi_{f,g}
is not injective, then there are integers
m,m^{\prime}\in[n]
with
m<m^{\prime}
such that
\phi_{f,g}(m)=\phi_{f,g}(m^{\prime})
, so we get
f(m)=f(m^{\prime})
and
g(m)=g(m^{\prime})
. But
f(m)\leq f(m+1)\leq f(m^{\prime})
and
g(m)\leq g(m+1)\leq g(m^{\prime})
, so
f(m)=f(m+1)
and
g(m)=g(m+1)
. This means that
m\in\{i_{1},...,i_{n-l}\}\cap\{j_{1},...,j_{n-k}\}
. Conversely, if
m\in\{i_{1},...,i_{n-l}\}\cap\{j_{1},...,j_{n-k}\}
, then
\phi_{f,g}(m)=\phi_{f,g}(m+1)
, so
\phi_{f,g}
is not injective. As a result,
\phi_{f,g}
is injective if and only if
\{i_{1},...,i_{n-l}\}\cap\{j_{1},...,j_{n-k}\}=\emptyset
. If
f
is bijective, then
f=1^{[n]}
, so
\{j_{1},...,j_{n-k}\}=\emptyset
, and
\phi_{f,g}
is injective. A similar result holds when
g
is bijective. Including these two degenerate cases as well, we can state in general that
\phi_{f,g}
is injective if and only if
\{i_{1},...,i_{n-l}\}\cap\{j_{1},...,j_{n-k}\}=\emptyset
.

Next let
n\geq 0
and
0\leq k,l\leq n
with
k+l=n
. Set:

\mathcal{S}_{n}^{k,l}=\left\{(f,g)\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}([n],[k])\times\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}([n],[l])\;|\;\begin{tabular}[]{ccc}$f$ and $g$ are surjective, and $\phi_{f,g}:[n]\rightarrow[k]\times[l]$ given\\
by $\phi_{f,g}(i)=(f(i),g(i))$ for every $i\in[n]$ is injective\end{tabular}\right\}

Let
(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{k,l}
. As before, let
0\leq j_{1}<\cdots<j_{l}<n
be the elements of
[n]
for which
f(j)=f(j+1)
so that
f=\sigma_{k,j_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{n-1,j_{l}}
, and
0\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}<n
the elements of
[n]
for which
g(i)=g(i+1)
so that
g=\sigma_{l,i_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{n-1,i_{k}}
. Define a shuffle
\nu_{f,g}\in\operatorname{Sh}(k,l)
by setting
\nu_{f,g}(\alpha)=i_{\alpha}+1
for every
1\leq\alpha\leq k
and
\nu_{f,g}(\alpha)=j_{\alpha-k}+1
for every
k+1\leq\alpha\leq n
. In other words,
\nu_{f,g}
can be represented as follows:

\nu_{f,g}=\begin{pmatrix}1&\cdots&k&k+1&\cdots&n\\
i_{1}+1&\cdots&i_{k}+1&j_{1}+1&\cdots&j_{l}+1\end{pmatrix}

In the degenerate case where
f
is bijective, we have
k=n
, so
l=0
, whence
g=0
, implying that
\{i_{1},...,i_{n}\}=\{0,...,n-1\}
. Therefore,
\nu_{f,g}=1^{[n]}
, so
\operatorname{sgn}(\nu_{f,g})=1
. The degenerate case where
g
is bijective leads to a similar conclusion. Now define a map
\eta:\mathcal{S}_{n}^{k,l}\rightarrow\operatorname{Sh}(k,l)
by setting
\eta(f,g)=\nu_{f,g}
for every
(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{k,l}
. On the other hand, for any shuffle
\nu\in\operatorname{Sh}(k,l)
, define two maps as follows:

f_{n,\nu}=\sigma_{k,\nu(k+1)-1}\cdots\sigma_{n-1,\nu(n)-1}:[n]\rightarrow[k]

and

g_{n,\nu}=\sigma_{l,\nu(1)-1}\cdots\sigma_{n-1,\nu(k)-1}:[n]\rightarrow[l]

Then it is clear that
(f_{n,\nu},g_{n,\nu})\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{k,l}
. Next define a map
\theta:\operatorname{Sh}(k,l)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{n}^{k,l}
by setting
\theta(\nu)=(f_{n,\nu},g_{n,\nu})
for every
\nu\in\operatorname{Sh}(k,l)
. It is easily seen that
\eta
and
\theta
are mutually inverse maps. Hence the two sets
\mathcal{S}_{n}^{k,l}
and
\operatorname{Sh}(k,l)
are in 1-1 correspondence.

We now define the shuffle product of connective chain complexes using the Dold-Kan functor. The terminology is justified by Remark
5.1
.

Definition 5.2
.

Let
R
be a ring,
X
a connective
R^{\operatorname{op}}
-complex, and
Y
a connective
R
-complex. Then the
shuffle product

X\boxtimes_{R}Y
is defined as follows. For any
n\geq 0
, set:

\mathcal{S}_{n}=\left\{(f,g)\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[k]\right)\times\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[l]\right)\;|\;\begin{tabular}[]{ccc}$0\leq k,l\leq n$, $f$ and $g$ are surjective, and $\phi_{f,g}:[n]\rightarrow[k]\times[l]$\\
given by $\phi_{f,g}(i)=\left(f(i),g(i)\right)$ for every $i\in[n]$ is injective\end{tabular}\right\}

Then we have

(X\boxtimes_{R}Y)_{n}:=\textstyle\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}(X_{k}\otimes_{R}Y_{l})

for every
n\geq 0
. Moreover, if
\lambda_{f}^{X}:X_{k}\rightarrow\operatorname{DK}(X)\left([n]\right)
is the canonical injection corresponding to the index
f\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[k]\right)
,
\lambda_{g}^{Y}:Y_{l}\rightarrow\operatorname{DK}(Y)\left([n]\right)
is the canonical injection corresponding to the index
g\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[l]\right)
, and
\lambda_{f,g}^{X,Y}:X_{k}\otimes_{R}Y_{l}\rightarrow(X\boxtimes_{R}Y)_{n}
is the canonical injection corresponding to the indices
f\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[k]\right)
and
g\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[l]\right)
, then the differential

\partial_{n}^{X\boxtimes_{R}Y}:(X\boxtimes_{R}Y)_{n}\rightarrow(X\boxtimes_{R}Y)_{n-1}

is given by

\partial_{n}^{X\boxtimes_{R}Y}\left(\lambda_{f,g}^{X,Y}(x\otimes y)\right):=\textstyle\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{f}^{X}(x)\right)\otimes d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\left(\lambda_{g}^{Y}(y)\right)

for every
n\geq 0
,
(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
,
x\in X_{k}
, and
y\in Y_{l}
.

We need to first show that the shuffle product of two complexes is indeed a complex.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
R
be a ring,
X
a connective
R^{\operatorname{op}}
-complex, and
Y
a connective
R
-complex. Then
X\boxtimes_{R}Y
is a connective
\mathbb{Z}
-complex.

Proof.

Let
n\geq 0
. Then we have:

\begin{split}C\left(\operatorname{DK}(X)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{DK}(Y)\right)_{n}&=\left(\operatorname{DK}(X)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{DK}(Y)\right)_{n}=\operatorname{DK}(X)\left([n]\right)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{DK}(Y)\left([n]\right)\\
&=\left(\textstyle\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}{f\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[k]\right)}\\
{f\textrm{ surjective}}\end{subarray}}X_{k}\right)\otimes_{R}\left(\textstyle\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}{g\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[l]\right)}\\
{g\textrm{ surjective}}\end{subarray}}Y_{l}\right)\\
&\cong\textstyle\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}{f\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[k]\right)}\\
{g\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[l]\right)}\\
{f\textrm{ and }g\textrm{ surjective}}\end{subarray}}(X_{k}\otimes_{R}Y_{l})\end{split}

We suppress the above isomorphism and consider it as an equality in what follows. Let
\lambda_{f}^{X}:X_{k}\rightarrow\operatorname{DK}(X)\left([n]\right)
be the canonical injection corresponding to the index
f\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[k]\right)
,
\lambda_{g}^{Y}:Y_{l}\rightarrow\operatorname{DK}(Y)\left([n]\right)
the canonical injection corresponding to the index
g\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[l]\right)
, and
\lambda_{f,g}^{X,Y}:X_{k}\otimes_{R}Y_{l}\rightarrow C\left(\operatorname{DK}(X)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{DK}(Y)\right)_{n}
the canonical injection corresponding to the indices
f\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[k]\right)
and
g\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[l]\right)
. Then it is clear that
\lambda_{f}^{X}(x)\otimes\lambda_{g}^{Y}(y)=\lambda_{f,g}^{X,Y}(x\otimes y)
for every
x\in X_{k}
and
y\in Y_{l}
, so we have
\lambda_{f}^{X}\otimes_{R}\lambda_{g}^{Y}=\lambda_{f,g}^{X,Y}
. As a result, we get

\begin{split}\partial_{n}^{C\left(\operatorname{DK}(X)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{DK}(Y)\right)}\left(\lambda_{f,g}^{X,Y}(x\otimes y)\right)&=\textstyle\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\left(\lambda_{f,g}^{X,Y}(x\otimes y)\right)\\
&=\textstyle\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}\left(d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\otimes_{R}d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\right)\left(\lambda_{f}^{X}(x)\otimes\lambda_{g}^{Y}(y)\right)\\
&=\textstyle\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{f}^{X}(x)\right)\otimes d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\left(\lambda_{g}^{Y}(y)\right)\end{split}

for every
\lambda_{f,g}^{X,Y}(x\otimes y)\in C\left(\operatorname{DK}(X)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{DK}(Y)\right)_{n}
. We show that
X\boxtimes_{R}Y
is a subcomplex of
C\left(\operatorname{DK}(X)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{DK}(Y)\right)
. Let
n\geq 0
, and let
\mathcal{S}_{n}
be as in Definition
5.2
. Then we can write:

\begin{split}C\left(\operatorname{DK}(X)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{DK}(Y)\right)_{n}&=\left(\textstyle\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}(X_{k}\otimes_{R}Y_{l})\right)\oplus\left(\textstyle\bigoplus_{(f,g)\notin\mathcal{S}_{n}}(X_{k}\otimes_{R}Y_{l})\right)\\
&=(X\boxtimes_{R}Y)_{n}\oplus\left(\textstyle\bigoplus_{(f,g)\notin\mathcal{S}_{n}}(X_{k}\otimes_{R}Y_{l})\right)\end{split}

Next let
(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
. We show that
\partial_{n}^{C\left(\operatorname{DK}(X)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{DK}(Y)\right)}\left(\lambda_{f,g}^{X,Y}(x\otimes y)\right)\in(X\boxtimes_{R}Y)_{n-1}
for every
x\in X_{k}
and
y\in Y_{l}
. To this end, fix
0\leq i\leq n
, and consider the following cases:

Case I:
f\delta_{n,i}
is not surjective but
g\delta_{n,i}
is surjective

If
0\leq i\leq n-1
, then by Corollary
4.12
,
d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{f}^{X}(x)\right)=0
, so
d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{f}^{X}(x)\right)\otimes d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\left(\lambda_{g}^{Y}(y)\right)=0\in(X\boxtimes_{R}Y)_{n-1}
. Hence we can assume that
i=n
. In this case, as in the proof of Corollary
4.12
,
f
induces a surjective map
\overline{f}\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n-1],[k-1]\right)
given by
\overline{f}(i)=f(i)
for every
i\in[n-1]
. Accordingly, using Corollary
4.12
, we have:

d_{n,n}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{f}^{X}(x)\right)\otimes d_{n,n}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\left(\lambda_{g}^{Y}(y)\right)=(-1)^{k}\lambda_{\overline{f}}^{X}\left(\partial_{k}^{X}(x)\right)\otimes\lambda_{g\delta_{n,n}}^{Y}(y)=(-1)^{k}\lambda_{\overline{f},g\delta_{n,n}}^{X,Y}\left(\partial_{k}^{X}(x)\otimes y\right)

If
\left(\overline{f},g\delta_{n,n}\right)\notin\mathcal{S}_{n-1}
, then
\phi_{\overline{f},g\delta_{n,n}}
is not injective, so there is an
0\leq m\leq n-2
such that
\phi_{\overline{f},g\delta_{n,n}}(m)=\phi_{\overline{f},g\delta_{n,n}}(m+1)
. But we note that:

\phi_{\overline{f},g\delta_{n,n}}(m)=\left(\overline{f}(m),(g\delta_{n,n})(m)\right)=\left(f(m),g(m)\right)=\phi_{f,g}(m)

and

\phi_{\overline{f},g\delta_{n,n}}(m+1)=\left(\overline{f}(m+1),(g\delta_{n,n})(m+1)\right)=\left(f(m+1),g(m+1)\right)=\phi_{f,g}(m+1)

It follows that
\phi_{f,g}
is not injective, so
(f,g)\notin\mathcal{S}_{n}
which is a contradiction. Therefore,
\left(\overline{f},g\delta_{n,n}\right)\in\mathcal{S}_{n-1}
, so
\lambda_{\overline{f},g\delta_{n,n}}^{X,Y}\left(\partial_{k}^{X}(x)\otimes y\right)\in(X\boxtimes_{R}Y)_{n-1}
.

Case II:
f\delta_{n,i}
is surjective but
g\delta_{n,i}
is not surjective

This case is similar to Case I.

Case III:
f\delta_{n,i}
and
g\delta_{n,i}
are both surjective

In this case, using Corollary
4.12
, we have:

d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{f}^{X}(x)\right)\otimes d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\left(\lambda_{g}^{Y}(y)\right)=\lambda_{f\delta_{n,i}}^{X}(x)\otimes\lambda_{g\delta_{n,i}}^{Y}(y)=\lambda_{f\delta_{n,i},g\delta_{n,i}}^{X,Y}\left(x\otimes y\right)

If
\left(f\delta_{n,i},g\delta_{n,i}\right)\notin\mathcal{S}_{n-1}
, then
\phi_{f\delta_{n,i},g\delta_{n,i}}
is not injective, so there is an
0\leq m\leq n-2
such that
\phi_{f\delta_{n,i},g\delta_{n,i}}(m)=\phi_{f\delta_{n,i},g\delta_{n,i}}(m+1)
. But we note that:

\phi_{f\delta_{n,i},g\delta_{n,i}}(m)=\left((f\delta_{n,i})(m),(g\delta_{n,i})(m)\right)=\begin{cases}\left(f(m+1),g(m+1)\right)=\phi_{f,g}(m+1)&\textrm{if }i\leq m\\
\left(f(m),g(m)\right)=\phi_{f,g}(m)&\textrm{if }i=m+1\\
\left(f(m),g(m)\right)=\phi_{f,g}(m)&\textrm{if }m+1<i\end{cases}

and
