\phi_{f\delta_{n,i},g\delta_{n,i}}(m+1)=\left((f\delta_{n,i})(m+1),(g\delta_{n,i})(m+1)\right)=\begin{cases}\left(f(m+2),g(m+2)\right)=\phi_{f,g}(m+2)&\textrm{if }i\leq m\\
\left(f(m+2),g(m+2)\right)=\phi_{f,g}(m+2)&\textrm{if }i=m+1\\
\left(f(m+1),g(m+1)\right)=\phi_{f,g}(m+1)&\textrm{if }m+1<i\end{cases}

In any case, it follows that
\phi_{f,g}
is not injective, so
(f,g)\notin\mathcal{S}_{n}
which is a contradiction. Therefore,
\left(f\delta_{n,i},g\delta_{n,i}\right)\in\mathcal{S}_{n-1}
, so
\lambda_{f\delta_{n,i},g\delta_{n,i}}^{X,Y}\left(x\otimes y\right)\in(X\boxtimes_{R}Y)_{n-1}
.

Case IV:
f\delta_{n,i}
and
g\delta_{n,i}
are neither surjective

If
0\leq i\leq n-1
, then by Corollary
4.12
,
d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{f}^{X}(x)\right)=0=d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\left(\lambda_{g}^{Y}(y)\right)
, so
d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{f}^{X}(x)\right)\otimes d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\left(\lambda_{g}^{Y}(y)\right)=0\in(X\boxtimes_{R}Y)_{n-1}
. Hence we can assume that
i=n
. In this case, as in the proof of Corollary
4.12
,
f
induces a surjective map
\overline{f}\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n-1],[k-1]\right)
given by
\overline{f}(i)=f(i)
for every
i\in[n-1]
, and
g
induces a surjective map
\overline{g}\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n-1],[l-1]\right)
given by
\overline{g}(i)=g(i)
for every
i\in[n-1]
. Accordingly, using Corollary
4.12
, we have:

d_{n,n}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{f}^{X}(x)\right)\otimes d_{n,n}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\left(\lambda_{g}^{Y}(y)\right)=(-1)^{k}\lambda_{\overline{f}}^{X}\left(\partial_{k}^{X}(x)\right)\otimes(-1)^{l}\lambda_{\overline{g}}^{Y}\left(\partial_{l}^{Y}(y)\right)=(-1)^{k+l}\lambda_{\overline{f},\overline{g}}^{X,Y}\left(\partial_{k}^{X}(x)\otimes\partial_{l}^{Y}(y)\right)

If
\left(\overline{f},\overline{g}\right)\notin\mathcal{S}_{n-1}
, then
\phi_{\overline{f},\overline{g}}
is not injective, so there is an
0\leq m\leq n-2
such that
\phi_{\overline{f},\overline{g}}(m)=\phi_{\overline{f},\overline{g}}(m+1)
. But we note that:

\phi_{\overline{f},\overline{g}}(m)=\left(\overline{f}(m),\overline{g}(m)\right)=\left(f(m),g(m)\right)=\phi_{f,g}(m)

and

\phi_{\overline{f},\overline{g}}(m+1)=\left(\overline{f}(m+1),\overline{g}(m+1)\right)=\left(f(m+1),g(m+1)\right)=\phi_{f,g}(m+1)

It follows that
\phi_{f,g}
is not injective, so
(f,g)\notin\mathcal{S}_{n}
which is a contradiction. Therefore,
\left(\overline{f},\overline{g}\right)\in\mathcal{S}_{n-1}
, so
\lambda_{\overline{f},\overline{g}}^{X,Y}\left(\partial_{k}^{X}(x)\otimes\partial_{l}^{Y}(y)\right)\in(X\boxtimes_{R}Y)_{n-1}
.

All in all, we conclude that

\partial_{n}^{C\left(\operatorname{DK}(X)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{DK}(Y)\right)}\left(\lambda_{f,g}^{X,Y}(x\otimes y)\right)=\textstyle\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{f}^{X}(x)\right)\otimes d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\left(\lambda_{g}^{Y}(y)\right)\in(X\boxtimes_{R}Y)_{n-1}

for every
x\in X_{k}
and
y\in Y_{l}
. It now follows that
X\boxtimes_{R}Y
is a subcomplex of
C\left(\operatorname{DK}(X)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{DK}(Y)\right)
.
∎

We next establish the functoriality of the shuffle product.

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
R
be a ring. Then the following assertions hold:

(i)

If
X
is a connective
R^{\operatorname{op}}
-complex, then
X\boxtimes_{R}-:\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(\mathbb{Z})
defines a covariant functor.

(ii)

If
Y
is a connective
R
-complex, then
-\boxtimes_{R}Y:\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R^{\operatorname{op}})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(\mathbb{Z})
defines a covariant functor.

Proof.

(i): Let
n\geq 0
, and let
\mathcal{S}_{n}
be as in Definition
5.2
. Let
X
be a connective
R^{\operatorname{op}}
-complex. If
Y
is a connective
R
-complex, then by Proposition
5.3
,
X\boxtimes_{R}Y
is a connective
\mathbb{Z}
-complex. On the other hand, if
\theta:Y\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
is a morphism of connective
R
-complexes, then we define the morphism
X\boxtimes_{R}\theta:X\boxtimes_{R}Y\rightarrow X\boxtimes_{R}Y^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{Z}
-complexes as follows. Let
n\geq 0
. Then we have
(X\boxtimes_{R}Y)_{n}=\textstyle\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}(X_{k}\otimes_{R}Y_{l})
and
(X\boxtimes_{R}Y^{\prime})_{n}=\textstyle\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}(X_{k}\otimes_{R}Y_{l}^{\prime})
. We set

(X\boxtimes_{R}\theta)_{n}\left(\lambda_{f,g}^{X,Y}(x\otimes y)\right):=\lambda_{f,g}^{X,Y^{\prime}}\left(x\otimes\theta_{l}(y)\right)

for every
(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
,
x\in X_{k}
, and
y\in Y_{l}
. Extending it linearly to
(X\boxtimes_{R}Y)_{n}
, we see that
(X\boxtimes_{R}\theta)_{n}
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-homomorphism. Moreover, the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X\boxtimes_{R}Y)_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X\boxtimes_{R}Y)_{n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X\boxtimes_{R}Y^{\prime})_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X\boxtimes_{R}Y^{\prime})_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{n}^{X\boxtimes_{R}Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X\boxtimes_{R}\theta)_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X\boxtimes_{R}\theta)_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{n}^{X\boxtimes_{R}Y^{\prime}}}

Indeed, we have for every
(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
,
x\in X_{k}
, and
y\in Y_{l}
:

\begin{split}\partial_{n}^{X\boxtimes_{R}Y^{\prime}}\left((X\boxtimes_{R}\theta)_{n}\left(\lambda_{f,g}^{X,Y}(x\otimes y)\right)\right)&=\partial_{n}^{X\boxtimes_{R}Y^{\prime}}\left(\lambda_{f,g}^{X,Y^{\prime}}\left(x\otimes\theta_{l}(y)\right)\right)\\
&=\textstyle\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{f}^{X}(x)\right)\otimes d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y^{\prime})}\left(\lambda_{g}^{Y^{\prime}}\left(\theta_{l}(y)\right)\right)\end{split}

and

\begin{split}(X\boxtimes_{R}\theta)_{n-1}\left(\partial_{n}^{X\boxtimes_{R}Y}\left(\lambda_{f,g}^{X,Y}(x\otimes y)\right)\right)&=(X\boxtimes_{R}\theta)_{n-1}\left(\textstyle\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{f}^{X}(x)\right)\otimes d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\left(\lambda_{g}^{Y}(y)\right)\right)\\
&=\textstyle\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}(X\boxtimes_{R}\theta)_{n-1}\left(d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{f}^{X}(x)\right)\otimes d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\left(\lambda_{g}^{Y}(y)\right)\right)\end{split}

Let
0\leq i\leq n
, and consider the following cases from Proposition
5.3
:

Case I:
f\delta_{n,i}
is not surjective but
g\delta_{n,i}
is surjective

If
0\leq i\leq n-1
, then
d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{f}^{X}(x)\right)=0
, so we have:

d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{f}^{X}(x)\right)\otimes d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y^{\prime})}\left(\lambda_{g}^{Y^{\prime}}\left(\theta_{l}(y)\right)\right)=0=d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{f}^{X}(x)\right)\otimes d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\left(\lambda_{g}^{Y}(y)\right)

If
i=n
, then we have:

\begin{split}d_{n,n}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{f}^{X}(x)\right)\otimes d_{n,n}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y^{\prime})}\left(\lambda_{g}^{Y^{\prime}}\left(\theta_{l}(y)\right)\right)&=(-1)^{k}\lambda_{\overline{f},g\delta_{n,n}}^{X,Y^{\prime}}\left(\partial_{k}^{X}(x)\otimes\theta_{l}(y)\right)\\
&=(X\boxtimes_{R}\theta)_{n-1}\left((-1)^{k}\lambda_{\overline{f},g\delta_{n,n}}^{X,Y}\left(\partial_{k}^{X}(x)\otimes y\right)\right)\\
&=(X\boxtimes_{R}\theta)_{n-1}\left(d_{n,n}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{f}^{X}(x)\right)\otimes d_{n,n}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\left(\lambda_{g}^{Y}(y)\right)\right)\end{split}

Case II:
f\delta_{n,i}
is surjective but
g\delta_{n,i}
is not surjective

This case is similar to Case I.

Case III:
f\delta_{n,i}
and
g\delta_{n,i}
are both surjective

In this case, we have:

\begin{split}d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{f}^{X}(x)\right)\otimes d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y^{\prime})}\left(\lambda_{g}^{Y^{\prime}}\left(\theta_{l}(y)\right)\right)&=\lambda_{f\delta_{n,i},g\delta_{n,i}}^{X,Y^{\prime}}\left(x\otimes\theta_{l}(y)\right)=(X\boxtimes_{R}\theta)_{n-1}\left(\lambda_{f\delta_{n,i},g\delta_{n,i}}^{X,Y}\left(x\otimes y\right)\right)\\
&=(X\boxtimes_{R}\theta)_{n-1}\left(d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{f}^{X}(x)\right)\otimes d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\left(\lambda_{g}^{Y}(y)\right)\right)\end{split}

Case IV:
f\delta_{n,i}
and
g\delta_{n,i}
are neither surjective

If
0\leq i\leq n-1
, then
d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{f}^{X}(x)\right)=d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\left(\lambda_{g}^{Y}(y)\right)=d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y^{\prime})}\left(\lambda_{g}^{Y^{\prime}}\left(\theta_{l}(y)\right)\right)=0
, so we have:

d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{f}^{X}(x)\right)\otimes d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y^{\prime})}\left(\lambda_{g}^{Y^{\prime}}\left(\theta_{l}(y)\right)\right)=0=d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{f}^{X}(x)\right)\otimes d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\left(\lambda_{g}^{Y}(y)\right)

If
i=n
, then using the fact that
\theta:Y\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
is a morphism of
R
-complexes, we get:

\begin{split}d_{n,n}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{f}^{X}(x)\right)\otimes d_{n,n}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y^{\prime})}\left(\lambda_{g}^{Y^{\prime}}\left(\theta_{l}(y)\right)\right)&=(-1)^{k+l}\lambda_{\overline{f},\overline{g}}^{X,Y^{\prime}}\left(\partial_{k}^{X}(x)\otimes\partial_{l}^{Y^{\prime}}\left(\theta_{l}(y)\right)\right)\\
&=(-1)^{k+l}\lambda_{\overline{f},\overline{g}}^{X,Y^{\prime}}\left(\partial_{k}^{X}(x)\otimes\theta_{l-1}\left(\partial_{l}^{Y}(y)\right)\right)\\
&=(X\boxtimes_{R}\theta)_{n-1}\left((-1)^{k+l}\lambda_{\overline{f},\overline{g}}^{X,Y}\left(\partial_{k}^{X}(x)\otimes\partial_{l}^{Y}(y)\right)\right)\\
&=(X\boxtimes_{R}\theta)_{n-1}\left(d_{n,n}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{f}^{X}(x)\right)\otimes d_{n,n}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\left(\lambda_{g}^{Y}(y)\right)\right)\end{split}

We observe from the above analysis that in any case we have:

\partial_{n}^{X\boxtimes_{R}Y^{\prime}}\left((X\boxtimes_{R}\theta)_{n}\left(\lambda_{f,g}^{X,Y}(x\otimes y)\right)\right)=(X\boxtimes_{R}\theta)_{n-1}\left(\partial_{n}^{X\boxtimes_{R}Y}\left(\lambda_{f,g}^{X,Y}(x\otimes y)\right)\right)

Therefore, it follows that
\partial_{n}^{X\boxtimes_{R}Y^{\prime}}(X\boxtimes_{R}\theta)_{n}=(X\boxtimes_{R}\theta)_{n-1}\partial_{n}^{X\boxtimes_{R}Y}
. As a result,
X\boxtimes_{R}\theta:X\boxtimes_{R}Y\rightarrow X\boxtimes_{R}Y^{\prime}
is a morphism of
\mathbb{Z}
-complexes. Finally, we note that
X\boxtimes_{R}-:\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(\mathbb{Z})
preserves the composition of morphisms as well as the identity morphisms, so it defines a covariant functor.

(ii): Similar to (i).
∎

We have the following special cases of the shuffle product.

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
R
be a ring,
X
a connective
R^{\operatorname{op}}
-complex,
Y
a connective
R
-complex,
M
a right
R
-module, and
N
a left
R
-module. Then the following assertions hold:

(i)

X\boxtimes_{R}N=X\otimes_{R}N
.

(ii)

M\boxtimes_{R}Y=M\otimes_{R}Y
.

Proof.

(i): Let
n\geq 0
, and let
\mathcal{S}_{n}
be as in Definition
5.2
. Then we have
(X\boxtimes_{R}N)_{n}=\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}(X_{k}\otimes_{R}N_{l})
. But
N_{l}=0
for every
l\neq 0
, so we should only consider those terms in the latter direct sum that correspond to
l=0
. However, if
(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}
and
l=0
, then
g=0
. But
\phi_{f,g}
is injective, so
f
must be injective. As
f
is already surjective, we conclude that
k=n
and
f=1^{[n]}
. As a result, we only have one possibly non-zero term, i.e.
(X\boxtimes_{R}N)_{n}=X_{n}\otimes_{R}N=(X\otimes_{R}N)_{n}
. On the other hand, since
1^{[n]}\delta_{n,i}=\delta_{n,i}
is not surjective, Corollary
4.12
implies that
d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{1^{[n]}}^{X}(x)\right)=0
for every
0\leq i\leq n-1
and
x\in X_{n}
. Furthermore,
\operatorname{DK}(N)
is a constant simplicial module, so
d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(N)}=1^{N}
for every
0\leq i\leq n
. Therefore, using Corollary
4.12
, we have for every
x\in X_{n}
and
y\in N
:

\begin{split}\partial_{n}^{X\boxtimes_{R}N}(x\otimes y)&=\textstyle\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{1^{[n]}}^{X}(x)\right)\otimes d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(N)}\left(\lambda_{0}^{N}(y)\right)=(-1)^{n}d_{n,n}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{1^{[n]}}^{X}(x)\right)\otimes d_{n,n}^{\operatorname{DK}(N)}\left(\lambda_{0}^{N}(y)\right)\\
&=(-1)^{n}(-1)^{n}\lambda_{1^{[n-1]}}^{X}\left(\partial_{n}^{X}(x)\right)\otimes_{R}\lambda_{0}^{N}(y)=\partial_{n}^{X}(x)\otimes y=\partial_{n}^{X\otimes_{R}N}(x\otimes y)\\
\end{split}

It follows that
\partial_{n}^{X\boxtimes_{R}N}=\partial_{n}^{X\otimes_{R}N}
. Thus
X\boxtimes_{R}N=X\otimes_{R}N
.

(ii): Similar to (i).
∎

The next proposition describes the shuffle product in terms of the normalization and Dold-Kan functors. This in turn provides some useful corollaries.

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
R
be a ring,
X
a connective
R^{\operatorname{op}}
-complex, and
Y
a connective
R
-complex. Then the following natural isomorphism of
\mathbb{Z}
-complexes holds:

X\boxtimes_{R}Y\cong\operatorname{Nor}\left(\operatorname{DK}(X)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{DK}(Y)\right)

Proof.

Let
n\geq 0
, and let
\mathcal{S}_{n}
be as in Definition
5.2
. As in the proof of Proposition
5.3
, we can write:

(5.6.1)

C\left(\operatorname{DK}(X)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{DK}(Y)\right)_{n}=(X\boxtimes_{R}Y)_{n}\oplus\left(\textstyle\bigoplus_{(f,g)\notin\mathcal{S}_{n}}(X_{k}\otimes_{R}Y_{l})\right)

Let
f\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[k]\right)
and
g\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[l]\right)
be surjective. Let
0\leq j_{1}<\cdots<j_{n-k}<n
be the elements of
[n]
for which
f(j)=f(j+1)
so that
f=\sigma_{k,j_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{n-1,j_{n-k}}
, and
0\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{n-l}<n
the elements of
[n]
for which
g(i)=g(i+1)
so that
g=\sigma_{l,i_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{n-1,i_{n-l}}
. If
(f,g)\notin\mathcal{S}_{n}
, then
\phi_{f,g}
is not injective, so there is an element
m\in\{i_{1},...,i_{n-l}\}\cap\{j_{1},...,j_{n-k}\}
. This implies that
f=\sigma_{k,j_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{p,m}\cdots\sigma_{n-1,j_{n-k}}
and
g=\sigma_{l,i_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{q,m}\cdots\sigma_{n-1,i_{n-l}}
where
k\leq p\leq n-1
and
l\leq q\leq n-1
. Using the face-degeneracy relations of Remark
4.2
, we can successively swap the face map
\sigma_{p,m}
with the terms appearing on its right to get
f=\sigma_{k,j_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{n-2,j_{n-k}-1}\sigma_{n-1,m}
. Similarly, we get
g=\sigma_{l,i_{1}}\cdots\sigma_{n-2,i_{n-l}-1}\sigma_{n-1,m}
. Applying the contravariant functor
\operatorname{DK}(X)
, we get
\operatorname{DK}(X)(f)=s_{n-1,m}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}s_{n-2,j_{n-k}-1}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\cdots s_{k,j_{1}}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}
and
\operatorname{DK}(Y)(g)=s_{n-1,m}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}s_{n-2,i_{n-l}-1}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\cdots s_{l,i_{1}}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}
. Moreover, since
f
and
g
are surjective, we can invoke Proposition
4.11
to write:

\lambda_{f}^{X}=\operatorname{DK}(X)(f)\lambda_{1^{[k]}}^{X}=s_{n-1,m}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}s_{n-2,j_{n-k}-1}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\cdots s_{k,j_{1}}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\lambda_{1^{[k]}}^{X}

and

\lambda_{g}^{Y}=\operatorname{DK}(Y)(g)\lambda_{1^{[l]}}^{Y}=s_{n-1,m}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}s_{n-2,i_{n-l}-1}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\cdots s_{l,i_{1}}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\lambda_{1^{[l]}}^{Y}

Therefore, we obtain:

\begin{split}\lambda_{f,g}^{X,Y}&=\lambda_{f}^{X}\otimes_{R}\lambda_{g}^{Y}\\
&=s_{n-1,m}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}s_{n-2,j_{n-k}-1}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\cdots s_{k,j_{1}}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\lambda_{1^{[k]}}^{X}\otimes_{R}s_{n-1,m}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}s_{n-2,i_{n-l}-1}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\cdots s_{l,i_{1}}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\lambda_{1^{[l]}}^{Y}\\
&=\left(s_{n-1,m}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\otimes_{R}s_{n-1,m}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\right)\left(s_{n-2,j_{n-k}-1}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\cdots s_{k,j_{1}}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\lambda_{1^{[k]}}^{X}\otimes_{R}s_{n-2,i_{n-l}-1}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\cdots s_{l,i_{1}}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\lambda_{1^{[l]}}^{Y}\right)\\
&=\left(s_{n-1,m}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\right)\left(s_{n-2,j_{n-k}-1}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\cdots s_{k,j_{1}}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\lambda_{1^{[k]}}^{X}\otimes_{R}s_{n-2,i_{n-l}-1}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\cdots s_{l,i_{1}}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\lambda_{1^{[l]}}^{Y}\right)\end{split}

This shows that:

\operatorname{Im}\left(\lambda_{f,g}^{X,Y}\right)\subseteq\operatorname{Im}\left(s_{n-1,m}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\right)\subseteq\textstyle\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\operatorname{Im}\left(s_{n-1,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\right)

Thus
\lambda_{f,g}^{X,Y}(x\otimes y)\in\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\operatorname{Im}\left(s_{n-1,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\right)
for every
x\in X_{k}
and
y\in Y_{l}
. It follows that:

\textstyle\bigoplus_{(f,g)\notin\mathcal{S}_{n}}(X_{k}\otimes_{R}Y_{l})\subseteq\textstyle\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\operatorname{Im}\left(s_{n-1,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\right)

On the other hand, let
f^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n-1],[k]\right)
and
g^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n-1],[l]\right)
be surjective. Using Corollary
4.13
, we have for every
0\leq i\leq n-1
,
x\in X_{k}
, and
y\in Y_{l}
:

\begin{split}s_{n-1,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\left(\lambda_{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}}^{X,Y}(x\otimes y)\right)&=\left(s_{n-1,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\otimes_{R}s_{n-1,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\right)\left(\left(\lambda_{f^{\prime}}^{X}\otimes_{R}\lambda_{g^{\prime}}^{Y}\right)(x\otimes y)\right)\\
&=\left(s_{n-1,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\lambda_{f^{\prime}}^{X}\otimes_{R}s_{n-1,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\lambda_{g^{\prime}}^{Y}\right)\left(x\otimes y\right)\\
&=\left(\lambda_{f^{\prime}\sigma_{n-1,i}}^{X}\otimes_{R}\lambda_{g^{\prime}\sigma_{n-1,i}}^{Y}\right)\left(x\otimes y\right)\\
&=\lambda_{f^{\prime}\sigma_{n-1,i},g^{\prime}\sigma_{n-1,i}}^{X,Y}\left(x\otimes y\right)\end{split}

Set
f=f^{\prime}\sigma_{n-1,i}\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[k]\right)
and
g=g^{\prime}\sigma_{n-1,i}\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[l]\right)
. As
\sigma_{n-1,i}(i)=i=\sigma_{n-1,i}(i+1)
, we see that
\phi_{f,g}(i)=\left(f(i),g(i)\right)=\left(f(i+1),g(i+1)\right)=\phi_{f,g}(i+1)
. Thus
\phi_{f,g}
is not injective, so
(f,g)\notin\mathcal{S}_{n}
. It follows that:

\textstyle\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\operatorname{Im}\left(s_{n-1,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\right)\subseteq\textstyle\bigoplus_{(f,g)\notin\mathcal{S}_{n}}(X_{k}\otimes_{R}Y_{l})

Therefore, we have:

(5.6.2)

\textstyle\bigoplus_{(f,g)\notin\mathcal{S}_{n}}(X_{k}\otimes_{R}Y_{l})=\textstyle\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\operatorname{Im}\left(s_{n-1,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\right)=D\left(\operatorname{DK}(X)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{DK}(Y)\right)_{n}

Hence we obtain from (
5.6.1
) and (
5.6.2
) that:

C\left(\operatorname{DK}(X)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{DK}(Y)\right)_{n}=(X\boxtimes_{R}Y)_{n}\oplus D\left(\operatorname{DK}(X)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{DK}(Y)\right)_{n}

In light of the proof of Proposition
5.3
and Remark
4.6
, we observe that
X\boxtimes_{R}Y
and
D\left(\operatorname{DK}(X)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{DK}(Y)\right)
are subcomplexes of
C\left(\operatorname{DK}(X)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{DK}(Y)\right)
, so we deduce that:

(5.6.3)

C\left(\operatorname{DK}(X)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{DK}(Y)\right)=(X\boxtimes_{R}Y)\oplus D\left(\operatorname{DK}(X)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{DK}(Y)\right)

On the other hand, we know from Remark
4.6
that:

(5.6.4)

C\left(\operatorname{DK}(X)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{DK}(Y)\right)=\operatorname{Nor}\left(\operatorname{DK}(X)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{DK}(Y)\right)\oplus D\left(\operatorname{DK}(X)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{DK}(Y)\right)

Therefore, it follows from (
5.6.3
) and (
5.6.4
) that
X\boxtimes_{R}Y\cong\operatorname{Nor}\left(\operatorname{DK}(X)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{DK}(Y)\right)
.
∎

Corollary 5.7
.

Let
R
be a ring,
M
a simplicial right
R
-module, and
N
a simplicial left
R
-module. Then the following natural isomorphism of
\mathbb{Z}
-complexes holds:

\operatorname{Nor}(M\otimes_{R}N)\cong\operatorname{Nor}(M)\boxtimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}(N)

Proof.

In view of Theorem
4.14
and Proposition
5.6
, we have the following natural isomorphisms:

\operatorname{Nor}(M\otimes_{R}N)\cong\operatorname{Nor}\left(\operatorname{DK}\left(\operatorname{Nor}(M)\right)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{DK}\left(\operatorname{Nor}(N)\right)\right)\cong\operatorname{Nor}(M)\boxtimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}(N)

∎

Corollary 5.8
.

Let
R
be a ring,
M
a simplicial right
R
-module,
N
a simplicial left
R
-module,
K
a right
R
-module, and
L
a left
R
-module. Then the following natural isomorphisms of
\mathbb{Z}
-complexes hold:

(i)

\operatorname{Nor}(M\otimes_{R}L)\cong\operatorname{Nor}(M)\otimes_{R}L
.

(ii)

\operatorname{Nor}(K\otimes_{R}N)\cong K\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}(N)
.

Proof.

(i): If we consider
L
as a constant simplicial left
R
-module, then
\operatorname{Nor}(L)=L
is the
R
-complex with only
L
concentrated in degree zero. Therefore, in light of Corollary
5.7
and Proposition
5.5
, we get the following natural isomorphism:

\operatorname{Nor}(M\otimes_{R}L)\cong\operatorname{Nor}(M)\boxtimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}(L)=\operatorname{Nor}(M)\boxtimes_{R}L=\operatorname{Nor}(M)\otimes_{R}L

(ii): Similar to (i).
∎

Now we are ready to establish a variant of the Eilenberg-Zilber Theorem; see
[
We
, Chapter 8, Section 8.5]
for the original version.

Theorem 5.9
.

Let
R
be a ring,
X
a connective
R^{\operatorname{op}}
-complex, and
Y
a connective
R
-complex. Then there exists a natural quasi-isomorphism
\nabla^{X,Y}:X\otimes_{R}Y\rightarrow X\boxtimes_{R}Y
of
\mathbb{Z}
-complexes.

Proof.

Let
n\geq 0
. Then
(X\otimes_{R}Y)_{n}=\bigoplus_{k+l=n}(X_{k}\otimes_{R}Y_{l})
. For any
0\leq k,l\leq n
, set:

\mathcal{S}_{n}^{k,l}=\left\{(f,g)\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}([n],[k])\times\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}([n],[l])\;|\;\begin{tabular}[]{ccc}$f$ and $g$ are surjective, and $\phi_{f,g}:[n]\rightarrow[k]\times[l]$ given\\
by $\phi_{f,g}(i)=(f(i),g(i))$ for every $i\in[n]$ is injective\end{tabular}\right\}

Moreover, set
\mathcal{S}_{n}=\bigcup_{0\leq k,l\leq n}\mathcal{S}_{n}^{k,l}
. Then
(X\boxtimes_{R}Y)_{n}=\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}(X_{k}\otimes_{R}Y_{l})
. Now let
k,l\geq 0
be such that
k+l=n
. Let
\iota_{k,l}^{X,Y}:X_{k}\otimes_{R}Y_{l}\rightarrow(X\otimes_{R}Y)_{n}
be the canonical injection. Also, let
\lambda_{f}^{X}:X_{k}\rightarrow\operatorname{DK}(X)([n])
be the canonical injection corresponding to the index
f\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[k]\right)
,
\lambda_{g}^{Y}:Y_{l}\rightarrow\operatorname{DK}(Y)([n])
the canonical injection corresponding to the index
g\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[l]\right)
, and
\lambda_{f,g}^{X,Y}:X_{k}\otimes_{R}Y_{l}\rightarrow(X\boxtimes_{R}Y)_{n}
the canonical injection corresponding to the indices
f\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[k]\right)
and
g\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[l]\right)
. Moreover, let
\nu_{f,g}\in\operatorname{Sh}(k,l)
denote the shuffle determined by a pair
(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{k,l}
as in Remark
5.1
. Define a map
\psi_{k,l}:X_{k}\times Y_{l}\rightarrow(X\boxtimes_{R}Y)_{n}
by setting

\psi_{k,l}(x,y)=\textstyle\sum_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{k,l}}\operatorname{sgn}(\nu_{f,g})\lambda_{f,g}^{X,Y}(x\otimes y)

for every
(x,y)\in X_{k}\times Y_{l}
. It is clear that
\psi_{k,l}
is an
R
-balanced map. Therefore, the universal property of tensor product yields a unique
\mathbb{Z}
-homomorphism
\overline{\psi_{k,l}}:X_{k}\otimes_{R}Y_{l}\rightarrow(X\boxtimes_{R}Y)_{n}
that makes the following diagram commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{k}\times Y_{l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X\boxtimes_{R}Y)_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{k}\otimes_{R}Y_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{k,l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{X_{k},Y_{l}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\psi_{k,l}}}

Then we have

\overline{\psi_{k,l}}(x\otimes y)=\textstyle\sum_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{k,l}}\operatorname{sgn}(\nu_{f,g})\lambda_{f,g}^{X,Y}(x\otimes y)

for every
x\in X_{k}
and
y\in Y_{l}
. By the universal property of direct sum, there is a unique
\mathbb{Z}
-homomorphism
\nabla_{n}^{X,Y}:(X\otimes_{R}Y)_{n}\rightarrow(X\boxtimes_{R}Y)_{n}
that makes the following diagram commutative for every
0\leq k,l\leq n
with
k+l=n
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{k}\otimes_{R}Y_{l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X\otimes_{R}Y)_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X\boxtimes_{R}Y)_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{k,l}^{X,Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\psi_{k,l}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla_{n}^{X,Y}}

Then we have

\nabla_{n}^{X,Y}\left(\textstyle\sum_{k+l=n}\iota_{k,l}^{X,Y}(x_{k}\otimes y_{l})\right)=\textstyle\sum_{k+l=n}\textstyle\sum_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{k,l}}\operatorname{sgn}(\nu_{f,g})\lambda_{f,g}^{X,Y}(x_{k}\otimes y_{l})

for every
\sum_{k+l=n}\iota_{k,l}^{X,Y}(x_{k}\otimes y_{l})\in(X\otimes_{R}Y)_{n}
.

Now set
\nabla^{X,Y}=\left(\nabla_{n}^{X,Y}\right)_{n\geq 0}:X\otimes_{R}Y\rightarrow X\boxtimes_{R}Y
. We show that
\nabla^{X,Y}
is a morphism of
\mathbb{Z}
-complexes. Let
n\geq 0
. Then the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X\otimes_{R}Y)_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X\otimes_{R}Y)_{n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X\boxtimes_{R}Y)_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X\boxtimes_{R}Y)_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{n}^{X\otimes_{R}Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla_{n}^{X,Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla_{n-1}^{X,Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{n}^{X\boxtimes_{R}Y}}

Indeed, we have for every
x\in X_{k}
and
y\in Y_{l}
with
k+l=n
:

\begin{split}\partial_{n}^{X\boxtimes_{R}Y}\left(\nabla_{n}^{X,Y}\left(\iota_{k,l}^{X,Y}(x\otimes y)\right)\right)&=\partial_{n}^{X\boxtimes_{R}Y}\left(\textstyle\sum_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{k,l}}\operatorname{sgn}(\nu_{f,g})\lambda_{f,g}^{X,Y}(x\otimes y)\right)\\
&=\textstyle\sum_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{k,l}}\operatorname{sgn}(\nu_{f,g})\partial_{n}^{X\boxtimes_{R}Y}\left(\lambda_{f,g}^{X,Y}(x\otimes y)\right)\\
&=\textstyle\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}\textstyle\sum_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{k,l}}\operatorname{sgn}(\nu_{f,g})d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{f}^{X}(x)\right)\otimes d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\left(\lambda_{g}^{Y}(y)\right)\\
\end{split}

Let
0\leq i\leq n
and
(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{k,l}
, and consider the following cases:

Case I:
f\delta_{n,i}
is not surjective but
g\delta_{n,i}
is surjective

If
0\leq i\leq n-1
, then by Corollary
4.12
,
d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{f}^{X}(x)\right)=0
, so
d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{f}^{X}(x)\right)\otimes d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\left(\lambda_{g}^{Y}(y)\right)=0
. Hence we can assume that
i=n
. In this case, by Corollary
4.12
,
f
induces a surjective map
\overline{f}\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n-1],[k-1]\right)
given by
\overline{f}(i)=f(i)
for every
i\in[n-1]
. Moreover, we note that
g\delta_{n,n}=g\mid_{[n-1]}:[n-1]\rightarrow[l]
. If
0\leq j_{1}<\cdots<j_{l}<n
are the elements of
[n]
for which
f(j)=f(j+1)
, and
0\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}<n
are the elements of
[n]
for which
g(i)=g(i+1)
, then we have:

\nu_{f,g}=\begin{pmatrix}1&\cdots&k&k+1&\cdots&n\\
i_{1}+1&\cdots&i_{k}+1&j_{1}+1&\cdots&j_{l}+1\end{pmatrix}

Set:

A=\left\{(i_{\alpha}+1,j_{\beta}+1)\mid 1\leq\alpha\leq k\textrm{, }1\leq\beta\leq l\textrm{, and }i_{\alpha}>j_{\beta}\right\}

If
|A|=r
, then
\operatorname{sgn}(\nu_{f,g})=(-1)^{r}
. As
f
is surjective but
f\delta_{n,n}
is not surjective, we see that
j_{l}<n-1
since otherwise,
f(n-1)=f(n)=k
which would imply that
f\delta_{n,n}=f\mid_{[n-1]}:[n-1]\rightarrow[k]
is surjective. Also, since
g
and
g\delta_{n,n}
are both surjective, we notice that
i_{k}=n-1
as
g(n-1)=l=g(n)
. As a result,
0\leq j_{1}<\cdots<j_{l}<n-1
are the elements of
[n-1]
for which
\overline{f}(j)=\overline{f}(j+1)
, and
0\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k-1}<n-1
are the elements of
[n-1]
for which
(g\delta_{n,n})(i)=(g\delta_{n,n})(i+1)
, so we have:

\nu_{\overline{f},g\delta_{n,n}}=\begin{pmatrix}1&\cdots&k-1&k&\cdots&n-1\\
i_{1}+1&\cdots&i_{k-1}+1&j_{1}+1&\cdots&j_{l}+1\end{pmatrix}

Set:

B=\left\{(i_{\alpha}+1,j_{\beta}+1)\mid 1\leq\alpha\leq k-1\textrm{, }1\leq\beta\leq l\textrm{, and }i_{\alpha}>j_{\beta}\right\}

If
|B|=s
, then
\operatorname{sgn}\left(\nu_{\overline{f},g\delta_{n,n}}\right)=(-1)^{s}
. Since
i_{k}=n-1>j_{l}>\cdots>j_{1}
, we conclude that

A=B\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\displaystyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\textstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}{\vtop{\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptscriptstyle#$\hfil\cr\cup\cr\cdot\crcr}}}}\{(i_{k}+1,j_{\beta}+1)\mid 1\leq\beta\leq l\},

which in turn implies that
r=s+l
. Therefore, we have
\operatorname{sgn}(\nu_{f,g})=(-1)^{r}=(-1)^{l}(-1)^{s}=(-1)^{l}\operatorname{sgn}\left(\nu_{\overline{f},g\delta_{n,n}}\right)
. Thus using Corollary
4.12
, we get:

\begin{split}(-1)^{n}\operatorname{sgn}(\nu_{f,g})d_{n,n}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{f}^{X}(x)\right)\otimes d_{n,n}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\left(\lambda_{g}^{Y}(y)\right)&=(-1)^{n}(-1)^{l}\operatorname{sgn}\left(\nu_{\overline{f},g\delta_{n,n}}\right)(-1)^{k}\lambda_{\overline{f}}^{X}\left(\partial_{k}^{X}(x)\right)\otimes\lambda_{g\delta_{n,n}}^{Y}(y)\\
&=\operatorname{sgn}\left(\nu_{\overline{f},g\delta_{n,n}}\right)\lambda_{\overline{f},g\delta_{n,n}}^{X,Y}\left(\partial_{k}^{X}(x)\otimes y\right)\end{split}

Case II:
f\delta_{n,i}
is surjective but
g\delta_{n,i}
is not surjective

If
0\leq i\leq n-1
, then by Corollary
4.12
,
d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\left(\lambda_{g}^{Y}(y)\right)=0
, so
d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{f}^{X}(x)\right)\otimes d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\left(\lambda_{g}^{Y}(y)\right)=0
. Hence we can assume that
i=n
. In this case, we have
f\delta_{n,n}=f\mid_{[n-1]}:[n-1]\rightarrow[k]
. Moreover, by Corollary
4.12
,
g
induces a surjective map
\overline{g}\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n-1],[l-1]\right)
given by
\overline{g}(i)=g(i)
for every
i\in[n-1]
. If
0\leq j_{1}<\cdots<j_{l}<n
are the elements of
[n]
for which
f(j)=f(j+1)
, and
0\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}<n
are the elements of
[n]
for which
g(i)=g(i+1)
, then we have:

\nu_{f,g}=\begin{pmatrix}1&\cdots&k&k+1&\cdots&n\\
i_{1}+1&\cdots&i_{k}+1&j_{1}+1&\cdots&j_{l}+1\end{pmatrix}

Set:

A=\left\{(i_{\alpha}+1,j_{\beta}+1)\mid 1\leq\alpha\leq k\textrm{, }1\leq\beta\leq l\textrm{, and }i_{\alpha}>j_{\beta}\right\}

If
|A|=r
, then
\operatorname{sgn}(\nu_{f,g})=(-1)^{r}
. As
f
and
f\delta_{n,n}
are both surjective, we notice that
j_{l}=n-1
as
f(n-1)=k=f(n)
. Also, since
g
is surjective but
g\delta_{n,n}
is not surjective, we see that
i_{k}<n-1
since otherwise,
g(n-1)=g(n)=l
which would imply that
g\delta_{n,n}=g\mid_{[n-1]}:[n-1]\rightarrow[l]
is surjective. As a result,
0\leq j_{1}<\cdots<j_{l-1}<n-1
are the elements of
[n-1]
for which
(f\delta_{n,n})(j)=(f\delta_{n,n})(j+1)
, and
0\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}<n-1
are the elements of
[n-1]
for which
\overline{g}(i)=\overline{g}(i+1)
, so we have:

\nu_{f\delta_{n,n},\overline{g}}=\begin{pmatrix}1&\cdots&k&k+1&\cdots&n-1\\
i_{1}+1&\cdots&i_{k}+1&j_{1}+1&\cdots&j_{l-1}+1\end{pmatrix}

Set:

B=\left\{(i_{\alpha}+1,j_{\beta}+1)\mid 1\leq\alpha\leq k\textrm{, }1\leq\beta\leq l-1\textrm{, and }i_{\alpha}>j_{\beta}\right\}

If
|B|=s
, then
\operatorname{sgn}\left(\nu_{f\delta_{n,n},\overline{g}}\right)=(-1)^{s}
. Since
j_{l}=n-1>i_{k}>\cdots>i_{1}
, we deduce that
A=B
, which in turn implies that
r=s
. Therefore, we have
\operatorname{sgn}(\nu_{f,g})=(-1)^{r}=(-1)^{s}=\operatorname{sgn}\left(\nu_{f\delta_{n,n},\overline{g}}\right)
. Thus using Corollary
4.12
, we get:

\begin{split}(-1)^{n}\operatorname{sgn}(\nu_{f,g})d_{n,n}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{f}^{X}(x)\right)\otimes d_{n,n}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\left(\lambda_{g}^{Y}(y)\right)&=(-1)^{n}\operatorname{sgn}\left(\nu_{f\delta_{n,n},\overline{g}}\right)\lambda_{f\delta_{n,n}}^{X}(x)\otimes(-1)^{l}\lambda_{\overline{g}}^{Y}\left(\partial_{l}^{Y}(y)\right)\\
&=(-1)^{k}\operatorname{sgn}\left(\nu_{f\delta_{n,n},\overline{g}}\right)\lambda_{f\delta_{n,n},\overline{g}}^{X,Y}\left(x\otimes\partial_{l}^{Y}(y)\right)\end{split}

Case III:
f\delta_{n,i}
and
g\delta_{n,i}
are both surjective

Let
0\leq j_{1}<\cdots<j_{l}<n
be the elements of
[n]
for which
f(j)=f(j+1)
, and
0\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}<n
the elements of
[n]
for which
g(i)=g(i+1)
. If
i=0
, then
f\delta_{n,0}
is surjective which means that if
0
is omitted from the domain of
f
, then it will remain surjective. This implies that
f(0)=0=f(1)
, so
j_{1}=0
. Similarly, we see that
i_{1}=0
. This is a contradiction as
\{i_{1},...,i_{k}\}\cap\{j_{1},...,j_{l}\}=\emptyset
. If
i=n
, then
f\delta_{n,n}
is surjective which means that if
n
is omitted from the domain of
f
, then it will remain surjective. This yields that
f(n-1)=k=f(n)
, so
j_{l}=n-1
. Similarly, we see that
i_{k}=n-1
. This is a contradiction as
\{i_{1},...,i_{k}\}\cap\{j_{1},...,j_{l}\}=\emptyset
. Hence we can assume that
1\leq i\leq n-1
. By Corollary
4.12
, we have:

\operatorname{sgn}(\nu_{f,g})d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{f}^{X}(x)\right)\otimes d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\left(\lambda_{g}^{Y}(y)\right)=\operatorname{sgn}(\nu_{f,g})\lambda_{f\delta_{n,i}}^{X}(x)\otimes\lambda_{g\delta_{n,i}}^{Y}(y)=\operatorname{sgn}(\nu_{f,g})\lambda_{f\delta_{n,i},g\delta_{n,i}}^{X,Y}(x\otimes y)

Since
f\delta_{n,i}
is surjective, we observe that if
i
is omitted from the domain of
f
, then it will remain surjective. This means that
f(i-1)=f(i)
or
f(i)=f(i+1)
, so
i-1\in\{j_{1},...,j_{l}\}
or
i\in\{j_{1},...,j_{l}\}
. Similarly, we have
i-1\in\{i_{1},...,i_{k}\}
or
i\in\{i_{1},...,i_{k}\}
. Since
\{i_{1},...,i_{k}\}\cap\{j_{1},...,j_{l}\}=\emptyset
, we should have either
i-1\in\{j_{1},...,j_{l}\}
and
i\in\{i_{1},...,i_{k}\}
, or
i\in\{j_{1},...,j_{l}\}
and
i-1\in\{i_{1},...,i_{k}\}
. Without loss of generality, suppose that
i-1\in\{j_{1},...,j_{l}\}
and
i\in\{i_{1},...,i_{k}\}
. Define a map
f^{\prime}:[n]\rightarrow[k]
by setting
f^{\prime}(t)=f(t)
for every
t\neq i
and
f^{\prime}(i)=f(i+1)
. Since
f^{\prime}(i-1)=f(i-1)=f(i)<f(i+1)=f^{\prime}(i)=f^{\prime}(i+1)
, it follows that
f^{\prime}
is order-preserving. That is,
f^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[k]\right)
. Moreover,
f^{\prime}(i-1)=f(i-1)=f(i)
, so
f(i)\in\operatorname{Im}(f^{\prime})
, whence we see that
f^{\prime}
is surjective. Also, since
f
and
f^{\prime}
have the same values except at
i
, we have
f\delta_{n,i}=f^{\prime}\delta_{n,i}
. Similarly, define a map
g^{\prime}:[n]\rightarrow[l]
by setting
g^{\prime}(t)=g(t)
for every
t\neq i
and
g^{\prime}(i)=g(i-1)
. Since
g^{\prime}(i-1)=g^{\prime}(i)=g(i-1)<g(i)=g(i+1)=g^{\prime}(i+1)
, it follows that
g^{\prime}
is order-preserving. That is,
g^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([n],[l]\right)
. Moreover,
g^{\prime}(i+1)=g(i+1)=g(i)
, so
g(i)\in\operatorname{Im}(g^{\prime})
, thereby we notice that
g^{\prime}
is surjective. Also, since
g
and
g^{\prime}
have the same values except at
i
, we have
g\delta_{n,i}=g^{\prime}\delta_{n,i}
. Now if
(f^{\prime},g^{\prime})\notin\mathcal{S}_{n}^{k,l}
, then
\phi_{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}}
is not injective, so there is an
0\leq m\leq n-1
such that
\phi_{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}}(m)=\phi_{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}}(m+1)
. But we note that:

\phi_{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}}(m)=\left(f^{\prime}(m),g^{\prime}(m)\right)=\begin{cases}\left(f(m),g(m)\right)=\phi_{f,g}(m)&\textrm{if }m<i-1\\
\left(f(m),g(m)\right)&\textrm{if }m=i-1\\
\left(f(m+1),g(m-1)\right)&\textrm{if }m=i\\
\left(f(m),g(m)\right)=\phi_{f,g}(m)&\textrm{if }m>i\end{cases}

and

\phi_{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}}(m+1)=\left(f^{\prime}(m+1),g^{\prime}(m+1)\right)=\begin{cases}\left(f(m+1),g(m+1)\right)=\phi_{f,g}(m+1)&\textrm{if }m<i-1\\
\left(f(m+2),g(m)\right)&\textrm{if }m=i-1\\
\left(f(m+1),g(m+1)\right)&\textrm{if }m=i\\
\left(f(m+1),g(m+1)\right)=\phi_{f,g}(m+1)&\textrm{if }m>i\end{cases}

Taking into account that
\phi_{f,g}
is injective, we notice that in any case,
\phi_{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}}(m)\neq\phi_{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}}(m+1)
which is a contradiction. Hence
(f^{\prime},g^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{k,l}
. We finally have:

\nu_{f,g}=\begin{pmatrix}1&\cdots&p&\cdots&k&k+1&\cdots&q&\cdots&n\\
i_{1}+1&\cdots&i&\cdots&i_{k}+1&j_{1}+1&\cdots&i-1&\cdots&j_{l}+1\end{pmatrix}

Furthermore, we have:

\nu_{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}}=\begin{pmatrix}1&\cdots&p&\cdots&k&k+1&\cdots&q&\cdots&n\\
i_{1}+1&\cdots&i-1&\cdots&i_{k}+1&j_{1}+1&\cdots&i&\cdots&j_{l}+1\end{pmatrix}

It is clear from the above displays that
\operatorname{sgn}(\nu_{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}})=-\operatorname{sgn}(\nu_{f,g})
. As a result, we have:

\operatorname{sgn}(\nu_{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}})d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{f^{\prime}}^{X}(x)\right)\otimes d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\left(\lambda_{g^{\prime}}^{Y}(y)\right)=\operatorname{sgn}(\nu_{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}})\lambda_{f^{\prime}\delta_{n,i}}^{X}(x)\otimes\lambda_{g^{\prime}\delta_{n,i}}^{Y}(y)=\operatorname{sgn}(\nu_{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}})\lambda_{f^{\prime}\delta_{n,i},g^{\prime}\delta_{n,i}}^{X,Y}(x\otimes y)=

-sgn(\nu_{f,g})\lambda_{f\delta_{n,i},g\delta_{n,i}}^{X,Y}(x\otimes y)

Consequently, fixing
1\leq i\leq n-1
, for any pair
(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{k,l}
, there is a dual pair
(f^{\prime},g^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{k,l}
whose corresponding term cancels that of
(f,g)
. Therefore, the sum of the terms arising in this way vanishes.

Case IV:
f\delta_{n,i}
and
g\delta_{n,i}
are neither surjective

Let
0\leq j_{1}<\cdots<j_{l}<n
be the elements of
[n]
for which
f(j)=f(j+1)
, and
0\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}<n
the elements of
[n]
for which
g(i)=g(i+1)
. Since
f\delta_{n,i}
is not surjective, we conclude that if
i
is omitted from the domain of
f
, then it will no longer remain surjective. This implies that
f(i)
is not assumed by any element of
[n]
other than
i
. If
i=0
, then
f(0)\neq f(1)
, so
0\notin\{j_{1},...,j_{l}\}
. Similarly,
0\notin\{i_{1},...,i_{k}\}
. But then
\{i_{1},...,i_{k},j_{1},...,j_{l}\}\subseteq\{0,1,...,n-1\}\setminus\{0\}
and
\{i_{1},...,i_{k}\}\cap\{j_{1},...,j_{l}\}=\emptyset
, so
n=k+l\leq n-1
which is a contradiction. If
1\leq i\leq n-1
, then
f(i-1)\neq f(i)\neq f(i+1)
, so
i-1,i\notin\{j_{1},...,j_{l}\}
. Similarly,
i-1,i\notin\{i_{1},...,i_{k}\}
. But then
\{i_{1},...,i_{k},j_{1},...,j_{l}\}\subseteq\{0,1,...,n-1\}\setminus\{i-1,i\}
, so
n=k+l\leq n-2
which is a contradiction. If
i=n
, then
f(n-1)\neq f(n)
, so
n-1\notin\{j_{1},...,j_{l}\}
. Similarly,
n-1\notin\{i_{1},...,i_{k}\}
. But then
\{i_{1},...,i_{k},j_{1},...,j_{l}\}\subseteq\{0,1,...,n-1\}\setminus\{n-1\}
, so
n=k+l\leq n-1
which is a contradiction. Therefore, this case never happens.

Now in light of the above analysis, we have:

\textstyle\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}\textstyle\sum_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{k,l}}\operatorname{sgn}(\nu_{f,g})d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(X)}\left(\lambda_{f}^{X}(x)\right)\otimes d_{n,i}^{\operatorname{DK}(Y)}\left(\lambda_{g}^{Y}(y)\right)=

\textstyle\sum_{\left(\overline{f},g\delta_{n,n}\right)\in\mathcal{S}_{n-1}^{k-1,l}}\operatorname{sgn}\left(\nu_{\overline{f},g\delta_{n,n}}\right)\lambda_{\overline{f},g\delta_{n,n}}^{X,Y}\left(\partial_{k}^{X}(x)\otimes y\right)+(-1)^{k}\textstyle\sum_{\left(f\delta_{n,n},\overline{g}\right)\in\mathcal{S}_{n-1}^{k,l-1}}\operatorname{sgn}\left(\nu_{f\delta_{n,n},\overline{g}}\right)\lambda_{f\delta_{n,n},\overline{g}}^{X,Y}\left(x\otimes\partial_{l}^{Y}(y)\right)

On the other hand, we have:

\begin{split}\nabla_{n-1}^{X,Y}\left(\partial_{n}^{X\otimes_{R}Y}\left(\iota_{k,l}^{X,Y}(x\otimes y)\right)\right)&=\nabla_{n-1}^{X,Y}\left(\iota_{k-1,l}^{X,Y}\left(\partial_{k}^{X}(x)\otimes y\right)\right)+(-1)^{k}\nabla_{n-1}^{X,Y}\left(\iota_{k,l-1}^{X,Y}\left(x\otimes\partial_{l}^{Y}(y)\right)\right)\\
&=\textstyle\sum_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{n-1}^{k-1,l}}\operatorname{sgn}(\nu_{f,g})\lambda_{f,g}^{X,Y}\left(\partial_{k}^{X}(x)\otimes y\right)+(-1)^{k}\textstyle\sum_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{n-1}^{k,l-1}}\operatorname{sgn}(\nu_{f,g})\lambda_{f,g}^{X,Y}\left(x\otimes\partial_{l}^{Y}(y)\right)\end{split}

After reindexing properly, we observe that
\partial_{n}^{X\boxtimes_{R}Y}\nabla_{n}^{X,Y}\iota_{k,l}^{X,Y}=\nabla_{n-1}^{X,Y}\partial_{n}^{X\otimes_{R}Y}\iota_{k,l}^{X,Y}
for every
k,l\geq 0
with
k+l=n
, so we infer that
\partial_{n}^{X\boxtimes_{R}Y}\nabla_{n}^{X,Y}=\nabla_{n-1}^{X,Y}\partial_{n}^{X\otimes_{R}Y}
. As a consequence,
\nabla^{X,Y}:X\otimes_{R}Y\rightarrow X\boxtimes_{R}Y
is a morphism of
\mathbb{Z}
-complexes.

We next establish the naturality of
\nabla^{X,Y}
. To this end, let
\eta:X\rightarrow X^{\prime}
be a morphism of
R^{\operatorname{op}}
-complexes. Then the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes_{R}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}\otimes_{R}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\boxtimes_{R}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}\boxtimes_{R}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta\otimes_{R}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla^{X,Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla^{X^{\prime},Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta\boxtimes_{R}Y}

Indeed, we have for every
x\in X_{k}
and
y\in Y_{l}
with
k+l=n
:

\begin{split}(\eta\boxtimes_{R}Y)_{n}\left(\nabla_{n}^{X,Y}\left(\iota_{k,l}^{X,Y}(x\otimes y)\right)\right)&=(\eta\boxtimes_{R}Y)_{n}\left(\textstyle\sum_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{k,l}}\operatorname{sgn}(\nu_{f,g})\lambda_{f,g}^{X,Y}(x\otimes y)\right)\\
&=\textstyle\sum_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{k,l}}\operatorname{sgn}(\nu_{f,g})(\eta\boxtimes_{R}Y)_{n}\left(\lambda_{f,g}^{X,Y}(x\otimes y)\right)\\
&=\textstyle\sum_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{k,l}}\operatorname{sgn}(\nu_{f,g})\lambda_{f,g}^{X^{\prime},Y}\left(\eta_{k}(x)\otimes y\right)\\
&=\nabla_{n}^{X^{\prime},Y}\left(\iota_{k,l}^{X^{\prime},Y}\left(\eta_{k}(x)\otimes y\right)\right)\\
&=\nabla_{n}^{X^{\prime},Y}\left((\eta\otimes_{R}Y)_{n}\left(\iota_{k,l}^{X,Y}(x\otimes y)\right)\right)\end{split}

As a result,
(\eta\boxtimes_{R}Y)_{n}\nabla_{n}^{X,Y}\iota_{k,l}^{X,Y}=\nabla_{n}^{X^{\prime},Y}(\eta\otimes_{R}Y)_{n}\iota_{k,l}^{X,Y}
for every
k,l\geq 0
with
k+l=n
, so we infer that
(\eta\boxtimes_{R}Y)_{n}\nabla_{n}^{X,Y}=\nabla_{n}^{X^{\prime},Y}(\eta\otimes_{R}Y)_{n}
for every
n\geq 0
, whence
(\eta\boxtimes_{R}Y)\nabla^{X,Y}=\nabla^{X^{\prime},Y}(\eta\otimes_{R}Y)
. If
\theta:Y\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
is a morphism of
R
-complexes, then a similar argument shows that the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes_{R}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes_{R}Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\boxtimes_{R}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\boxtimes_{R}Y^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes_{R}\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla^{X,Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla^{X,Y^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\boxtimes_{R}\theta}

As a consequence,
\nabla^{X,Y}
is natural in
X
and
Y
.

We finally show that
\nabla^{X,Y}
is a quasi-isomorphism. The author has learned the ideas of the following delicate argument from
[
Lu
, proof of Theorem 2.5.7.14]
. We first consider the special case in which
X
and
Y
are left-bounded. Thus we assume that
X_{i}=0
for every
i>n
, and
Y_{i}=0
for every
i>m
. We argue by induction on
n
to show that
\nabla^{X,Y}
is a quasi-isomorphism. If
n=0
, then
X
has only one non-zero term in degree
0
, so we can identify
X
with the left
R
-module
X_{0}
. Consequently,
X\otimes_{R}Y=X\boxtimes_{R}Y
by Proposition
5.5
, and
\nabla_{i}^{X,Y}:(X\otimes_{R}Y)_{i}\rightarrow(X\boxtimes_{R}Y)_{i}
is the identity map for every
i\geq 0
. Therefore,
\nabla^{X,Y}
is a quasi-isomorphism. Now let
n\geq 1
, and assume the result for
n-1
. Consider the degreewise split short exact sequence

0\rightarrow X_{n-1\sqsubset}\rightarrow X\rightarrow\Sigma^{n}X_{n}\rightarrow 0

of
R
-complexes in which
\sqsubset
designates the hard truncation from left; see
[
Ch
, A.1.14]
. Then we obtain the following commutative diagram with exact rows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{n-1\sqsubset}\otimes_{R}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes_{R}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{n}X_{n}\otimes_{R}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{n-1\sqsubset}\boxtimes_{R}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\boxtimes_{R}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{n}X_{n}\boxtimes_{R}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla^{X_{n-1\sqsubset},Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla^{X,Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla^{\Sigma^{n}X_{n},Y}}

By the induction hypothesis,
\nabla^{X_{n-1\sqsubset},Y}
is a quasi-isomorphism. Therefore, the above diagram implies that
\nabla^{X,Y}
is a quasi-isomorphism if and only if
\nabla^{\Sigma^{n}X_{n},Y}
is a quasi-isomorphism. We note that
X_{n}\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right)
is a left-bounded
R
-complex with

\left(X_{n}\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right)\right)_{n}=X_{n}\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right)_{n}=X_{n}\otimes_{R}R\cong X_{n},

so by the above argument,
\nabla^{X_{n}\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right),Y}
is a quasi-isomorphism if and only if
\nabla^{\Sigma^{n}X_{n},Y}
is a quasi-isomorphism. It follows that
\nabla^{X,Y}
is a quasi-isomorphism if and only if
\nabla^{X_{n}\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right),Y}
is a quasi-isomorphism. Now we argue by induction on
m
to show that
\nabla^{X_{n}\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right),Y}
is a quasi-isomorphism. If
m=0
, then
Y
has only one non-zero term in degree
0
, so we can identify
Y
with the left
R
-module
Y_{0}
, and thus a similar argument as above shows that
\nabla^{X_{n}\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right),Y}
is a quasi-isomorphism. Let
m\geq 1
, and assume the result for
m-1
. Consider the degreewise split short exact sequence

0\rightarrow Y_{m-1\sqsubset}\rightarrow Y\rightarrow\Sigma^{m}Y_{m}\rightarrow 0

of
R
-complexes. Then we achieve the following commutative diagram with exact rows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(X_{n}\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right)\right)\otimes_{R}Y_{m-1\sqsubset}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(X_{n}\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right)\right)\otimes_{R}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(X_{n}\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right)\right)\otimes_{R}\Sigma^{m}Y_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(X_{n}\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right)\right)\boxtimes_{R}Y_{m-1\sqsubset}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(X_{n}\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right)\right)\boxtimes_{R}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(X_{n}\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right)\right)\boxtimes_{R}\Sigma^{m}Y_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla^{X_{n}\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right),Y_{m-1\sqsubset}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla^{X_{n}\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right),Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla^{X_{n}\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right),\Sigma^{m}Y_{m}}}

By the induction hypothesis,
\nabla^{X_{n}\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right),Y_{m-1\sqsubset}}
is a quasi-isomorphism. Therefore, the above diagram implies that
\nabla^{X_{n}\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right),Y}
is a quasi-isomorphism if and only if
\nabla^{X_{n}\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right),\Sigma^{m}Y_{m}}
is a quasi-isomorphism. We note that
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{m})}\right)\otimes_{R}Y_{m}
is a left-bounded
R
-complex with

\left(\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{m})}\right)\otimes_{R}Y_{m}\right)_{m}=\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{m})}\right)_{m}\otimes_{R}Y_{m}=R\otimes_{R}Y_{m}\cong Y_{m},

so by the above argument,
\nabla^{X_{n}\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right),\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{m})}\right)\otimes_{R}Y_{m}}
is a quasi-isomorphism if and only if
\nabla^{X_{n}\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right),\Sigma^{m}Y_{m}}
is a quasi-isomorphism. It follows that
\nabla^{X_{n}\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right),Y}
is a quasi-isomorphism if and only if
\nabla^{X_{n}\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right),\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{m})}\right)\otimes_{R}Y_{m}}
is a quasi-isomorphism. Altogether, we infer that
\nabla^{X,Y}
is a quasi-isomorphism if and only if
\nabla^{X_{n}\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right),\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{m})}\right)\otimes_{R}Y_{m}}
is a quasi-isomorphism.

Now let
n,m\geq 0
. Viewing
[n]
as a small category, we have
\operatorname{nrv}([n])=\Delta^{n}
. Since
[n]
has a least element
0
, we can define a morphism
\zeta^{\Delta^{n}}:\Delta^{0}\rightarrow\Delta^{n}
of simplicial sets by setting
\zeta_{k}^{\Delta^{n}}(0,...,0)=(0,...,0)
for every
k\geq 0
. Then by Lemma
4.8
,
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{\left(\zeta^{\Delta^{n}}\right)}\right):\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{0})}\right)\rightarrow\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right)
is a homotopy equivalence. Similarly,
\operatorname{nrv}([m])=\Delta^{m}
,
\zeta^{\Delta^{m}}:\Delta^{0}\rightarrow\Delta^{m}
is given by
\zeta_{k}^{\Delta^{m}}(0,...,0)=(0,...,0)
for every
k\geq 0
, and
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{\left(\zeta^{\Delta^{m}}\right)}\right):\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{0})}\right)\rightarrow\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{m})}\right)
is a homotopy equivalence. Also,
[n]\times[m]
is a poset with componentwise order, so
\operatorname{nrv}([n]\times[m])=\Delta^{n}\times\Delta^{m}
,
\zeta^{\Delta^{n}\times\Delta^{m}}:\Delta^{0}\rightarrow\Delta^{n}\times\Delta^{m}
is given by
\zeta_{k}^{\Delta^{n}\times\Delta^{m}}(0,...,0)=\left((0,...,0),(0,...,0)\right)
for every
k\geq 0
, and
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{\left(\zeta^{\Delta^{n}\times\Delta^{m}}\right)}\right):\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{0})}\right)\rightarrow\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n}\times\Delta^{m})}\right)
is a homotopy equivalence. Let

\eta:\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{0})}\right)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{0})}\right)\rightarrow\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{0})}\right)

be the isomorphism given by
\eta_{0}(a\otimes b)=ab
for every
a,b\in R
, and
\eta_{i}=0
for every
i\neq 0
. Furthermore, let
\chi
be the composition of the following morphisms of
\mathbb{Z}
-complexes:

\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{m})}\right)\xrightarrow{\nabla^{\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right),\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{m})}\right)}}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right)\boxtimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{m})}\right)

\xrightarrow{\cong}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\otimes_{R}R^{(\Delta^{m})}\right)\xrightarrow{\cong}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n}\times\Delta^{m})}\right)

Then the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{0})}\right)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{0})}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{0})}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{m})}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n}\times\Delta^{m})}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{\left(\zeta^{\Delta^{n}}\right)}\right)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{\left(\zeta^{\Delta^{m}}\right)}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{\left(\zeta^{\Delta^{n}\times\Delta^{m}}\right)}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}

To see this, we note that
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{0})}\right)
has only one non-zero term in degree
0
, so we must have
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{\left(\zeta^{\Delta^{n}}\right)}\right)_{i}=\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{\left(\zeta^{\Delta^{m}}\right)}\right)_{i}=\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{\left(\zeta^{\Delta^{n}\times\Delta^{m}}\right)}\right)_{i}=0
for every
i\neq 0
. Therefore, it suffices to check the commutativity of the above diagram for
i=0
. If
i=0
, then we have the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\otimes_{R}R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{n}\otimes_{R}R^{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{nm}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{\left(\zeta_{0}^{\Delta^{n}}\right)}\otimes_{R}R^{\left(\zeta_{0}^{\Delta^{m}}\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{\left(\zeta_{0}^{\Delta^{n}\times\Delta^{m}}\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{0}}

Indeed, we have for every
a,b\in R
:

\begin{split}\chi_{0}\left(\left(R^{\left(\zeta_{0}^{\Delta^{n}}\right)}\otimes_{R}R^{\left(\zeta_{0}^{\Delta^{m}}\right)}\right)(a\otimes b)\right)&=\chi_{0}\left(R^{\left(\zeta_{0}^{\Delta^{n}}\right)}(a)\otimes R^{\left(\zeta_{0}^{\Delta^{m}}\right)}(b)\right)=\chi_{0}\left((a,0,...,0)\otimes(b,0,...,0)\right)\\
&=(ab,0,...,0)=R^{\left(\zeta_{0}^{\Delta^{n}\times\Delta^{m}}\right)}(ab)=R^{\left(\zeta_{0}^{\Delta^{n}\times\Delta^{m}}\right)}\left(\eta_{0}(a\otimes b)\right)\end{split}

Now
\eta
is an isomorphism, and
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{\left(\zeta^{\Delta^{n}}\right)}\right)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{\left(\zeta^{\Delta^{m}}\right)}\right)
and
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{\left(\zeta^{\Delta^{n}\times\Delta^{m}}\right)}\right)
are homotopy equivalences, so the above diagram implies that
\chi
is a homotopy equivalence. By the definition of
\chi
, we conclude that
\nabla^{\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right),\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{m})}\right)}
is a homotopy equivalence. Consider the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{n}\otimes_{R}\left(\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right)\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{m})}\right)\right)\otimes_{R}Y_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(X_{n}\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right)\right)\otimes_{R}\left(\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{m})}\right)\otimes_{R}Y_{m}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{n}\otimes_{R}\left(\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right)\boxtimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{m})}\right)\right)\otimes_{R}Y_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(X_{n}\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right)\right)\boxtimes_{R}\left(\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{m})}\right)\otimes_{R}Y_{m}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{n}\otimes_{R}\nabla^{\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right),\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{m})}\right)}\otimes_{R}Y_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla^{X_{n}\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right),\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{m})}\right)\otimes_{R}Y_{m}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Since tensor product functor preserves homotopy equivalences,
X_{n}\otimes_{R}\nabla^{\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right),\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{m})}\right)}\otimes_{R}Y_{m}
is a homotopy equivalence, so we deduce from the above diagram that
\nabla^{X_{n}\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{n})}\right),\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{m})}\right)\otimes_{R}Y_{m}}
is a homotopy equivalence, so in particular, it is a quasi-isomorphism. Therefore, the above discussion shows that
\nabla^{X,Y}
is a quasi-isomorphism.

Next suppose that
X
and
Y
are arbitrary. Then we have the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{(n,m)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}}{\varinjlim}\left(X_{n\sqsubset}\otimes_{R}Y_{m\sqsubset}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{(n,m)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}}{\varinjlim}\left(X_{n\sqsubset}\boxtimes_{R}Y_{m\sqsubset}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{Z}}{\varinjlim}X_{n\sqsubset}\right)\otimes_{R}\left(\underset{m\in\mathbb{Z}}{\varinjlim}Y_{m\sqsubset}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\underset{n\in\mathbb{Z}}{\varinjlim}X_{n\sqsubset}\right)\boxtimes_{R}\left(\underset{m\in\mathbb{Z}}{\varinjlim}Y_{m\sqsubset}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes_{R}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\boxtimes_{R}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{(n,m)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}}{\varinjlim}\nabla^{X_{n\sqsubset}\otimes_{R}Y_{m\sqsubset}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla^{\underset{n\in\mathbb{Z}}{\varinjlim}X_{n\sqsubset},\underset{m\in\mathbb{Z}}{\varinjlim}Y_{m\sqsubset}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla^{X,Y}}

By the special case,
\nabla^{X_{n\sqsubset}\otimes_{R}Y_{m\sqsubset}}
is a quasi-isomorphism for every
n,m\geq 0
, so
\underset{(n,m)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}}{\varinjlim}\nabla^{X_{n\sqsubset}\otimes_{R}Y_{m\sqsubset}}
is a quasi-isomorphism. Then the above diagram shows that
\nabla^{X,Y}
is a quasi-isomorphism.
∎

Corollary 5.10
.

Let
R
be a ring,
X
a connective
R^{\operatorname{op}}
-complex, and
Y
a connective
R
-complex. Then the following natural
\mathbb{Z}
-isomorphism holds for every
i\geq 0
:

H_{i}(X\boxtimes_{R}Y)\cong H_{i}(X\otimes_{R}Y)

Proof.

Follows from Theorem
5.9
.
∎

Corollary 5.11
.

Let
R
be a ring. Then the following assertions hold:

(i)

If
X
is a connective
R^{\operatorname{op}}
-complex of flat modules, then
X\boxtimes_{R}-:\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(\mathbb{Z})
preserves quasi-isomorphisms.

(ii)

If
Y
is a connective
R
-complex of flat modules, then
-\boxtimes_{R}Y:\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R^{\operatorname{op}})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(\mathbb{Z})
preserves quasi-isomorphisms.

Proof.

(i): Since
X
is a right-bounded
R^{\operatorname{op}}
-complex of flat modules,
[
CT
, Corollary A.2]
or
[
CFH
, Example 5.4.8]
implies that
X\otimes_{R}-:\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(\mathbb{Z})
preserves acyclicity, so by
[
AF
, Examples 1.1.F, (3)]
, it also preserves quasi-isomorphisms. On the other hand, by Theorem
5.9
, there is a natural quasi-isomorphism
\nabla^{X,Y}:X\otimes_{R}Y\rightarrow X\boxtimes_{R}Y
of
\mathbb{Z}
-complexes. Let
g:Y\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
be a quasi-isomorphism of
R
-complexes. Consider the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes_{R}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes_{R}Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\boxtimes_{R}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\boxtimes_{R}Y^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\otimes_{R}g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla^{X,Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla^{X,Y^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\boxtimes_{R}g}

As
X\otimes_{R}g
,
\nabla^{X,Y}
, and
\nabla^{X,Y^{\prime}}
are quasi-isomorphisms, the above diagram implies that
X\boxtimes_{R}g
is a quasi-isomorphism.

(ii): Similar to (i).
∎

6.
Model Structure on Simplicial Commutative Algebras

In this section, we deploy Theorem
2.9
to transfer the model structure on connective chain complexes to simplicial commutative algebras. In order to overcome the acyclicity condition, we need to construct certain cylinder and path objects for simplicial commutative algebras. To this end, we need Lemmas
6.1
and
6.3
below. Variants of these lemmas are incorporated as Axiom SM7 into Quillen’s theory of model categories to define a simplicially enriched model category; see
[
Qu2
, Definition 2 on page 2.2]
or
[
GJ
, Proposition 11.5 and Axiom 3.1]
. He proves that the category of simplicial sets satisfy these lemmas. However, the proofs that are available in the literature are based on the model structure of simplicial sets which we have totally avoided in this article. Instead, we leverage the shuffle product of connective chain complexes developed in the previous section to prove these lemmas for simplicial modules.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring,
\rho:M\rightarrow N
a fibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
, and
\iota:U\rightarrow V
a morphism of simplicial sets. Consider the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,N)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,N)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,\rho)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(\iota,M)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(\iota,N)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,\rho)}

and form the following pullback diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,M)\mathop{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\textstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}}_{\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,N)}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,N)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,N)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,N)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(\iota,M)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,\rho)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(\iota,N)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,\rho)}

If
R^{(\iota)}:R^{(U)}\rightarrow R^{(V)}
is a cofibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
, then
\eta
is a fibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
which is trivial if either
\rho
or
R^{(\iota)}
is.

Proof.

Let
\varrho:K\rightarrow L
be a morphism of simplicial
R
-modules. Consider a commutative diagram as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,M)\mathop{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\textstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}}_{\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,N)}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,N)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

Considering the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{(V)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{(V)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho^{(U)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{(\iota)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{(\iota)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho^{(V)}}

there is a unique morphism
\zeta:K^{(V)}\bigsqcup_{K^{(U)}}L^{(U)}\rightarrow L^{(V)}
that makes the following pushout diagram commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{(V)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{(V)}\bigsqcup_{K^{(U)}}L^{(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{(V)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho^{(U)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{(\iota)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{(\iota)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho^{(V)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}

On the other hand, considering the natural bijections

\phi_{KVM}:\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)}\left(K,\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,M)\right)\rightarrow\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)}\left(K^{(V)},M\right)

and

\phi_{LUM}:\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)}\left(L,\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,M)\right)\rightarrow\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)}\left(L^{(U)},M\right)

from Remark
4.4
, we get the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{(V)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho^{(U)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{(\iota)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{LUM}(sq)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{KVM}(p)}

Hence there is a unique morphism
\xi:K^{(V)}\bigsqcup_{K^{(U)}}L^{(U)}\rightarrow M
that makes the following pushout diagram commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{(V)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{(V)}\bigsqcup_{K^{(U)}}L^{(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho^{(U)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{(\iota)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{LUM}(sq)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{KVM}(p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}

Then considering the natural bijection

\phi_{LVN}:\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)}\left(L,\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,N)\right)\rightarrow\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)}\left(L^{(V)},N\right)

we get the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{(V)}\bigsqcup_{K^{(U)}}L^{(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{(V)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{LVN}(rq)}

If
\tau:L^{(V)}\rightarrow M
is a morphism of simplicial
R
-modules that makes the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{(V)}\bigsqcup_{K^{(U)}}L^{(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{(V)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{LVN}(rq)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}

commutative, then considering the natural bijection

\phi_{LVM}:\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)}\left(L,\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,M)\right)\rightarrow\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)}\left(L^{(V)},M\right)

we see that the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,M)\mathop{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\textstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}}_{\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,N)}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,N)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{LVM}^{-1}(\tau)}

Therefore, in order to show that
\eta
has the right lifting property against
\varrho
, it suffices to show that
\rho
has the right lifting property against
\zeta
. Indeed, if
\rho
is a fibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
, then it suffices to show that
\zeta
is a trivial cofibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
, and if
\rho
is a trivial fibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
, then it suffices to show that
\zeta
is a cofibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
.

Denote the morphism
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\iota)}\right):\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(U)}\right)\rightarrow\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(V)}\right)
in
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
by
\mu:Z\rightarrow W
for convenience. If
X
is an
R
-complex and
T
is a simplicial set, then in view of Remark
4.4
, Corollary
5.7
, and Theorem
4.14
, we have the following natural isomorphisms:

\operatorname{Nor}\left(\operatorname{DK}(X)^{(T)}\right)\cong\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(T)}\otimes_{R}\operatorname{DK}(X)\right)\cong\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(T)}\right)\boxtimes_{R}\operatorname{Nor}\left(\operatorname{DK}(X)\right)\cong\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(T)}\right)\boxtimes_{R}X

As a consequence, assuming that
\varrho:K\rightarrow L
is of the form
\operatorname{DK}(h):\operatorname{DK}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{DK}(Y)
for some morphism
h:X\rightarrow Y
of connective
R
-complexes, then we get a commutative diagram as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Nor}\left(K^{(V)}\bigsqcup_{K^{(U)}}L^{(U)}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Nor}\left(L^{(V)}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Nor}\left(K^{(V)}\right)\bigsqcup_{\operatorname{Nor}\left(K^{(U)}\right)}\operatorname{Nor}\left(L^{(U)}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Nor}\left(L^{(V)}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Nor}\left(\operatorname{DK}(X)^{(V)}\right)\bigsqcup_{\operatorname{Nor}\left(\operatorname{DK}(X)^{(U)}\right)}\operatorname{Nor}\left(\operatorname{DK}(Y)^{(U)}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Nor}\left(\operatorname{DK}(Y)^{(V)}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(V)}\right)\boxtimes_{R}X\right)\bigsqcup_{\left(\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(U)}\right)\boxtimes_{R}X\right)}\left(\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(U)}\right)\boxtimes_{R}Y\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(V)}\right)\boxtimes_{R}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(W\boxtimes_{R}X\right)\bigsqcup_{\left(Z\boxtimes_{R}X\right)}\left(Z\boxtimes_{R}Y\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W\boxtimes_{R}Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Nor}(\zeta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta^{\prime}}

Thus in order to show that
\zeta
is a (trivial) cofibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
, i.e.
\operatorname{Nor}(\zeta)
is a (trivial) cofibration in
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
, it suffices to show that
\zeta^{\prime}
is a (trivial) cofibration in
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
.

By Theorem
3.1
,
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
is a cofibrantly generated model category with the corresponding sets

\mathcal{X}=\{\iota^{n}:S(n-1)\rightarrow D(n)\;|\;n\geq 1\}\cup\{\lambda:0\rightarrow S(0)\}

where
\iota^{n}
is the inclusion morphism for every
n\geq 1
, and

\mathcal{Y}=\{\kappa^{n}:0\rightarrow D(n)\;|\;n\geq 1\}.

In light of the proof of Corollary
2.16
and Theorem
4.15
, we see that
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
is a cofibrantly generated model category with the corresponding sets
\operatorname{DK}(\mathcal{X})
and
\operatorname{DK}(\mathcal{Y})
. In particular,
\operatorname{RLP}\left(\operatorname{DK}(\mathcal{X})\right)
is the class of trivial fibrations and
\operatorname{RLP}\left(\operatorname{DK}(\mathcal{Y})\right)
is the class of fibrations in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
. As a result, in order to show that
\eta
is a fibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
, it suffices to show that
\eta
has the right lifting property against morphisms in
\operatorname{DK}(\mathcal{Y})
, i.e. to choose
\varrho:K\rightarrow L
from
\operatorname{DK}(\mathcal{Y})
and solve the corresponding lifting problem. Similarly, to show that
\eta
is a trivial fibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
, it suffices to show that
\eta
has the right lifting property against morphisms in
\operatorname{DK}(\mathcal{X})
, i.e. to choose
\varrho:K\rightarrow L
from
\operatorname{DK}(\mathcal{X})
and solve the corresponding lifting problem.

Let
X
be a connective
R
-complex. We analyze the shuffle products
X\boxtimes_{R}D(n)
and
X\boxtimes_{R}S(n-1)
for every
n\geq 1
more closely. For any
m\geq 0
, set:

\mathcal{S}_{m}=\left\{(f,g)\in\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([m],[k]\right)\times\operatorname{Mor}_{\Delta}\left([m],[l]\right)\;|\;\begin{tabular}[]{ccc}$0\leq k,l\leq m$, $f$ and $g$ are surjective, and $\phi_{f,g}:[m]\rightarrow[k]\times[l]$\\
given by $\phi_{f,g}(i)=\left(f(i),g(i)\right)$ for every $i\in[m]$ is injective\end{tabular}\right\}

Let
n\geq 1
and
m\geq 0
. If
l\neq n,n-1
, then
D(n)_{l}=0
. Moreover,
D(n)_{n}=D(n)_{n-1}=R
. Let
\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}
be the subset of
\mathcal{S}_{m}
in which we assume
l=n
or
n-1
. Then we have the following natural isomorphisms:

\left(X\boxtimes_{R}D(n)\right)_{m}=\textstyle\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}}\left(X_{k}\otimes_{R}D(n)_{l}\right)\cong\textstyle\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\left(X_{k}\otimes_{R}R\right)\cong\textstyle\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}X_{k}

If
l\neq n-1
, then
S(n-1)_{l}=0
. Moreover,
S(n-1)_{n-1}=R
. Let
\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}
be the subset of
\mathcal{S}_{m}
in which we assume
l=n-1
. Then we have the following natural isomorphisms:

\left(X\boxtimes_{R}S(n)\right)_{m}=\textstyle\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}}\left(X_{k}\otimes_{R}S(n)_{l}\right)\cong\textstyle\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}}\left(X_{k}\otimes_{R}R\right)\cong\textstyle\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}}X_{k}

It is clear that
\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}
. Accordingly, we let
\nu_{m}:\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}
be the inclusion map.

Now suppose that
R^{(\iota)}:R^{(U)}\rightarrow R^{(V)}
is a cofibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
, i.e.
\mu:Z\rightarrow W
is a cofibration in
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
. We will show that
\eta
is a fibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
. To this end, let
n\geq 1
, and let
\varrho:K\rightarrow L
be the morphism
\operatorname{DK}(\kappa^{n}):\operatorname{DK}(0)\rightarrow\operatorname{DK}\left(D(n)\right)
. As we observed before, it suffices to show that

\zeta^{\prime}:\left(W\boxtimes_{R}0\right)\textstyle\bigsqcup_{\left(Z\boxtimes_{R}0\right)}\left(Z\boxtimes_{R}D(n)\right)\rightarrow W\boxtimes_{R}D(n)

is a trivial cofibration. We have the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(W\boxtimes_{R}0\right)\bigsqcup_{\left(Z\boxtimes_{R}0\right)}\left(Z\boxtimes_{R}D(n)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W\boxtimes_{R}D(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\bigsqcup_{0}\left(Z\boxtimes_{R}D(n)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W\boxtimes_{R}D(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z\boxtimes_{R}D(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W\boxtimes_{R}D(n)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu\boxtimes_{R}D(n)}

As
\mu
is a cofibration, we have the short exact sequence
0\rightarrow Z\xrightarrow{\mu}W\rightarrow\operatorname{Coker}(\mu)\rightarrow 0
of
R
-complexes in which
\operatorname{Coker}(\mu)
is an
R
-complex of projective modules. In particular, for any
k\geq 0
, we have the short exact sequence
0\rightarrow Z_{k}\xrightarrow{\mu_{k}}W_{k}\rightarrow\operatorname{Coker}(\mu_{k})\rightarrow 0
of
R
-modules in which
\operatorname{Coker}(\mu_{k})
is projective. Now consider the following sequence:

0\rightarrow Z\boxtimes_{R}D(n)\xrightarrow{\mu\boxtimes_{R}D(n)}W\boxtimes_{R}D(n)\rightarrow\operatorname{Coker}(\mu)\boxtimes_{R}D(n)\rightarrow 0

For each
m\geq 0
, we have the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(Z\boxtimes_{R}D(n)\right)_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(W\boxtimes_{R}D(n)\right)_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\operatorname{Coker}(\mu)\boxtimes_{R}D(n)\right)_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}Z_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}W_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\operatorname{Coker}(\mu_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\mu\boxtimes_{R}D(n)\right)_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\mu_{k}}

Now the second row is exact and
\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\operatorname{Coker}(\mu_{k})
is projective, so it follows that the first row is exact and
\left(\operatorname{Coker}(\mu)\boxtimes_{R}D(n)\right)_{m}
is projective. As a result, the previous sequence is exact and
\operatorname{Coker}(\mu)\boxtimes_{R}D(n)
is an
R
-complex of projective modules. This means that
\mu\boxtimes_{R}D(n)
is a cofibration, so
\zeta^{\prime}
is a cofibration. On the other hand, we have the following commutative diagram for every
i\geq 0
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i}\left(Z\boxtimes_{R}D(n)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i}\left(W\boxtimes_{R}D(n)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i}\left(Z\otimes_{R}D(n)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i}\left(W\otimes_{R}D(n)\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i}\left(\mu\boxtimes_{R}D(n)\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i}\left(\mu\otimes_{R}D(n)\right)}

We note that
D(n)
is an exact
R
-complex whose terms and boundaries are all flat, so by the Künneth isomorphisms in
[
Ro
, Corollary 10.84]
,
Z\otimes_{R}D(n)
and
W\otimes_{R}D(n)
are exact. As a result, for any
i\geq 0
, we notice that

0=H_{i}\left(Z\otimes_{R}D(n)\right)\xrightarrow{H_{i}\left(\mu\otimes_{R}D(n)\right)}H_{i}\left(W\otimes_{R}D(n)\right)=0

is an isomorphism, so the above diagram implies that
H_{i}\left(\mu\boxtimes_{R}D(n)\right)
is an isomorphism. Thus
\mu\boxtimes_{R}D(n)
is a quasi-isomorphism, so
\zeta^{\prime}
is a quasi-isomorphism. Altogether,
\zeta^{\prime}
is a trivial cofibration.

Next suppose that either
R^{(\iota)}:R^{(U)}\rightarrow R^{(V)}
is a trivial cofibration or
\rho:M\rightarrow N
is a trivial fibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
. We will show that
\eta
is a trivial fibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
. To this end, let
n\geq 1
, and let
\varrho:K\rightarrow L
be the morphism
