\operatorname{DK}(\iota^{n}):\operatorname{DK}\left(S(n-1)\right)\rightarrow\operatorname{DK}\left(D(n)\right)
. We show that

\zeta^{\prime}:\left(W\boxtimes_{R}S(n-1)\right)\textstyle\bigsqcup_{\left(Z\boxtimes_{R}S(n-1)\right)}\left(Z\boxtimes_{R}D(n)\right)\rightarrow W\boxtimes_{R}D(n)

is a cofibration. Consider the following pushout diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z\boxtimes_{R}S(n-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z\boxtimes_{R}D(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W\boxtimes_{R}S(n-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(W\boxtimes_{R}S(n-1)\right)\bigsqcup_{\left(Z\boxtimes_{R}S(n-1)\right)}\left(Z\boxtimes_{R}D(n)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W\boxtimes_{R}D(n)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z\boxtimes_{R}\iota^{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu\boxtimes_{R}S(n-1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varpi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu\boxtimes_{R}D(n)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W\boxtimes_{R}\iota^{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta^{\prime}}

In each degree
m\geq 0
, the above diagram is naturally isomorphic to the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}}Z_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}Z_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}}W_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}}W_{k}\right)\bigsqcup_{\left(\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}}Z_{k}\right)}\left(\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}Z_{k}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}W_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\nu_{m}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}}\mu_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\mu_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\nu_{m}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{m}}

Let
m\geq 0
. By definition, we have:

\left(\textstyle\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}}W_{k}\right)\textstyle\bigsqcup_{\left(\textstyle\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}}Z_{k}\right)}\left(\textstyle\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}Z_{k}\right)=\frac{\left(\textstyle\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}}W_{k}\right)\oplus\left(\textstyle\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}Z_{k}\right)}{\left\{\left(\left(\textstyle\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}}\mu_{k}\right)(z),-\overline{\nu_{m}}(z)\right)\;|\;z\in\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}}Z_{k}\right\}}

Let
x=\left(x_{f,g}\right)_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}}\in\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}}W_{k}
and
y=\left(y_{f,g}\right)_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\in\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}Z_{k}
. For any
(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}
, define:

x_{f,g}^{\prime}:=\begin{cases}x_{f,g}&\textrm{if }(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}\\
0&\textrm{if }(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}\backslash\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}\end{cases}

Then
\overline{\nu_{m}}(x)=\left(x_{f,g}^{\prime}\right)_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}
. Thus we have:

q_{m}\left(\overline{(x,y)}\right)=\overline{\nu_{m}}(x)+\left(\textstyle\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\mu_{k}\right)(y)=\left(x_{f,g}^{\prime}+\mu_{k}\left(y_{f,g}\right)\right)_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}

Now if
q_{m}\left(\overline{(x,y)}\right)=0
, then
x_{f,g}^{\prime}=-\mu_{k}\left(y_{f,g}\right)
for every
(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}
. Therefore,
\mu_{k}\left(y_{f,g}\right)=-x_{f,g}^{\prime}=-x_{f,g}
for every
(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}
. Moreover,
\mu_{k}\left(y_{f,g}\right)=-x_{f,g}^{\prime}=0
for every
(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}\backslash\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}
. But
\mu
is a cofibration, so
\mu_{k}
is injective, implying that
y_{f,g}=0
for every
(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}\backslash\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}
. Set
z=\left(-y_{f,g}\right)_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}}\in\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}}Z_{k}
. Then
\overline{\nu_{m}}(z)=\left(-y_{f,g}^{\prime}\right)_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}=\left(-y_{f,g}\right)_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}
where
y_{f,g}^{\prime}
is defined similar to
x_{f,g}^{\prime}
. Thus we have
x=\left(x_{f,g}\right)_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}}=\left(-\mu_{k}\left(y_{f,g}\right)\right)_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}}=\left(\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}}\mu_{k}\right)(z)
and
y=\left(y_{f,g}\right)_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}=-\overline{\nu_{m}}(z)
. This shows that
\overline{(x,y)}=0
. Therefore,
q_{m}
is injective. On the other hand, from the above diagram, we have
q_{m}p_{m}=\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\mu_{k}
, so
\operatorname{Im}\left(\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\mu_{k}\right)\subseteq\operatorname{Im}(q_{m})
. Therefore, we get an
R
-epimorphism
\psi_{m}:\operatorname{Coker}\left(\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\mu_{k}\right)\rightarrow\operatorname{Coker}(q_{m})
given by
\psi_{m}\left(x+\operatorname{Im}\left(\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\mu_{k}\right)\right)=x+\operatorname{Im}(q_{m})
for every
x\in\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}W_{k}
. We next define an
R
-homomorphism
\chi_{m}:\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}W_{k}\rightarrow\operatorname{Coker}\left(\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\mu_{k}\right)
as follows. Let
w=\left(w_{f,g}\right)_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\in\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}W_{k}
. For any
(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}
, define:

w_{f,g}^{\prime\prime}:=\begin{cases}w_{f,g}&\textrm{if }(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}\backslash\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}\\
0&\textrm{if }(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}\end{cases}

Set
\chi_{m}(w):=\left(w_{f,g}^{\prime\prime}\right)_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}+\operatorname{Im}\left(\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\mu_{k}\right)
. If
w\in\operatorname{Im}(q_{m})
, then there are elements
x=\left(x_{f,g}\right)_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}}\in\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}}W_{k}
and
y=\left(y_{f,g}\right)_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\in\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}Z_{k}
such that
w=q_{m}\left(\overline{(x,y)}\right)=\left(x_{f,g}^{\prime}+\mu_{k}\left(y_{f,g}\right)\right)_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}
. We note that
x_{f,g}^{\prime}=0
for every
(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}\backslash\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}
, so by the above construction,
\left(x_{f,g}^{\prime}\right)^{\prime\prime}=0
for every
(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}
. Therefore, we get:

\begin{split}\chi_{m}(w)&=\left(\left(x_{f,g}^{\prime}\right)^{\prime\prime}+\mu_{k}\left(y_{f,g}^{\prime\prime}\right)\right)_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}+\operatorname{Im}\left(\textstyle\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\mu_{k}\right)\\
&=\left(\mu_{k}\left(y_{f,g}^{\prime\prime}\right)\right)_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}+\operatorname{Im}\left(\textstyle\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\mu_{k}\right)\\
&=\left(\textstyle\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\mu_{k}\right)\left(\left(y_{f,g}^{\prime\prime}\right)_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\right)+\operatorname{Im}\left(\textstyle\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\mu_{k}\right)\\
&=0\end{split}

As a result,
\chi_{m}\left(\operatorname{Im}(q_{m})\right)=0
, so
\chi_{m}
induces an
R
-homomorphism
\overline{\chi_{m}}:\operatorname{Coker}(q_{m})\rightarrow\operatorname{Coker}\left(\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\mu_{k}\right)
given by
\overline{\chi_{m}}\left(w+\operatorname{Im}(q_{m})\right)=\left(w_{f,g}^{\prime\prime}\right)_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}+\operatorname{Im}\left(\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\mu_{k}\right)
. Now we have for every
w=\left(w_{f,g}\right)_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\in\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}W_{k}
:

\psi_{m}\left(\overline{\chi_{m}}\left(w+\operatorname{Im}(q_{m})\right)\right)=\psi_{m}\left(\left(w_{f,g}^{\prime\prime}\right)_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}+\operatorname{Im}\left(\textstyle\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\mu_{k}\right)\right)=\left(w_{f,g}^{\prime\prime}\right)_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}+\operatorname{Im}(q_{m})

Let
\widetilde{\nu_{m}}:\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}W_{k}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}}W_{k}
be the induced
R
-homomorphism which projects a tuple onto its components from
\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}
. Setting
x=\widetilde{\nu_{m}}\left(\left(w_{f,g}-w_{f,g}^{\prime\prime}\right)_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\right)\in\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}}W_{k}
, we see that:

w-\left(w_{f,g}^{\prime\prime}\right)_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}=\left(w_{f,g}-w_{f,g}^{\prime\prime}\right)_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}=\overline{\nu_{m}}(x)=q_{m}\left(\overline{(x,0)}\right)

Therefore,
w-\left(w_{f,g}^{\prime\prime}\right)_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\in\operatorname{Im}(q_{m})
, so we get:

\psi_{m}\left(\overline{\chi_{m}}\left(w+\operatorname{Im}(q_{m})\right)\right)=\left(w_{f,g}^{\prime\prime}\right)_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}+\operatorname{Im}(q_{m})=w+\operatorname{Im}(q_{m})

This shows that
\psi_{m}\overline{\chi_{m}}=1^{\operatorname{Coker}(q_{m})}
. Thus the short exact sequence

0\rightarrow\operatorname{Ker}(\psi_{m})\rightarrow\operatorname{Coker}\left(\textstyle\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\mu_{k}\right)\xrightarrow{\psi_{m}}\operatorname{Coker}(q_{m})\rightarrow 0

splits, so in particular,
\operatorname{Coker}(q_{m})
is isomorphic to a direct summand of
\operatorname{Coker}\left(\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\mu_{k}\right)
. But
\operatorname{Coker}\left(\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\mu_{k}\right)\cong\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime}}\operatorname{Coker}(\mu_{k})
is projective as
\mu
is a cofibration. As a consequence,
\operatorname{Coker}(q_{m})
is projective. This shows that
\zeta^{\prime}
is a cofibration.

If
\rho:M\rightarrow N
is a trivial fibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
, then we are done. If
R^{(\iota)}:R^{(U)}\rightarrow R^{(V)}
is a trivial cofibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
, i.e.
\mu:Z\rightarrow W
is a trivial cofibration in
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
, then we need to further show that
\zeta^{\prime}
is a weak equivalence, i.e. a quasi-isomorphism. Since for any
n\geq 1
,
D(n)
and
S(n-1)
are connective
R
-complexes of flat modules, and
\mu
is a weak equivalence i.e. a quasi-isomorphism, Corollary
5.11
implies that
\mu\boxtimes_{R}S(n-1)
and
\mu\boxtimes_{R}D(n)
are quasi-isomorphisms. On the other hand, since
\mu
is a cofibration,
\mu_{k}:Z_{k}\rightarrow W_{k}
is injective with projective cokernel for every
k\geq 0
, so
\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}}\mu_{k}:\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}}Z_{k}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}}W_{k}
is injective with projective cokernel. It follows that
\mu\boxtimes_{R}S(n-1)
is a cofibration. Thus
\mu\boxtimes_{R}S(n-1)
is a trivial cofibration, so by
[
Hi
, Proposition 7.2.12]
, we deduce that
\varpi
is a trivial cofibration, whence
\varpi
is a quasi-isomorphism. Now
\zeta^{\prime}\varpi=\mu\boxtimes_{R}D(n)
, so we conclude that
\zeta^{\prime}
is a quasi-isomorphism.

Now let
\varrho:K\rightarrow L
be the morphism
\operatorname{DK}(\lambda):\operatorname{DK}(0)\rightarrow\operatorname{DK}\left(S(0)\right)
. We show that

\zeta^{\prime}:\left(W\boxtimes_{R}0\right)\textstyle\bigsqcup_{\left(Z\boxtimes_{R}0\right)}\left(Z\boxtimes_{R}S(0)\right)\rightarrow W\boxtimes_{R}S(0)

is a cofibration. We have the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(W\boxtimes_{R}0\right)\bigsqcup_{\left(Z\boxtimes_{R}0\right)}\left(Z\boxtimes_{R}S(0)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W\boxtimes_{R}S(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\bigsqcup_{0}\left(Z\boxtimes_{R}S(0)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W\boxtimes_{R}S(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z\boxtimes_{R}S(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W\boxtimes_{R}S(0)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu\boxtimes_{R}S(0)}

As
\mu
is a cofibration, we have the short exact sequence
0\rightarrow Z\xrightarrow{\mu}W\rightarrow\operatorname{Coker}(\mu)\rightarrow 0
of
R
-complexes in which
\operatorname{Coker}(\mu)
is an
R
-complex of projective modules. In particular, for any
k\geq 0
, we have the short exact sequence
0\rightarrow Z_{k}\xrightarrow{\mu_{k}}W_{k}\rightarrow\operatorname{Coker}(\mu_{k})\rightarrow 0
of
R
-modules in which
\operatorname{Coker}(\mu_{k})
is projective. Now consider the following sequence:

0\rightarrow Z\boxtimes_{R}S(0)\xrightarrow{\mu\boxtimes_{R}S(0)}W\boxtimes_{R}S(0)\rightarrow\operatorname{Coker}(\mu)\boxtimes_{R}S(0)\rightarrow 0

For each
m\geq 0
, we have the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(Z\boxtimes_{R}S(0)\right)_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(W\boxtimes_{R}S(0)\right)_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\operatorname{Coker}(\mu)\boxtimes_{R}S(0)\right)_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}}Z_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}}W_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}}\operatorname{Coker}(\mu_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\mu\boxtimes_{R}S(0)\right)_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}}\mu_{k}}

Now the second row is exact and
\bigoplus_{(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}_{m}^{\prime\prime}}\operatorname{Coker}(\mu_{k})
is projective, so it follows that the first row is exact and
\left(\operatorname{Coker}(\mu)\boxtimes_{R}S(0)\right)_{m}
is projective. As a result, the previous sequence is exact and
\operatorname{Coker}(\mu)\boxtimes_{R}S(0)
is an
R
-complex of projective modules. This means that
\mu\boxtimes_{R}S(0)
is a cofibration, so
\zeta^{\prime}
is a cofibration.

If
\rho:M\rightarrow N
is a trivial fibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
, then we are done. If
R^{(\iota)}:R^{(U)}\rightarrow R^{(V)}
is a trivial cofibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
, i.e.
\mu:Z\rightarrow W
is a trivial cofibration in
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
, then we need to further show that
\zeta^{\prime}
is a weak equivalence, i.e. a quasi-isomorphism. Using the fact
S(0)=\Sigma^{0}R=R
and Corollary
5.10
, we have the following commutative diagram for every
i\geq 0
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i}\left(Z\boxtimes_{R}S(0)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i}\left(W\boxtimes_{R}S(0)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i}\left(Z\otimes_{R}S(0)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i}\left(W\otimes_{R}S(0)\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i}\left(Z\otimes_{R}R\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i}\left(W\otimes_{R}R\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i}(Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i}(W)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i}\left(\mu\boxtimes_{R}S(0)\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i}\left(\mu\otimes_{R}S(0)\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i}\left(\mu\otimes_{R}R\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{i}(\mu)}

As
\mu
is a weak equivalence, i.e. a quasi-isomorphism,
H_{i}(\mu):H_{i}(Z)\rightarrow H_{i}(W)
is an isomorphism for every
i\geq 0
, so the above diagram shows that
H_{i}\left(\mu\boxtimes_{R}S(0)\right)
is an isomorphism for every
i\geq 0
, whence
\mu\boxtimes_{R}S(0)
is a quasi-isomorphism. Thus
\zeta^{\prime}
is a quasi-isomorphism.
∎

Corollary 6.2
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring,
M
a simplicial
R
-module, and
\iota:U\rightarrow V
a morphism of simplicial sets. If
R^{(\iota)}:R^{(U)}\rightarrow R^{(V)}
is a (trivial) cofibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
, then
\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(\iota,M):\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,M)\rightarrow\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,M)
is a (trivial) fibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
.

Proof.

It is clear from Theorem
4.15
that the unique morphism
f:M\rightarrow 0
is a fibration. Consider the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,0)=0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,0)=0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(\iota,M)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(\iota,0)=0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,f)}

and form the following pullback diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,M)\mathop{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\textstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}}_{0}0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(\iota,M)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

If
R^{(\iota)}:R^{(U)}\rightarrow R^{(V)}
is a (trivial) cofibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
, then Lemma
6.1
implies that
\eta
is a (trivial) fibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
, so by the above diagram, we deduce that
\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(\iota,M)
is a (trivial) fibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
.
∎

The following lemma is dual to Lemma
6.1
:

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring,
\varrho:M\rightarrow N
a cofibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
, and
\iota:U\rightarrow V
a morphism of simplicial sets. Consider the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{(V)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{(V)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho^{(U)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{(\iota)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{(\iota)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho^{(V)}}

and form the following pushout diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{(V)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{(V)}\bigsqcup_{M^{(U)}}N^{(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{(V)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho^{(U)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{(\iota)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{(\iota)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho^{(V)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}

If
R^{(\iota)}:R^{(U)}\rightarrow R^{(V)}
is a cofibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
, then
\zeta
is a cofibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
which is trivial if either
\varrho
or
R^{(\iota)}
is.

Proof.

Let
\rho:K\rightarrow L
be a morphism of simplicial
R
-modules. Consider a commutative diagram as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{(V)}\bigsqcup_{M^{(U)}}N^{(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{(V)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

Considering the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,L)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,\rho)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(\iota,K)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(\iota,L)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,\rho)}

there is a unique morphism
\eta:\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,K)\rightarrow\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,K)\textstyle\mathop{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\textstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}}_{\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,L)}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,L)
that makes the following pullback diagram commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,K)\mathop{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\textstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}}_{\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,L)}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,L)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(\iota,K)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,\rho)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(\iota,L)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,\rho)}

On the other hand, considering the natural bijections

\phi_{NVL}:\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)}\left(N,\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,L)\right)\rightarrow\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)}\left(N^{(V)},L\right)

and

\phi_{NUK}:\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)}\left(N,\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,K)\right)\rightarrow\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)}\left(N^{(U)},K\right)

from Remark
4.4
, we get the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,L)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{NVL}^{-1}(q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{NUK}^{-1}(pr)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(\iota,L)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,\rho)}

Hence there is a unique morphism
\theta:N\rightarrow\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,K)\mathop{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\textstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}}_{\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,L)}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,L)
that makes the following diagram commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,K)\mathop{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\textstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}}_{\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,L)}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,L)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{NUK}^{-1}(pr)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{NVL}^{-1}(q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(\iota,L)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,\rho)}

Then considering the natural bijection

\phi_{MVK}:\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)}\left(M,\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,K)\right)\rightarrow\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)}\left(M^{(V)},K\right)

we get the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,K)\textstyle\mathop{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\textstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}}_{\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,L)}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,L)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{MVK}^{-1}(ps)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}

If
\tau:N\rightarrow\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,K)
is a morphism of simplicial
R
-modules that makes the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,K)\textstyle\mathop{\mathchoice{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\textstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{180.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}}_{\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(U,L)}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,L)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{MVK}^{-1}(ps)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}

commutative, then considering the natural bijection

\phi_{NVK}:\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)}\left(N,\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(V,K)\right)\rightarrow\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)}\left(N^{(V)},K\right)

we see that the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{(V)}\bigsqcup_{M^{(U)}}N^{(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N^{(V)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{NVK}(\tau)}

Now if
\varrho
and
R^{(\iota)}
are cofibrations, and
\rho
is a trivial fibration, then Lemma
6.1
implies that
\eta
is a trivial fibration, so
\tau
exists, whence we conclude from the above diagram that
\zeta
is a cofibration. If
\varrho
is a trivial cofibration,
R^{(\iota)}
is a cofibration, and
\rho
is a fibration, then Lemma
6.1
implies that
\eta
is a fibration, so
\tau
exists, whence we deduce from the above diagram that
\zeta
is a trivial cofibration. If
\varrho
is a cofibration,
R^{(\iota)}
is a trivial cofibration, and
\rho
is a fibration, then Lemma
6.1
implies that
\eta
is a trivial fibration, so
\tau
exists, whence we infer from the above diagram that
\zeta
is a trivial cofibration.
∎

Corollary 6.4
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring,
M
a cofibrant simplicial
R
-module, and
\iota:U\rightarrow V
a morphism of simplicial sets. If
R^{(\iota)}:R^{(U)}\rightarrow R^{(V)}
is a (trivial) cofibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
, then
M^{(\iota)}:M^{(U)}\rightarrow M^{(V)}
is a (trivial) cofibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
.

Proof.

Since
M
is cofibrant, the unique morphism
f:0\rightarrow M
is a cofibration. Consider the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0=0^{(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0=0^{(V)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{(V)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{(U)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0=0^{(\iota)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{(\iota)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{(V)}}

and form the following pushout diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\bigsqcup_{0}M^{(U)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{(V)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{(\iota)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{(V)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta}

If
R^{(\iota)}:R^{(U)}\rightarrow R^{(V)}
is a (trivial) cofibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
, then Lemma
6.3
implies that
\zeta
is a (trivial) cofibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
, so by the above diagram, we deduce that
M^{(\iota)}
is a (trivial) cofibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
.
∎

Now we can construct specific cylinder and path objects in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
. These objects are introduced in
[
GS
, Corollary 4.14]
but the proof outlined there relies heavily on the model structure of simplicial sets. Here we provide a proof based on Lemmas
6.1
and
6.3
whose proofs are independent of the model structure of simplicial sets.

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring, and
M
a simplicial
R
-module. Then the following assertions hold:

(i)

If
M
is cofibrant in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
, then
M^{(\Delta^{1})}
is a cylinder object for
M
.

(ii)

\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}\left(\Delta^{1},M\right)
is a path object for
M
.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon:\Delta^{1}\rightarrow\Delta^{0}
and
\delta:\partial\Delta^{1}\rightarrow\Delta^{0}
be the unique morphisms, and
\iota:\partial\Delta^{1}\rightarrow\Delta^{1}
the inclusion morphism. Then we have the following commutative diagram of simplicial sets:

(6.5.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial\Delta^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}

We note that
\Delta_{n}^{0}=\{(\underbrace{0,...,0}_{n+1\textrm{ times}})\}
and
\Delta_{n}^{1}=\{(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})\;|\;0\leq\alpha_{0}\leq\alpha_{1}\leq\ldots\leq\alpha_{n}\leq 1\}
for every
n\geq 0
. Define a morphism
\zeta=:\Delta^{0}\rightarrow\Delta^{1}
of simplicial sets by setting
\zeta_{n}(0,...,0)=(0,...,0)
for every
n\geq 0
. Then it is clear that
\varepsilon\zeta=1^{\Delta^{0}}
. We show that the induced morphism
R^{(\zeta)}:R^{(\Delta^{0})}\rightarrow R^{(\Delta^{1})}
is a trivial cofibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
, i.e.
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\zeta)}\right):\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{0})}\right)\rightarrow\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{1})}\right)
is a trivial cofibration in
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
. To this end, we analyze this morphism more closely. We have observed in the proof of Lemma
4.8
that
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{0})}\right)
is the
R
-complex

0\rightarrow R\rightarrow 0

with
R
in degree
0
. On the other hand, we have
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{1})}\right)_{0}=R^{(\Delta_{0}^{1})}=R^{(\{0,1\})}=R^{2}
with a basis
\{e_{1},e_{2}\}
corresponding to
\Delta_{0}^{1}=\{0,1\}
, and
R^{(\Delta_{1}^{1})}=R^{(\{(0,0),(0,1),(1,1)\})}=R^{3}
with a basis
\{e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime},e_{3}^{\prime}\}
corresponding to
\Delta_{1}^{1}=\left\{(0,0),(0,1),(1,1)\right\}
. We describe the morphisms

R^{3}=R^{(\Delta_{1}^{1})}\xrightarrow{d_{1,0}^{R^{(\Delta^{1})}}=R^{\left(d_{1,0}^{\Delta^{1}}\right)}}R^{(\Delta_{0}^{1})}=R^{2}

and

R^{3}=R^{(\Delta_{1}^{1})}\xrightarrow{d_{1,1}^{R^{(\Delta^{1})}}=R^{\left(d_{1,1}^{\Delta^{1}}\right)}}R^{(\Delta_{0}^{1})}=R^{2}

as follows. We note that
e_{1}^{\prime}
corresponds to
(0,0)
, so since
d_{1,0}^{\Delta^{1}}(0,0)=0
and
0
corresponds to
e_{1}
, we get
d_{1,0}^{R^{(\Delta^{1})}}(e_{1}^{\prime})=e_{1}
. Similarly, we have:

d_{1,0}^{\Delta^{1}}:\begin{cases}(0,0)\mapsto 0\\
(0,1)\mapsto 1\\
(1,1)\mapsto 1\end{cases}\quad\text{and}\quad d_{1,1}^{\Delta^{1}}:\begin{cases}(0,0)\mapsto 0\\
(0,1)\mapsto 0\\
(1,1)\mapsto 1\end{cases}

Hence we get:

d_{1,0}^{R^{(\Delta^{1})}}:\begin{cases}e_{1}^{\prime}\mapsto e_{1}\\
e_{2}^{\prime}\mapsto e_{2}\\
e_{3}^{\prime}\mapsto e_{2}\end{cases}\quad\text{and}\quad d_{1,1}^{R^{(\Delta^{1})}}:\begin{cases}e_{1}^{\prime}\mapsto e_{1}\\
e_{2}^{\prime}\mapsto e_{1}\\
e_{3}^{\prime}\mapsto e_{2}\end{cases}

It follows that
d_{1,0}^{R^{(\Delta^{1})}}(a,b,c)=(a,b+c)
and
d_{1,1}^{R^{(\Delta^{1})}}(a,b,c)=(a+b,c)
for every
(a,b,c)\in R^{3}
. Thus we have:

\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{1})}\right)_{1}=\operatorname{Ker}\left(d_{1,0}^{R^{(\Delta^{1})}}\right)=\left\{(a,b,c)\in R^{3}\;|\;a=0\textrm{ and }b=-c\right\}=\left\{(0,-a,a)\;|\;a\in R\right\}\cong R

Moreover, in light of the above isomorphism,
\partial_{1}=\partial_{1}^{\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{1})}\right)}:R\rightarrow R^{2}
is given by
\partial_{1}(a)=-d_{1,1}^{R^{(\Delta^{1})}}(0,-a,a)=(a,-a)
for every
a\in R
. As we saw in the proof of Lemma
4.8
, if
n\geq 2
, then
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{1})}\right)_{n}=0
. As a consequence,
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{1})}\right)
is the
R
-complex

0\rightarrow R\xrightarrow{\partial_{1}}R^{2}\rightarrow 0

with
R
in degree
1
and
R^{2}
in degree
0
. Now consider
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\zeta)}\right):\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{0})}\right)\rightarrow\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{1})}\right)
. We note that
\zeta_{0}:\Delta_{0}^{0}\rightarrow\Delta_{0}^{1}
is given by
\zeta_{0}(0)=(0)
, so
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\zeta)}\right)_{0}=R^{(\zeta_{0})}:R\rightarrow R^{2}
is given by
R^{(\zeta_{0})}(a)=(a,0)
for every
a\in R
. Indeed,
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\zeta)}\right)
is as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{(\zeta_{0})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}}

It is now clear that
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\zeta)}\right)
is injective whose cokernel is an
R
-complex of projective modules, so
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\zeta)}\right)
is a cofibration. Furthermore, by Lemma
4.8
,
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\zeta)}\right)
is a homotopy equivalence, hence a quasi-isomorphism, i.e. a weak equivalence. Therefore,
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\zeta)}\right)
is a trivial cofibration in
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
.

We next show that
R^{(\iota)}:R^{(\partial\Delta^{1})}\rightarrow R^{(\Delta^{1})}
is a cofibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
, i.e.
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\iota)}\right):\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\partial\Delta^{1})}\right)\rightarrow\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{1})}\right)
is a cofibration in
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
. To this end, we analyze this morphism more closely. We have
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\partial\Delta^{1})}\right)_{0}=R^{(\partial\Delta_{0}^{1})}=R^{(\{0,1\})}=R^{2}
. If
n\geq 1
, then every element of
\partial\Delta_{n}^{1}
is degenerate, so
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\partial\Delta^{1})}\right)_{n}=0
. As a consequence,
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\partial\Delta^{1})}\right)
is the
R
-complex

0\rightarrow R^{2}\rightarrow 0

with
R^{2}
in degree
0
. Now consider
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\iota)}\right):\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\partial\Delta^{1})}\right)\rightarrow\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\Delta^{1})}\right)
. We note that
\partial\Delta_{0}^{1}=\Delta_{0}^{1}=\{0,1\}
, so
\iota_{0}:\partial\Delta_{0}^{1}\rightarrow\Delta_{0}^{1}
is the identity map, thereby
R^{(\iota_{0})}=1^{R^{2}}:R^{2}\rightarrow R^{2}
. Indeed,
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\iota)}\right)
is as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{(\iota_{0})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{1}}

It is now clear that
\operatorname{Nor}\left(R^{(\iota)}\right)
is injective whose cokernel is an
R
-complex of projective modules, so it is a cofibration. Now we have:

(i): The diagram (
6.5.1
) yields the following commutative diagram of simplicial
R
-modules:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{(\partial\Delta^{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{(\Delta^{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{(\Delta^{1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{(\delta)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{(\iota)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{(\varepsilon)}}

From the relation
\varepsilon\zeta=1^{\Delta^{0}}
, we get
M^{(\varepsilon)}M^{(\zeta)}=1^{M^{(\Delta^{0})}}
. Since
R^{(\zeta)}:R^{(\Delta^{0})}\rightarrow R^{(\Delta^{1})}
is a trivial cofibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
, Corollary
6.4
shows that
M^{(\zeta)}:M^{(\Delta^{0})}\rightarrow M^{(\Delta^{1})}
is a trivial cofibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
, hence in particular a weak equivalence. Thus
M^{(\varepsilon)}
is a weak equivalence in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
. On the other hand,
R^{(\iota)}:R^{(\partial\Delta^{1})}\rightarrow R^{(\Delta^{1})}
is a cofibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
, so Corollary
6.4
implies that
M^{(\iota)}:M^{(\partial\Delta^{1})}\rightarrow M^{(\Delta^{1})}
is a cofibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
. We finally note that
M^{(\Delta^{0})}\cong M
, and also we have
M^{(\partial\Delta^{1})}\cong M^{(\Delta^{0}\sqcup\Delta^{0})}\cong M^{(\Delta^{0})}\oplus M^{(\Delta^{0})}\cong M\oplus M
by Example
4.3
. Taking these isomorphisms into account, the above diagram yields a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\oplus M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\left(\Delta^{1}\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla^{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa^{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{M}}

in which
\kappa^{M}
is a cofibration and
\xi^{M}
is a weak equivalence. This means that
M^{(\Delta^{1})}
is a cylinder object for
M
.

(ii): The diagram (
6.5.1
) yields the following commutative diagram of simplicial
R
-modules:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}\left(\Delta^{0},M\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}\left(\partial\Delta^{1},M\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}\left(\Delta^{1},M\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(\delta,M)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(\varepsilon,M)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(\iota,M)}

From the relation
\varepsilon\zeta=1^{\Delta^{0}}
, we get
\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(\zeta,M)\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(\varepsilon,M)=1^{\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}\left(\Delta^{0},M\right)}
. Since
R^{(\zeta)}:R^{(\Delta^{0})}\rightarrow R^{(\Delta^{1})}
is a trivial cofibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
, the Corollary
6.2
shows that
\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(\zeta,M):\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}\left(\Delta^{1},M\right)\rightarrow\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}\left(\Delta^{0},M\right)
is a trivial fibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
, hence in particular a weak equivalence. Thus
\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(\varepsilon,M)
is a weak equivalence in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
. On the other hand,
R^{(\iota)}:R^{(\partial\Delta^{1})}\rightarrow R^{(\Delta^{1})}
is a cofibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
, so Corollary
6.2
implies that
\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}(\iota,M):\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}\left(\Delta^{1},M\right)\rightarrow\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}\left(\partial\Delta^{1},M\right)
is a fibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
. We finally note that
\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}\left(\Delta^{0},M\right)\cong M
, and also we have
\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}\left(\partial\Delta^{1},M\right)\cong\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}\left(\Delta^{0}\sqcup\Delta^{0},M\right)\cong\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}\left(\Delta^{0},M\right)\times\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}\left(\Delta^{0},M\right)\cong M\times M
. Taking these isomorphisms into account, the above diagram yields a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\times M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}\left(\Delta^{1},M\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta^{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu^{M}}

in which
\nu^{M}
is a fibration and
\zeta^{M}
is a weak equivalence. This means that
\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}\left(\Delta^{1},M\right)
is a path object for
M
.
∎

We are now ready to prove the main theorem.

Theorem 6.6
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring, and
\sharp:\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{C}\mathpzc{om}\mathcal{A}\mathpzc{lg}(R)\rightarrow\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
the forgetful functor. Then
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{C}\mathpzc{om}\mathcal{A}\mathpzc{lg}(R)
is a cofibrantly generated model category with the model structure given as follows:

(i)

Fibrations are morphisms
f:A\rightarrow B
in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{C}\mathpzc{om}\mathcal{A}\mathpzc{lg}(R)
such that
\operatorname{Nor}\left(f^{\sharp}\right)_{i}:\operatorname{Nor}\left(A^{\sharp}\right)_{i}\rightarrow\operatorname{Nor}\left(B^{\sharp}\right)_{i}
is surjective for every
i\geq 1
.

(ii)

Weak equivalences are morphisms
f:A\rightarrow B
in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{C}\mathpzc{om}\mathcal{A}\mathpzc{lg}(R)
such that
\operatorname{Nor}\left(f^{\sharp}\right):\operatorname{Nor}\left(A^{\sharp}\right)\rightarrow\operatorname{Nor}\left(B^{\sharp}\right)
is a quasi-isomorphism in
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
.

(iii)

Cofibration are morphisms in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{C}\mathpzc{om}\mathcal{A}\mathpzc{lg}(R)
that have the left lifting property against trivial fibrations defined in (i) and (ii).

Proof.

By Theorems
4.14
and
4.15
,
(\operatorname{DK},\operatorname{Nor}):\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)\leftrightarrows\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
is an adjoint equivalence of categories through which
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
becomes a cofibrantly generated model category. If
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
are the sets of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
corresponding to its cofibrant generation, then we have observed that the domains of morphisms in
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
are small with respect to
\operatorname{Mor}\left(\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)\right)
. It follows that the domains of morphisms in
\operatorname{DK}(\mathcal{X})
and
\operatorname{DK}(\mathcal{Y})
are small with respect to
\operatorname{Mor}\left(\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)\right)
. On the other hand, we have the adjoint pair of functors
\left(\operatorname{Sym}_{R}(-),\sharp\right):\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)\leftrightarrows\mathcal{C}\mathpzc{om}\mathcal{A}\mathpzc{lg}(R)
that extends degreewise to an adjoint pair of functors
\left(\operatorname{Sym}_{R}(-),\sharp\right):\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)\leftrightarrows\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{C}\mathpzc{om}\mathcal{A}\mathpzc{lg}(R)
. Also,
\mathcal{C}\mathpzc{om}\mathcal{A}\mathpzc{lg}(R)
is locally small and bicomplete, so
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{C}\mathpzc{om}\mathcal{A}\mathpzc{lg}(R)
is locally small and bicomplete. By the construction of filtered direct limits, we notice that the forgetful functor
\sharp:\mathcal{C}\mathpzc{om}\mathcal{A}\mathpzc{lg}(R)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
preserves filtered direct limits, so its extension
\sharp:\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{C}\mathpzc{om}\mathcal{A}\mathpzc{lg}(R)\rightarrow\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
also preserves filtered direct limits, hence sequential direct limits in particular. Therefore, Corollary
2.11
implies that the domains of morphisms in
\operatorname{Sym}_{R}\left(\operatorname{DK}(\mathcal{X})\right)
and
\operatorname{Sym}_{R}\left(\operatorname{DK}(\mathcal{Y})\right)
are small with respect to
\operatorname{Mor}\left(\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{C}\mathpzc{om}\mathcal{A}\mathpzc{lg}(R)\right)
. In particular,
\operatorname{Sym}_{R}\left(\operatorname{DK}(\mathcal{X})\right)
and
\operatorname{Sym}_{R}\left(\operatorname{DK}(\mathcal{Y})\right)
permit the small object argument. Now we let a morphism
f:A\rightarrow B
in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{C}\mathpzc{om}\mathcal{A}\mathpzc{lg}(R)
be a fibration or a weak equivalence if
f^{\sharp}:A^{\sharp}\rightarrow B^{\sharp}
is a fibration or a weak equivalence in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
, respectively. As a consequence,
f:A\rightarrow B
is a fibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{C}\mathpzc{om}\mathcal{A}\mathpzc{lg}(R)
if and only if
\operatorname{Nor}\left(f^{\sharp}\right)_{i}:\operatorname{Nor}\left(A^{\sharp}\right)_{i}\rightarrow\operatorname{Nor}\left(B^{\sharp}\right)_{i}
is surjective for every
i\geq 1
. Also,
f:A\rightarrow B
is a weak equivalence in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{C}\mathpzc{om}\mathcal{A}\mathpzc{lg}(R)
if and only if
\operatorname{Nor}\left(f^{\sharp}\right):\operatorname{Nor}\left(A^{\sharp}\right)\rightarrow\operatorname{Nor}\left(B^{\sharp}\right)
is a quasi-isomorphism. Finally, we let a morphism
f:A\rightarrow B
in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{C}\mathpzc{om}\mathcal{A}\mathpzc{lg}(R)
be a cofibration if it has the left lifting property against trivial fibrations.

In order to apply Theorem
2.9
, we need to establish the acyclicity condition, i.e. we need to show that any cofibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{C}\mathpzc{om}\mathcal{A}\mathpzc{lg}(R)
that has the left lifting property against fibrations is also a weak equivalence. Let
g:A\rightarrow B
be a cofibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{C}\mathpzc{om}\mathcal{A}\mathpzc{lg}(R)
that has the left lifting property against fibrations. If
f:A\rightarrow 0
is the unique morphism in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{C}\mathpzc{om}\mathcal{A}\mathpzc{lg}(R)
, then
f^{\sharp}:A^{\sharp}\rightarrow 0^{\sharp}
is a fibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
, so
f
is a fibration in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{C}\mathpzc{om}\mathcal{A}\mathpzc{lg}(R)
. Therefore, the commutative diagram on the left can be completed to the commutative diagram on the right:

In particular,
hg=1^{A}
. On the other hand, by the universal property of product, there is a unique morphism
l:B\rightarrow B\sqcap B
that makes the following diagrams commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\sqcap B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1^{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces gh}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}^{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{B}}

Consider the path object
\operatorname{Path}\left(B^{\sharp}\right)=\operatorname{Map}_{\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{S}\mathpzc{et}}\left(\Delta^{1},B^{\sharp}\right)
in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
. Then there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\sharp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\sharp}\sqcap B^{\sharp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Path}\left(B^{\sharp}\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{B^{\sharp}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{B^{\sharp}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{B^{\sharp}}}

in which
\nu_{B^{\sharp}}
is a fibration and
\zeta_{B^{\sharp}}
is a weak equivalence in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{M}\mathpzc{od}(R)
. But we observed in the proof of Proposition
6.5
that this diagram is isomorphic to a diagram in which morphisms could be either morphisms of simplicial modules or simplicial commutative algebras depending on what structure we consider; See also Remark
4.4
. Therefore, we can also have the commutative diagram

(6.6.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\sqcap B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Path}(B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{B}}

in which
\nu_{B}
is a fibration and
\zeta_{B}
is a weak equivalence in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{C}\mathpzc{om}\mathcal{A}\mathpzc{lg}(R)
. Then the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Path}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\sqcap B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{B}g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}

Indeed, we have

\pi_{1}^{B}lg=1^{B}g=\pi_{1}^{B}\Delta_{B}g=\pi_{1}^{B}\nu_{B}\zeta_{B}g

and

\pi_{2}^{B}lg=ghg=g1^{A}=g=1^{B}g=\pi_{2}^{B}\Delta_{B}g=\pi_{2}^{B}\nu_{B}\zeta_{B}g,

so it follows that
lg=\nu_{B}\zeta_{B}g
. As
\nu_{B}
is a fibration, the above diagram can be completed to the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Path}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\sqcap B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{B}g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

Now the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Path}(B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1^{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces gh}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}^{B}\nu_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{B}\nu_{B}}

Indeed, we have
\pi_{1}^{B}\nu_{B}\psi=\pi_{1}^{B}l=1^{B}
and
\pi_{2}^{B}\nu_{B}\psi=\pi_{2}^{B}l=gh
. Let
\mathcal{G}=\operatorname{Nor}\left(-^{\sharp}\right):\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{C}\mathpzc{om}\mathcal{A}\mathpzc{lg}(R)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
. Applying the functor
\mathcal{G}
to the diagram (
6.6.1
) yields the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(B)\sqcap\mathcal{G}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}\left(\operatorname{Path}(B)\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta\mathcal{G}(\Delta_{B})=\Delta_{\mathcal{G}(B)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(\zeta_{B})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta\mathcal{G}(\nu_{B})}

in
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
in which
\eta:\mathcal{G}(B\sqcap B)\rightarrow\mathcal{G}(B)\sqcap\mathcal{G}(B)
is the natural isomorphism. Since
\nu_{B}
is a fibration and
\zeta_{B}
is a weak equivalence in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{C}\mathpzc{om}\mathcal{A}\mathpzc{lg}(R)
, we see by definition that
\eta\mathcal{G}(\Delta_{B})
is a fibration and
\mathcal{G}(\zeta_{B})
is a weak equivalence in
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
. This means that
\mathcal{G}\left(\operatorname{Path}(B)\right)
is a path object for
\mathcal{G}(B)
. Moreover, the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}\left(\operatorname{Path}(B)\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1^{\mathcal{G}(B)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(g)\mathcal{G}(h)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(\psi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}^{\mathcal{G}(B)}\eta\mathcal{G}(\nu_{B})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}^{\mathcal{G}(B)}\eta\mathcal{G}(\nu_{B})}

Since
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
is a model category by Theorem
3.1
, the above diagram shows that
\mathcal{G}(g)\mathcal{G}(h)\sim_{r}1^{\mathcal{G}(B)}
. Applying the localization functor
\mathcal{L}_{R}:=\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)}:\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)\rightarrow\operatorname{Ho}\left(\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)\right)
, noting that
\mathcal{L}_{R}
maps weak equivalences to isomorphisms, and invoking
[
Hi
, Lemma 8.3.4]
, we get
\mathcal{L}_{R}\left(\mathcal{G}(g)\right)\mathcal{L}_{R}\left(\mathcal{G}(h)\right)=1^{\mathcal{G}(B)}
. On the other hand, from the relation
hg=1^{A}
, we get
\mathcal{L}_{R}\left(\mathcal{G}(h)\right)\mathcal{L}_{R}\left(\mathcal{G}(g)\right)=1^{\mathcal{G}(A)}
. Therefore,
\mathcal{L}_{R}\left(\mathcal{G}(g)\right)
is an isomorphism, so by Proposition
2.7
,
\mathcal{G}(g)
is a weak equivalence in
\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0}(R)
, so by definition,
g
is a weak equivalence in
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{C}\mathpzc{om}\mathcal{A}\mathpzc{lg}(R)
.

It now follows from Theorem
2.9
that
\mathpzc{s}\mathcal{C}\mathpzc{om}\mathcal{A}\mathpzc{lg}(R)
is a cofibrantly generated model category.
∎
